**Summary.** The paper presents a method utilizing stroboscopic Rydberg dressing on neutral atom arrays to generate highly squeezed, non-classical motional states. By controlling the collective motion of the atoms, researchers can drastically reduce interatomic distance fluctuations that typically degrade quantum gate fidelity. This technique offers a powerful resource for enhancing precision measurements and improving quantum computation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical blueprint for using engineered quantum correlations (squeezing) in trapped atoms to overcome fundamental limitations imposed by environmental coupling or geometric uncertainties in quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to engineer many-body motional squeezing of interatomic distances to reduce quantum gate infidelities in neutral atom arrays.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that stroboscopic Rydberg dressing can generate highly squeezed, non-classical collective motional states characterized by Wigner negativity. This technique reduces distance uncertainty significantly below the vacuum level.

**Method.** They derive the time evolution operator for the system over multiple dressing cycles and use it to analytically calculate the variances and covariances of interatomic distances using bosonic mode decomposition.

**Model / system.** The platform is an array of neutral atoms (e.g., Rb) trapped in optical tweezers, interacting via a Rydberg blockade potential ($V(r) \propto 1/r^6$). The dynamics are controlled by applying periodic stroboscopic laser pulses.

**Key observables.** Positional covariance matrix $C_{jk}$, variance of interatomic distances $	ext{Var}(\delta_{j, j+1})$, and the Wigner function negativity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $x_0/a_0$ (initial displacement relative to lattice spacing), the dressing phase $\omega_T$, and the anharmonicity parameter $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis heavily relies on the fast-pulse approximation ($\Omega \gg V(r)$) and, in some derivations, assumes Gaussian probability distributions for calculating variances.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the reduction of interatomic distance uncertainty across multiple dressing cycles and show Wigner functions confirming non-Gaussianity when anharmonicities are present.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the need to control distance fluctuations, deriving the time evolution operator via stroboscopic dressing, decomposing this operator into independent normal modes, and finally calculating the resulting motional observables like variances and covariances.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Realizing conditional quantum operations, e.g., quantum gates, for quantum computing and simulation requires controlled interactions between particles. Often, these interactions depend on the interparticle distance, and accordingly, an uncertainty of the relative particle position may translate into gate infidelities. We consider here a quantum computing platform based on an array of neutral atoms and present a method that allows to reduce the uncertainty of all interatomic distances. Our approach exploits the coupling between atomic motion and stroboscopically excited atomic Rydberg states. It allows to collectively squeeze the modes corresponding to interatomic displacements, thereby reducing distance fluctuations down to a fraction of the motional vacuum state. Furthermore, the method permits the creation of non-classical states with substantial Wigner negativity. These correlated states may allow reducing motional decoherence, increasing gate fidelity, and potentially yield a resource for quantum-enhanced metrology.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates that confining a superconducting film within an optical cavity enhances its superconducting response in underdoped cuprates. By measuring the change in superfluid weight via THz spectroscopy, the authors confirm that cavity confinement increases the effective phase stiffness of the superconductor. This work showcases cavity engineering as a powerful tool for controlling emergent quantum functionalities in correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges condensed matter physics (high-Tc superconductors, phase transitions) with quantum optics/cavity QED principles. The concept of using engineered electromagnetic environments to tune fundamental material parameters like phase stiffness is highly relevant for AMO physicists working on open quantum systems and cavity coupling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether engineering the electromagnetic environment using a resonant cavity can enhance superconducting coherence, particularly by stabilizing phase fluctuations in underdoped cuprates.

**Main result.** Experiments show that placing an underdoped YBCO film inside a terahertz cavity enhances the measured superfluid weight and causes an upward shift in the superconducting onset temperature. Theory confirms this enhancement is due to increased effective phase stiffness ($J$).

**Method.** The research combines THz time-domain spectroscopy measurements with theoretical modeling based on the XY model for phase-fluctuating superconductors.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an underdoped YBa2Cu3O7-delta thin film placed inside a tunable Fabry-Pérot terahertz cavity. The theory models superconductivity using the XY Hamiltonian, coupling superconducting phase dynamics to the quantized electromagnetic field of the cavity.

**Key observables.** Superfluid weight ($ar{\sigma}_2$), superconducting onset temperature ($T_{onset}$), and the renormalized phase stiffness ($J$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cavity length ($L$), temperature ($T$), and the ratio of cavity confinement to intrinsic material parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system can be described by a phase-only action after integrating out EM fields, and that measurements avoid spurious shifts caused by local heating or misalignment.

**Figures summary.** Figures show temperature dependence of conductivity ($ar{\sigma}_2$) confirming enhancement inside the cavity; theoretical plots illustrate how cavity size ($L$) modifies the plasmon dispersion relation and increases the phase stiffness $J$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the problem (phase fluctuations limit cuprate superconductivity) to presenting THz measurements showing cavity enhancement, followed by detailed theoretical derivations using action functionals and Renormalization Group analysis to quantify the resulting increase in phase stiffness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconductors carry electrical current without resistance when paired electrons condense into a coherent macroscopic quantum state. In underdoped cuprates, evidence suggests that pairing-related correlations and superconducting fluctuations can survive above the temperature at which global coherence is lost, pointing to phase fluctuations as a key limitation on superconductivity in this regime. Motivated by recent demonstrations of cavity-modified collective states in quantum materials, we investigate whether superconducting coherence can be stabilized by engineering the electromagnetic environment of the superconductor. We study an underdoped YBa$_2$Cu$_3$O$_{7-δ}$ thin film in a tunable terahertz cavity formed with a semi-transparent gold mirror. From temperature-dependent terahertz transmission measurements, we find that the cavity enhances the superconducting response below the critical temperature, with an increase of the inferred superfluid weight. The effect becomes more pronounced at smaller cavity lengths and is accompanied by an upward shift of the superconducting onset temperature. Calculations based on a cavity-coupled model for phase-fluctuating superconductors capture these trends and support an interpretation in terms of cavity-enhanced phase stiffness. These results showcase the potential of cavity engineering for designing emergent functionalities in correlated systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This review and study investigates how turbulent Bose gases relax far from equilibrium by analyzing universal scaling laws. The authors combine theory (NTFPs, WKE) with experiments on BECs to show that the decay proceeds through distinct direct and inverse cascade stages. This confirms turbulence's role in establishing universal transport dynamics in quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete realization of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics in an ultracold atomic setting, linking fundamental concepts like wave turbulence and universality classes to measurable physical observables in quantum gases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is understanding the relaxation dynamics of isolated quantum systems, particularly how far-from-equilibrium evolution in turbulent Bose gases can be described by universal scaling laws.

**Main result.** The system exhibits self-similar evolution characterized by Nonthermal Fixed Points (NTFPs), demonstrating distinct direct and inverse cascade stages governed by universal power-law exponents ($\alpha$ and $eta$).

**Method.** The study combines theoretical analysis using weak kinetic equations, renormalization group concepts, and differential equation approaches with experimental measurements of momentum distributions via Time-of-Flight imaging.

**Model / system.** The system under investigation is a Bose gas (e.g., $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ BEC) driven into a turbulent, far-from-equilibrium state using controlled excitation potentials in an ultracold atomic trap.

**Key observables.** Momentum distribution $n(k, t)$, average particle number ($N$), and mean kinetic energy ($\langle k^2 angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Universal scaling exponents ($\alpha$ and $eta$), momentum scales (UV/IR limits), and time evolution relative to characteristic times ($t/t_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The emergence of universal dynamical scaling despite different microscopic interactions, and the applicability of simplified differential equations in specific momentum regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the collapse of momentum distributions into single universal functions after rescaling for both direct (depletion) and inverse (repopulation) cascades. One figure shows the time evolution of low-momentum states ($n(k 	o 0, t)$) validating scaling laws.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework using weak kinetic equations and NTFP concepts to detailing experimental measurements that confirm self-similar scaling. It then presents alternative analytical methods (differential equations) to extract universal exponents ($\alpha$ and $eta$) from limited data regions, confirming consistency across different analysis techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the relaxation dynamics of isolated quantum systems remains a central challenge in nonequilibrium physics. In this short review, we discuss recent experimental and theoretical advances in universal dynamics of turbulent Bose gases, with particular emphasis on nonthermal fixed points and wave turbulence in our experimental system at the São Carlos Institute of Physics of the University of São Paulo. We highlight the emergence of self-similar scaling behavior and its connection to particle and energy transport across momentum scales. In addition, we discuss an approach in which a differential equation has a universal scaling solution, providing a practical framework for extracting universal exponents from limited regions of the momentum distribution. Furthermore, we recap experimental observations of direct and inverse cascades in a three-dimensional trapped Bose--Einstein condensate, revealing distinct relaxation stages governed by universal scaling laws. These results demonstrate that turbulence plays a key role in far-from-equilibrium dynamics, offering a unified perspective on transport processes and thermalization in quantum many-body systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a bosonic active Ising model to study emergent collective behaviors like flocking and aster formation in non-equilibrium settings. The key finding is that bosonic quantum statistics significantly enhance the stability of these ordered phases compared to classical models. Using mean-field theory, the authors map out phase diagrams showing how this enhancement competes with suppression from external magnetic fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical framework for understanding non-equilibrium collective dynamics in quantum many-body systems. The explicit demonstration of 'bosonic enhancement' offers insights into how quantum statistics can stabilize complex ordered phases beyond classical mean-field predictions, which is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how bosonic quantum statistics enhance collective behaviors like flocking and aster formation in an active Ising model, contrasting these findings with classical models and other quantum generalizations.

**Main result.** Bosonic indistinguishability markedly enhances the stability of both flocking and aster phases compared to classical predictions, allowing these ordered states to persist at higher temperatures. Furthermore, the analysis maps out how external transverse magnetic fields suppress these collective phenomena.

**Method.** The authors employ a mean-field approximation on the Lindblad master equation for ideal bosons, deriving closed kinetic equations for single-particle density matrices and mean occupations.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional quantum lattice variant of the Active Ising Model (AIM) based on ideal bosons with spin degrees of freedom ($\uparrow$ and $\downarrow$). The dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation incorporating coherent, spin-flip, and biased hopping processes.

**Key observables.** Mean magnetization ($\langle m angle$), local magnetizations ($ho_l, ho_{l+1}$), mean occupations ($\langle n_{ls} angle$), and the critical temperature for phase transitions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Activity parameter ($\epsilon$), inverse temperature ($eta=1/T$), external transverse magnetic field strength ($\omega$), and system density ($ho_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on mean-field approximations and the use of Wick's theorem to truncate the BBGKY hierarchy, neglecting higher-order correlations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams comparing classical vs. quantum predictions (showing enhanced stability in the quantum case), and dynamic simulations showing the formation and evolution of large moving flocks or stationary asters under different parameter regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the full quantum master equation to deriving mean-field kinetic equations for occupations, analyzing the classical limit recovery, quantifying bosonic enhancement effects, and finally mapping out phase diagrams dependent on temperature, density, and external fields.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Collective behaviour such as flocking (the collective motion of a spontaneously formed group along a common direction) or aster formation (the binding of opposing flocks, inhibiting each others motion) are intriguing emergent phenomena in active systems with local alignment rules. Until recently, their occurrence was mainly studied for classical systems, a prime example being the active Ising model (AIM), which translates the main ingredients of flocking and aster formation (i.e., alignment and self-propulsion) to a lattice framework. Here we introduce and study a one-dimensional (1D) quantum lattice variant of the AIM, based on ideal bosons with a spin degree of freedom. We find that both the collective behaviours of the 1D classical model, flocking and aster formation, are markedly enhanced by the bosonic quantum statistics. This contrasts with a recent quantum generalization of the AIM based onto hard-core bosons [Khasseh et al., Phys. Rev. Lett. 135, 248302 (2025)], where flocking, but neither its quantum-statistical stabilization nor aster states were observed as a consequence of interactions. Moreover, we investigate the competition of this quantum statistical stabilization of collective phases with their suppression by the quantum fluctuations induced by a transverse external magnetic field.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers experimentally demonstrated continuous-variable Einstein–Podolsky–Rosen correlations between two macroscopically separated mechanical oscillators. They achieved this by coupling the resonators to a superconducting qubit and engineering a Two-Mode Squeezing (TMS) interaction, which functions as the core element of an SU(1,1) interferometer. This work confirms that quantum correlations stronger than entanglement can be observed in large-scale physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges macroscopic quantum mechanics with fundamental quantum information protocols by using mechanical oscillators as a robust platform for continuous-variable entanglement witnesses. It expands the operational toolbox in cQAD systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To experimentally observe continuous-variable Einstein–Podolsky–Rosen (EPR) correlations between two spatially-separated mechanical oscillators, which are quantum correlations stronger than entanglement.

**Main result.** The experiment successfully observed these continuous-variable EPR correlations by realizing a mechanical SU(1,1) interferometer using engineered Two-Mode Squeezing (TMS) interactions. This demonstrates that macroscopic systems can exhibit quantum correlations beyond simple entanglement.

**Method.** The study employs parametric driving to engineer the TMS interaction between two high-overtone bulk acoustic wave resonators coupled via a superconducting qubit. Quantum correlations are then witnessed by implementing an SU(1,1) interferometer sequence.

**Model / system.** The platform is a circuit quantum acoustodynamics (cQAD) device featuring two HBARs coupled to a transmon qubit. The dynamics are modeled using Hamiltonians that allow for the generation of effective Two-Mode Squeezing (TMS) interactions, which replace traditional beam splitters in the interferometer.

**Key observables.** EPR steering criterion $E(\phi)$, output phonon number $\langle N_{	ext{out}} angle$, and interference fringes observed by measuring output populations as a function of relative phase $\phi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Effective mass ($\sim 16 \, \mu	ext{g}$), coupling strengths ($g_a, g_b$), parametric drive strength, and the time duration of the squeezing operations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the engineered TMS interaction accurately models the required quantum operation for the interferometer. Theoretical predictions for the TMS rate showed minor deviations from measurement.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the device micrograph and spectroscopy confirming non-overlapping phonon modes, followed by characterization of the TMS rate dependence on drive strength, and finally showing interference fringes and the minimum value of the EPR steering criterion $E(\phi)$ vs. phase $\phi$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical platform (cQAD) and the goal of observing strong quantum correlations. It then details the characterization of the key interaction (TMS rate calibration). Finally, it applies this calibrated interaction to build and test the mechanical SU(1,1) interferometer to witness EPR steering.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum correlations are essential for achieving quantum advantage in computing, communication and sensing. Moreover, their observation challenges and constrains our fundamental understanding of nature. Mechanical oscillators in the quantum regime provide an appealing platform for preparing and investigating quantum correlations at macroscopic scales. Despite substantial progress, however, continuous-variable quantum correlations stronger than entanglement have not yet been observed in this macroscopic regime. Here, we report the experimental observation of continuous-variable Einstein-Podolsky-Rosen correlations between two spatially-separated mechanical oscillators with an effective mass of $\sim 16 \,μg$ each. This is achieved by coupling them to a superconducting qubit which allows for engineering a two-mode squeezing interaction when parametrically driven. Crucially, we show that this interaction can be used to witness quantum correlations through the realization of a mechanical SU(1,1) interferometer. Our results expand the toolbox of operations in circuit quantum acoustodynamics and demonstrate that quantum correlations stronger than entanglement can also be observed in macroscopic systems, thereby shedding light on the boundary between quantum and classical regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** This research experimentally and theoretically characterizes Measurement-Induced State-Transitions (MIST) in a fluxonium qubit during microwave readout. By incorporating the effects of array modes into advanced theoretical models, the authors accurately predict error hotspots across the entire external flux range. This work is crucial for improving the fidelity and scalability of superconducting quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles fundamental limitations in superconducting quantum hardware readout fidelity by modeling complex, non-linear coupling mechanisms involving higher-order circuit modes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study characterizes Measurement-Induced State-Transitions (MIST) in a fluxonium superconducting qubit, which are non-ideal errors arising during dispersive quantum non-demolition (QND) readout.

**Main result.** The theory accurately predicts multiple regions of increased MIST, and these errors are shown to be significantly influenced by the transmission-line-like array modes of the superinductor.

**Method.** The work combines experimental characterization across the full flux range with advanced numerical simulations using Floquet branch analysis to model error sources.

**Model / system.** The system is a fluxonium superconducting qubit coupled to a readout resonator and an array of Josephson junctions forming the superinductor. The Hamiltonian incorporates terms for the fluxonium, resonator, and multiple array modes.

**Key observables.** Measurement-Induced State-Transitions (MIST) occurrence, assignment fidelity ($F_{|qangle_a}$), state-dependent resonator frequency shifts ($\Delta\omega_R$), and the dispersive shift $\chi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** External flux range ($\phi_{	ext{ext}}$), readout drive strength ($\Omega_{	ext{RT}}$ or $\Omega$), and the coupling strengths to array modes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as assuming the resonator is in equilibrium for calculating photon number, and using semi-classical methods for simulating the cavity field evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail spectroscopic measurements (e.g., qubit frequency vs. flux), readout fidelity dependence on drive strength, and calculated error terms ($E^{MIST}$) plotted against external flux or drive amplitude, often visualized via branch overlap analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from initial parameter extraction using spectroscopy to developing an error separation model that distinguishes MIST errors from other decoherence sources. It then applies advanced Floquet branch analysis (including array modes) to map out the full phase space of MIST susceptibility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting qubits are most often measured using dispersive readout, which, ideally, implements a projective quantum non-demolition (QND) measurement. While a larger readout drive can increase the signal and, thus, reduce discrimination errors in the readout, strong microwave drives may also cause non-QND errors by driving the qubit to a state outside the computational subspace. In this work, we experimentally characterize measurement-induced state transitions (MIST) in a fluxonium qubit over its full external flux range. We further numerically calculate the MIST errors, and find that the theory accurately predicts eleven experimentally identified regions with increased MIST. In addition to transitions to higher fluxonium levels, we also find that, at certain flux points, MIST errors are dominated by transitions that include the transmission-line-like array modes of the fluxonium's superinductor. The excellent match between theory and experiment validates that the models accurately predict the occurrence of MIST in these systems, and further highlights the influence of array modes in fluxonium readout.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors experimentally demonstrate using reinforcement learning (RL) that feedback control can extract usable work from thermal baths in colloidal systems. They show this occurs via specific 'backward' trap movements, which is confirmed by comparing RL results against exact theoretical solutions derived from stochastic control theory. The methodology is robust enough to handle complex, externally forced environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful demonstration of how machine learning can solve complex optimal control problems in physical systems, offering insights into non-equilibrium thermodynamics and fluctuation-driven energy harvesting that are highly relevant to open quantum systems research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates how optimal closed-loop feedback control can minimize energetic costs when steering a physical system toward a target state in the presence of thermal fluctuations.

**Main result.** Optimal policies identified via Reinforcement Learning (RL) quantitatively match analytical solutions, demonstrating that work extraction ($\langle W angle < 0$) occurs by exploiting backward trap displacements, even extending to heterogeneous non-equilibrium environments.

**Method.** The study combines experimental realization using real-time optical tweezers and in situ RL (PPO) with rigorous theoretical analysis derived from stochastic control theory (LQG formulation).

**Model / system.** The system is an overdamped colloidal particle suspended in a water–glycerol mixture, controlled by time-varying optical traps. The dynamics are governed by stochastic processes influenced by thermal noise.

**Key observables.** Average work ($\langle W angle$) as a function of protocol duration ($t_f$), and the classification of feedback actions into quadrants based on trap displacement ($\Delta\lambda$) versus work increment ($\Delta W$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Trap stiffness ($\kappa = 2 \pm 0.2 	ext{ pN}/\mu	ext{m}$), viscous relaxation time ($	au_r \approx 25 	ext{ ms}$), and the protocol duration ($t_f$) which dictates the crossover regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that RL can successfully learn optimal policies without requiring a detailed dynamical model, although theoretical benchmarks are used for comparison.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $\langle W angle$ vs $t_f$, showing work extraction at long times; action distributions classify feedback into quadrants (Q1-Q4); and later figures map out these quadrant probabilities in both equilibrium and non-equilibrium settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the experimental setup and RL methodology for optimal control. It then analyzes the crossover of work cost with protocol time, identifying backward motion as key to energy extraction. Finally, it extends this analysis to heterogeneous external forcing fields, confirming the robustness of the feedback strategy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We experimentally realize finite-time feedback control in an overdamped colloidal system using real-time optical tweezers with in situ reinforcement learning (RL). By varying the protocol duration tf for displacing the optical trap between prescribed positions, the optimal strategies identified by RL reveal a crossover from deterministic dragging toward the target to feedback-assisted exploitation of thermal fluctuations, reducing and eventually overcoming the energetic cost. The resulting policies agree quantitatively with the exact optimal closed-loop solution. By extending the approach to spatially localized external forcing, we further show that RL can identify optimal feedback strategies in heterogeneous stochastic environments where direct analytical control design is challenging.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous Lindblad master equation describing holographic Brownian motion, which models heavy particle diffusion in a hot plasma using the AdS/CFT correspondence. By expanding the string endpoint influence functional and taking the Markovian limit, the authors derive dissipation coefficients for specific black hole backgrounds. The results confirm that the system thermalizes correctly, satisfying equipartition laws.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete realization of open quantum system dynamics (Lindbladian evolution) derived from fundamental gravitational/string theory principles (AdS/CFT), offering tools to study thermalization in strongly coupled systems relevant to condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives a Lindbladian description for holographic Brownian motion, modeling the stochastic dynamics of a heavy probe quark coupled to a thermal plasma.

**Main result.** It successfully constructs a completely positive and trace-preserving quantum master equation describing the system's time evolution in the Markovian limit. The analysis confirms energy relaxation approaches classical equipartition values under appropriate conditions.

**Method.** The methodology involves deriving an influence functional from the Nambu–Goto action, applying the Schwinger–Keldysh formalism, and taking a high-temperature local Markovian expansion to yield the Lindblad generator.

**Model / system.** The system is holographic Brownian motion, modeled by an open string endpoint fluctuation in AdS space coupled to a thermal bath. The dynamics are captured by a master equation involving dissipation coefficients derived from Green's functions for specific backgrounds like BTZ and AdS$_5$.

**Key observables.** Phase-space moments ($\langle q angle, \langle p angle$), energy relaxation rates, and the stationary covariance matrix $\Sigma(	au)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High temperature regime ($T^*$), heavy mass limit ($M_q$), and specific background parameters (BTZ/AdS$_5$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the high-temperature Markovian limit, a local derivative expansion of the influence functional, and quadratic approximations of the underlying actions.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes; however, the analysis involves deriving coefficients (Table 3.1) and solving differential equations for covariance matrix components ($\Sigma(	au)$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem via the influence functional to performing the mathematical expansion using Green's functions, culminating in the explicit form of the Lindblad master equation and analyzing its solutions for free and harmonic potentials.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive a Lindbladian description of holographic Brownian motion in the high-temperature regime. Starting from the influence functional for a trailing string endpoint, we identify the corresponding quantum master equation and prove that it is completely positive and trace-preserving. We determine the coefficients of the Lindbladian explicitly for two holographic backgrounds: the BTZ black hole and the AdS$_5$ black brane, restricting in the latter case to the endpoint fluctuation along the $x^1$-direction. We then analyze the time evolution of phase-space moments, energy relaxation, and steady states.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using a hybrid Ferromagnet/Superconductor structure to create highly sensitive single-photon detectors that operate at significantly higher temperatures than previously possible. By engineering the magnetic coupling such that an incident photon triggers a controlled phase transition in the ferromagnet, the superconducting detection mechanism is activated efficiently while minimizing dark noise. This breakthrough promises simpler and more practical quantum sensing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work models a non-equilibrium phase transition driven by external stimuli (photons) in a coupled magnet-superconductor system, which has strong parallels with studying energy dissipation and thermalization dynamics in quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop infrared single-photon detectors that operate at significantly higher temperatures than current cryogenic limits (T < 1K) by engineering the operating mechanism.

**Main result.** A hybrid Ferromagnet/Superconductor bilayer can achieve high sensitivity up to 3.25–3.75K by coupling a photon-induced order-to-disorder transition in the ferromagnet to a superconducting phase transition.

**Method.** The analysis uses vortex-crossing theory, time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau simulations, and atomistic spin dynamics to model the coupled magnetic and superconducting transitions.

**Model / system.** The system is a hybrid Ferromagnet/Superconductor (FM/S) bilayer. The mechanism relies on the local heating from an incident photon causing disordering in the FM layer, which modifies the magnetic field profile and lowers the vortex crossing barrier in the superconductor.

**Key observables.** Noise Equivalent Power (NEP), Dark Count Rate (DCR), System Detection Efficiency (SDE), and operating temperature ($T_{op}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating Temperature (3.25–3.75K), Curie Temperature ($T_{	ext{Curie}}$) of the FM layer, magnetic field strength ($\sim 40	ext{mT}$), and thermal time constant ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes that the single-photon event triggers a localized hotspot leading to an order-to-disorder transition in the ferromagnet, which then drives the superconducting detection mechanism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the operational cycle (no photon vs. photon incidence), show the dependence of DCR and NEP on applied magnetic fields and thermal time constants, and map out potential device designs based on critical temperatures ($T_{	ext{crit}}$, $T_{	ext{Curie}}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the limitations of current detectors, then proposing the hybrid FM/S structure. It details the underlying physics using vortex-crossing theory and simulations to show how magnetic field engineering allows for higher temperature operation while maintaining high sensitivity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The development of midwave and longwave infrared single photon detectors is crucial for their emerging applications in spectroscopy, remote sensing, exoplanet detection, and free space quantum communications. However, existing sensors need to be operated at extremely low temperatures (0.08-0.9K) to reduce dark noise and hence require the use of advanced cryogenics such as dilution refrigerators or $^3$He cryogens, significantly limiting applications. Here we propose a vortex-engineering approach based on a hybrid phase transition in a ferromagnet/superconductor bilayer to increase the operating temperature of infrared single photon detectors up to 3.75K. We show that the introduction of a ferromagnetic layer produces a local magnetic field which impedes vortex crossing in the superconductor, reducing dark noise. When a single photon is incident, the photon-induced hotspot causes an order-to-disorder transition in the ferromagnet, leading to a vortex-induced phase transition in the superconducting layer. By engineering the ferromagnet's Curie temperature to be close to the device's operating temperature, single photon sensitivity can be achieved at increased operating temperatures. We predict at midwave/longwave infrared wavelengths (3-14$μ$m) the operating temperature can be raised to 3.25-3.75K, enabling significantly simpler cooling systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces subchiral symmetry as a general control principle for manipulating the corner modes found in higher-order topological insulators using the 2D BBH model. By selectively breaking these symmetries, researchers can isolate individual zero-energy states and execute adiabatic protocols to coherently transfer both single modes and superpositions of two modes. This capability was successfully demonstrated on an IBM quantum processor, establishing a pathway for programmable topological state control.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses programmable topological dynamics by linking symmetry protection (subchiral symmetry) to physical state manipulation, which is highly relevant for designing robust quantum simulators or quantum memory elements based on topological materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a general, controllable principle for manipulating topological corner modes (zero-energy states) in higher-order topological insulators (SOTIs). This requires selectively creating, isolating, and coherently transferring these degenerate modes.

**Main result.** Subchiral symmetry was established as the control mechanism, allowing for the stepwise reduction of fourfold zero-mode manifolds to single isolated modes. Furthermore, adiabatic protocols successfully transferred both single modes and coherent superpositions of two modes with high fidelity on quantum hardware.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical analysis using symmetry protection (subchiral symmetry) with experimental validation via time-dependent Hamiltonian engineering and implementation on an IBM quantum processor.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the two-dimensional Benalcazar–Bernevig–Hughes (BBH) model, a paradigmatic SOTI. The control is achieved by selectively breaking the four associated subchiral symmetries using controlled intercell hoppings in the Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Zero-energy corner states, single/superposition state transfer fidelity ($F^*$), and density distributions $P(x, y)$ showing mode localization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The control strength of the subchiral symmetry breaking terms ($H_\alpha$) and the adiabatic evolution time scale ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ability to realize and maintain phase coherence during state transfer protocols on physical quantum hardware (IBM processor).

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the energy spectrum showing stepwise lifting of zero modes using symmetry breaking, and density plots/fidelity curves demonstrating successful single-mode and superposition state transfer between corners.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first introducing the BBH model and its fourfold degenerate corner states. It then details how subchiral symmetries provide a control principle for sequentially isolating these modes. Finally, it presents adiabatic protocols to coherently transfer both single and two-state superpositions of these modes, validated numerically and on quantum hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Higher-order topological phases provide robust corner modes, but their use requires controllable creation, isolation, and transfer of individual modes and their superpositions. Here we demonstrate, using the two-dimensional Benalcazar-Bernevig-Hughes model as an example, that subchiral symmetry provides a general control principle for manipulating topological corner modes. The conventional chiral symmetry decomposes into four subchiral symmetries, each associated with one zero-energy corner mode. By selectively breaking these subsymmetries with controlled intercell hoppings, we reduce the fourfold corner-state manifold step by step to single isolated modes. We further design adiabatic protocols that transfer either a single corner state or a superposition of two corner states between selected corners, while preserving the relative phase in the latter case. Both numerical simulations and IBM quantum-processor implementations show that the proposed protocols can be executed with high fidelity, establishing subchiral symmetry as a route to programmable higher-order topological state manipulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the randomness induced in quantum error correction codes (like the surface code) after syndrome measurements. It establishes that this resulting ensemble of logical states is mathematically equivalent to the scattering matrix statistics of a quantum dot. This connection allows researchers to use established tools from random matrix theory and mesoscopic physics to predict fundamental properties of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful physical realization of abstract concepts in QEC by connecting them directly to well-understood physics models from condensed matter theory, specifically mesoscopic transport phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study characterizes the statistical properties of post-measurement logical states (the Projected Logical Ensemble, PLE) arising in Quantum Error Correction (QEC) on topological codes.

**Main result.** The PLE is shown to be isomorphic to the scattering matrix ensemble of a quantum dot, establishing a fundamental link between QEC universality and mesoscopic physics.

**Method.** The analysis maps the syndrome extraction process onto calculating the scattering matrix ($S$) of an effective quantum dot network derived from the surface code structure.

**Model / system.** The system uses the Surface Code (SC) on 2D lattices, where measurement back-action generates randomness. This is mathematically mapped to a mesoscopic quantum dot whose transport properties are governed by Random Matrix Theory (RMT).

**Key observables.** Projected Logical Ensemble (PLE), scattering matrices ($S$), bulk conductivity ($g$), and the Wasserstein-1 distance ($W_1$) used for comparison.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coherent rotation angle ($\phi$) determines the regime, with a critical threshold $\phi_{th}$ separating insulating/coherent regimes from metallic/chaotic ones.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes maximum-likelihood decoding and relies on establishing an isomorphism between the quantum circuit evolution and the scattering matrix of the associated quantum dot.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SC geometry, its mapping to a network model with $S$-matrices, and plots show the dependence of bulk conductivity ($g$) and logical error rate ($P_L$) on the rotation angle $\phi$, identifying critical thresholds.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first defining the PLE via syndrome measurement (Chunk 1), establishing the isomorphism to quantum dot scattering matrices (Chunks 2 & 4), analyzing constraints using tensor networks and symmetry classes (Chunks 3, 5), and finally quantifying the transition between regimes using RMT diagnostics like conductance scaling (Chunks 6 & 7).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Measurements underpin active quantum error correction (QEC) and have been recognized as a source of novel measurement-induced many-body phenomena. Here, we study the statistical properties of post-measurement logical states arising in QEC on topological codes subject to deterministic transversal unitary gates. Upon syndrome extraction followed by maximum-likelihood decoding, a Born-weighted ensemble arises which we dub the "projected logical ensemble" (PLE). Focusing on surface codes subject to uniform single-qubit Pauli-$X$ rotations, we characterize the measurement-induced randomness of the PLE. To this end, we show that for a code with a single logical qubit, the PLE is isomorphic to an ensemble of scattering matrices describing mesoscopic quantum dots obtained from a 2D Majorana network model with suitable boundary conditions. We uncover regimes where these quantum dots are chaotic such that their scattering matrices are well-described by random matrix theory. In these regimes, the PLE approaches a universal ensemble that is maximally random up to symmetry and decoder-induced constraints. The symmetry constraints, set by stabilizer and logical operator weights, realize Altland-Zirnbauer classes D or DIII, which we both illustrate. Our results establish a fundamental connection between emergent universality concepts in mesoscopic physics, quantum many-body systems, and QEC.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the time evolution of entanglement entropy in a 2D CFT subjected to periodic driving, revealing distinct dynamical phases. By comparing these results with calculations on the holographic dual ($	ext{AdS}_3$), the authors provide strong evidence supporting the Faulkner-Lewkowycz-Maldacena conjecture for quantum corrections to entanglement entropy.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between dynamical phases observed in quantum field theory dynamics (like those driven by Floquet Hamiltonians) and geometric properties derived from gravity (via the FLM conjecture) is highly relevant to understanding non-equilibrium quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how semiclassical entanglement entropy (EE) evolves over time in driven quantum field theories, specifically looking for signatures of underlying dynamical phases.

**Main result.** The computed bulk entanglement entropy successfully matches the CFT entanglement entropy at leading order, providing a non-trivial verification of the Faulkner-Lewkowycz-Maldacena (FLM) conjecture for first quantum corrections.

**Method.** The authors employ both boundary CFT calculations using the replica method and holographic methods involving solving Einstein's equations to find backreacted geometries and calculating minimal areas.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D Conformal Field Theory (CFT) in $1+1$ dimensions, driven by $	ext{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})$ parameters. Its gravitational dual is Anti-de Sitter space ($	ext{AdS}_3$).

**Key observables.** Entanglement Entropy (EE), the perturbed minimal area ($\delta A$), and the stress tensor expectation value ($\langle T_{\mu
u} angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Drive parameters ($\alpha, eta$), stroboscopic time ($nT$), and the gravitational coupling constant ($G_N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The bulk computation is restricted to small backreaction and short distance approximations; calculations are performed up to specific orders in $c$ or $G_N$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the CFT geometry on a cylinder, plot the total entanglement entropy ($	ext{S}_{	ext{tot}}$) versus time for different dynamical phases (Heating/Non-heating), and show cross-sections of the $	ext{AdS}_3$ bulk.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the effective Hamiltonian governing the stroboscopic dynamics, calculates the EE in the CFT using replica methods across three distinct dynamic regimes, and then performs a detailed holographic calculation matching these results by solving Einstein's equations for metric perturbations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the time evolution of semiclassical entanglement entropy in a class of $sl(2,\mathbb{R})$ driven states in a large $c$ conformal field theory (CFT) in $1+1$ spacetime dimensions. Upon varying the parameters of the drive, we find that the entanglement entropy exhibits the signature of the dynamical phases of the driven CFT. We further study the holographic dual of this CFT where the excited states of a minimally coupled scalar in $AdS_3$ induce a backreaction that modifies the background geometry. We compute the back reacted geometry by solving Einstein's equation with the expectation value of the stress tensor in the coherent state as the source term. Subsequently, we calculate the time evolution of the perturbed minimal area and the bulk entanglement entropy at $O(G_N^0)$, up to the sub-leading order in short distance approximation. These results match the CFT entanglement entropy in the boundary at $O(c^0)$, and serve as a nontrivial check of the Faulkner-Lewkowycz-Maldacena (FLM) conjecture for the first quantum correction of holographic entanglement entropy. The details of the check has been provided in the accompanying ancillary notebook.

</details>

### [Dimension-Free Approximate Tensorization of Quantum Hypercontractivity for Qudit Depolarizing Semigroups](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17729v1)

**Authors:** Yangjing Dong, Li Gao, Fengning Ou, Penghui Yao, Haigang Zhou  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17729v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `entanglement & information structure` **4/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

**Summary.** The paper develops rigorous mathematical techniques to prove that certain properties of quantum evolution, specifically hypercontractivity and the Log-Sobolev constant, exhibit approximate tensorization when applied to product qudit systems. This dimension-independent result is crucial for understanding how local dynamics scale across large quantum registers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful tools for analyzing quantum correlations across many sites in open quantum systems, directly impacting lower bounds on entanglement measures and information processing capacity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is proving dimension-free approximate tensorization bounds for hypercontractivity and logarithmic-Sobolev constants in quantum Markov semigroups.

**Main result.** The authors establish that the log-Sobolev constant of a product semigroup scales by at least a factor of 2/(3ln 2) times the single-site constant, independent of local dimension or spectral gap.

**Method.** The proof combines establishing exact tensorization for integer hypercontractivity parameters using complex interpolation to extend results to all real values. It relies heavily on structural properties like the Positive Off-Diagonal Scaling (PODS) condition.

**Model / system.** The study concerns reversible quantum Markov semigroups ($\Phi_t$) acting on qudits of arbitrary finite dimension $d$, specifically focusing on generalized depolarizing semigroups. The analysis uses tensor products of these processes, $\Phi^{\otimes n}_t$.

**Key observables.** Logarithmic-Sobolev constant ($\alpha_2(L)$), hypercontractivity parameters ($p, q$), and the approximation factor $eta \approx 0.96$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The local dimension $d$ (the result is independent of it); the number of sites $n$; and the time parameter $t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The semigroups must satisfy the Positive Off-Diagonal Scaling (PODS) property. The results are approximate ($eta < 1$) rather than exact tensorization.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 outlines a proof roadmap connecting hypercontractivity statements to Logarithmic-Sobolev Inequality applications for establishing the main theorem.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first proving specific hypercontractive inequalities (e.g., for $q=3$), then using complex interpolation to generalize these results, and finally applying these techniques to derive the dimension-independent tensorization bounds for $\alpha_2(L)$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove almost tensorization for hypercontractivity and logarithmic-Sobolev constants for a class of reversible quantum Markov semigroups satisfying the positive off-diagonal scaling (PODS) property. This class includes qubit examples and generalized depolarizing semigroups with respect to full-rank states in arbitrary finite dimensions. For any such semigroup $(Φ_t)_{t\ge 0}$ and every tensor power $n$, we show that the log-Sobolev constant of the product semigroup $Φ_t^{\otimes n}$ is at least $2/(3\ln 2)$, approximately 0.96, times the log-Sobolev constant of the single-site semigroup $Φ_t$, independently of $n$ and the local dimension $d$. The proof first establishes exact tensorization of the $(q,2)$-hypercontractive inequality for integer $q$, in particular $q=3$, and then extends the estimate to all real $q>2$ by complex interpolation; the standard implication from hypercontractivity to logarithmic-Sobolev inequalities yields the stated almost tensorization result. As an application of the same method, we also obtain sharp $(q,2)$-hypercontractivity estimates for qubit depolarizing channels.

</details>

**Summary.** The research uses ultrafast X-ray scattering at an XFEL facility to study transient Charge Density Waves (CDWs) in $	ext{LaTe}_3$ induced by light. They found that the photoexcited CDW state possesses a distinct wavevector and relative phase compared to the material's known equilibrium orders. This work provides direct, time-resolved evidence of novel electronic phases inaccessible under standard thermodynamic conditions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates how non-equilibrium excitation can stabilize or reveal fundamentally different electronic ground states compared to those predicted by static theory, which is crucial for understanding strongly correlated materials dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating novel electronic states, specifically transient Charge Density Waves (CDWs), induced by photoexcitation that are inaccessible under equilibrium conditions.

**Main result.** The transient CDW state exhibits a distinct ordering wavevector ($\mathbf{q}_{a,tr} \approx (2/7, 0, 0)$) compared to the equilibrium order ($\mathbf{q}_a \approx (5/7, 0, 0)$), suggesting a $\pi$ phase offset between adjacent planes.

**Method.** Ultrafast x-ray scattering using an XFEL provides high momentum and time resolution to probe the dynamics of these transient phases.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on rare-earth tritellurides ($	ext{RTe}_3$), which are known for exhibiting complex, competing charge ordering instabilities like CDWs.

**Key observables.** CDW Bragg peak location in reciprocal space ($\mathbf{q}$), time evolution of intensity ($\Delta I(\mathbf{q}, t)$), and correlation length along different crystal directions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultrafast excitation pulse duration (e.g., 35 fs) and the characteristic wavevectors defining transient vs. equilibrium orders.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that x-ray scattering can microscopically resolve the structural differences between photoinduced and equilibrium phases.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of $\mathbf{q}$ vectors in reciprocal space, showing the distinct location of the transient peak relative to equilibrium peaks; time evolution plots track the narrowing/shifting of the transient peak profile over picoseconds.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the background on photoinduced CDWs, presents the experimental methodology using XFEL scattering, details the observation that the transient state has a different wavevector and phase relationship than the equilibrium state, and concludes by highlighting the unique nature of this photoexcited phase.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photoexcitation can induce novel states in materials that are inaccessible in equilibrium, a recent example being the light-induced charge density wave (CDW) observed in $\text{LaTe}_3$. Here, we investigate this transient CDW using infrared-pump x-ray-probe scattering at a free-electron laser, with high momentum and time resolution. We find that the transient CDW Bragg peak is broad in reciprocal space, indicating a highly disordered state. The ordering wavevector of the transient state is different from the equilibrium orders that develop in this class of materials - the transient peak appears near (2/7, 0, 0) reciprocal lattice units, whereas the equilibrium $a$ order and $c$ order occur at $\approx (5/7, 0, 0)$ and $(0, 0, 2/7)$, respectively. The transient CDW is therefore distinct from the equilibrium $a$ order, differing in the relative phase of the CDW displacement between the two equivalent nearly-square Te-Te nets in the conventional unit cell. Our work highlights how photoexcitation can access states with no equilibrium analog, and how x-ray scattering can provide microscopic insight into such elusive phases.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a novel tensor-network approach to realize the holographic principle in de Sitter space, moving beyond the established Anti-de Sitter framework. By applying non-unitary continuous MERA to a critical fermion chain, the authors show that entanglement structure naturally encodes an emergent dS geometry. This establishes a bottom-up dictionary (dS/cMERA) by showing how extremal surfaces map onto zero-cost links in the tensor network skeleton.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, calculable mechanism (bond counting) to connect quantum information concepts (MERA) directly to fundamental aspects of quantum gravity (dS holography), which is highly relevant for theoretical physicists studying emergent spacetime structures from microscopic degrees of freedom.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is extending the holographic principle, which relates boundary entanglement to bulk geometry (like AdS/CFT), to de Sitter (dS) spacetimes by developing a microscopic tensor-network framework.

**Main result.** The authors establish a bottom-up dS/(c)MERA correspondence, demonstrating that non-unitary continuous MERA applied to a critical fermion chain yields an emergent de Sitter spacetime whose entanglement entropy scales correctly with the extremal surface length.

**Method.** The work combines concepts from quantum information theory (MERA/tensor networks), condensed matter physics (non-Hermitian criticality), and general relativity (dS geodesics) by mapping continuum geometric quantities onto discrete bond-counting rules.

**Model / system.** The model is a non-Hermitian critical fermion chain, analyzed using the continuous Multi-Scale Entanglement Renormalization Ansatz (cMERA). This system exhibits criticality that leads to an emergent Lorentzian de Sitter spacetime geometry in its holographic dual.

**Key observables.** Non-unitary critical entanglement entropy ($S \propto i \ln l$), extremal Ryu-Takayanagi surfaces, and the resulting dS metric components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The non-Hermitian coupling parameters ($\mu$) defining criticality; the scale renormalization parameter $u$; and the geometric constants related to the de Sitter curvature ($\Lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that a bottom-up tensor network description can capture the physics of dS holography, requiring the use of biorthogonal structures due to non-Hermiticity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the embedding of the cMERA circuit into the de Sitter Penrose diagram and show how the extremal geodesic path corresponds to a specific zero-cost continuation (null link) within the tensor network structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first reviewing AdS/CFT holography via MERA, then generalizing this framework to non-Hermitian systems to address dS space. The core is establishing the continuum metric from entanglement flow and subsequently deriving the discrete bond-counting rules that reproduce the expected logarithmic scaling for the dS extremal surface.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Extending the holographic principle to de Sitter (dS) spacetimes remains one of the most vital open frontiers in quantum gravity, where a microscopic, bottom-up tensor-network framework that relates boundary quantum data to emergent de Sitter spacetime is still lacking. In this work, we first show the emergence of de Sitter spacetime from boundary entanglement by formulating a non-unitary continuous multi-scale entanglement renormalization ansatz (cMERA) for a concrete non-Hermitian critical fermion chain. Within this emergent spacetime, we analyze the associated geodesics and show that they act as extremal Ryu-Takayanagi (RT) surfaces undergoing a smooth timelike-to-null transition. Remarkably, we demonstrate that this continuum trajectory dictates a distinct tensor-network architecture in which the bond-counting contribution naturally truncates at the discrete timelike-to-null transition toward the deep infrared. In the resulting architecture, the null ray along the horizon is represented by zero-cost links, since the associated cut severs no tensor legs. This network structure successfully reproduces the logarithmic scaling of non-unitary critical entanglement entropy, offering a bond-counting picture for the de Sitter RT formula. Our results provide the long-sought dS/(c)MERA correspondence at the level of both emergent spacetime and discrete holographic entanglement.

</details>

**Summary.** The research successfully generated the first fully-distributed GHZ state involving single atomic qubits across three remote nodes. This was achieved by coupling trapped ions to photons and interfering these photons at an external generator. The results confirm multipartite entanglement via Mermin's inequality violation, advancing the feasibility of large-scale quantum networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses scalability in quantum networking by demonstrating multipartite entanglement across physically separated nodes using photonic links, which is a cornerstone challenge for distributed quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to generate and demonstrate fully-distributed Greenberger–Horne–Zeilinger (GHZ) states of qubits across a three-node quantum network using photonic interconnects.

**Main result.** The authors reported the first such demonstration, achieving a bounded fidelity between 0.841 and 0.881 and closing the detection loophole for the first time in this fully-distributed multipartite entangled state.

**Method.** Entanglement was generated by coupling single trapped ion qubits to emitted photons, which were then sent through optical fibers to an external GHZ-state generator where they interfered.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes a three-node quantum network composed of spatially separated trapped ions ($^{138}	ext{Ba}^+$). Entanglement is mediated by single photons collected from spontaneous emission and interfering at a central optical setup.

**Key observables.** GHZ State Fidelity (F), violation of Mermin's inequality, entanglement generation rate, and pairwise visibilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fidelity bounds ($0.841 \le F \le 0.881$), entanglement rate ($\sim 0.095/	ext{sec}$), and the classical bound violation for Mermin's parameter ($M > 2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fidelity analysis relies on bounding techniques using measured populations and parity contrasts, while error sources include photonic spatial mode mismatch and polarization mixing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the three ion trap nodes and the GHZ generator setup; subsequent figures detail state characterization via parity fringes and correlator measurements used to quantify entanglement violation.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental setup, describes the process of generating single-photon entangled states between ions and photons, outlines the interference mechanism at the GHZ generator, and finally presents quantitative results on fidelity bounding and Mermin inequality violation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distributed entanglement across multi-node quantum networks is essential for a wide range of quantum technologies, including modular quantum computers, distributed sensing and metrology, and multi-party secure communication protocols. Such large-scale quantum networks will require photonic interconnects to generate and sustain entangled states across localized nodes. Previously, three-node distributed Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states have been generated between solid-state qubits and atomic ensembles, but not yet in the platform of individual atomic qubits, which can be replicated, detected, and individually controlled with high fidelity. Here we report the first fully-distributed GHZ state of qubits across a three-node quantum network of single atomic memories, using photonic interconnects. We achieve a bounded fidelity of $0.841(17) \leq \mathcal{F} \leq 0.881(17)$ at an entanglement generation rate of 0.095(5)/sec and measure a clear violation of Mermin's inequality while closing the detection loophole for the first time in a fully-distributed multipartite entangled state.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the Brownian gyration of an inertial ellipsoid moving under asymmetric trapping potentials and thermal gradients. By using inertial Langevin dynamics, it quantifies how particle shape, inertia, and external asymmetries dictate the steady-state rotational motion. The findings reveal that non-equilibrium effects are crucial for measurable gyration signals.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed, quantitative framework for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in mesoscopic systems, bridging concepts from classical statistical mechanics (NESS) with measurable physical quantities like angular momentum and heat flow.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Brownian gyration (BG) of an inertial, spherically asymmetric ellipsoid under non-equilibrium conditions.

**Main result.** Gyration is fundamentally dependent on the particle's shape, orientation, and inertia, showing clear differences from spherical or overdamped counterparts. The signal-to-noise ratio can be maximized by tuning potential asymmetry ($\alpha$) and shape anisotropy ($|\Delta\gamma|$).

**Method.** The analysis employs inertial Langevin dynamics to model coupled translational and rotational motion in a dissipative medium.

**Model / system.** The system is an ellipsoid with mass $m$, confined in a spherically asymmetric trap potential $U(x, y)$, and subjected to two distinct thermal reservoirs driving it into a Non-Equilibrium Steady State (NESS).

**Key observables.** Brownian gyration ($\langle h angle$), cross-correlation $\langle xy angle$, average specific angular momentum, and the heat flux $\langle\dot{Q}_iangle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Trap asymmetry ($\alpha$), temperature difference ($\Delta T = T_y - T_x$), shape anisotropy ($|\Delta\gamma|$), particle inertia ($m$), and external torques ($k_\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The translational dynamics are treated as underdamped while orientational dynamics are overdamped in some analyses; approximations involve linearizing equations of motion for small anisotropy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of $\langle h angle$ on potential asymmetry ($\alpha$) and shape anisotropy ($|\Delta\gamma|$), showing non-monotonic behavior, as well as plots detailing heat flux variation with these parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the Langevin equations for translation and orientation, analyzes the steady-state gyration using numerical simulations, derives analytical results for small anisotropies, and extends the model to include external magnetic fields or analyze heat flux generation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent studies on Brownian gyration (BG) have focused primarily on spherically symmetric particles under overdamped conditions. To explore BG in the underdamped regime with a spherically asymmetric particle, we investigate the inertial dynamics of a microscopic ellipsoid in a dissipative medium. The particle is confined in a spherically asymmetric trap and simultaneously coupled to two distinct thermal reservoirs. This configuration drives the system into a nonequilibrium steady state (NESS) characterised by BG, which is quantified by the mean and fluctuation of the particle's specific angular momentum. Using inertial Langevin dynamics, we systematically analyze how this microscopic gyration depends not merely on the trap asymmetry and temperature difference, but also on the particle's intrinsic physical properties like shape and axial orientation, besides inertia. Our study uncovers fundamental differences between the gyration of spherical and non-spherical particles in overdamped as well as underdamped conditions, at microscopic scales. These findings provide key insights for optimizing Brownian gyration across a broader landscape of experimentally tuneable parameters.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the Born-Reciprocal Tensor Network (BRTN) as a novel computational framework to model Quantum Field Theories exhibiting UV/IR mixing. It reframes this mixing as a measurable entanglement structure across renormalization scales. The key finding is that while mixing occurs, standard local QFT behavior is recovered in the large volume limit by analyzing how this cross-bridge entanglement vanishes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a sophisticated information-theoretic lens (entanglement structure) to analyze fundamental concepts like renormalization group flow and scale separation in QFTs, which is highly relevant for understanding emergent physics in complex quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses UV/IR mixing in Quantum Field Theories (QFTs), where traditional scale separation assumptions fail due to residual ultraviolet structure influencing long-distance physics.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that the Born-Reciprocal Tensor Network (BRTN) provides a calculable platform for studying this mixing, showing that standard Wilsonian behavior is restored in the large-volume limit by analyzing cross-bridge entanglement.

**Method.** The core method involves recasting UV/IR mixing as a Hilbert-space phenomenon and implementing it using the novel Born-Reciprocal Tensor Network (BRTN), which extends tensor network techniques to incorporate global phase-space reciprocity.

**Model / system.** The model is based on QFTs, specifically analyzing scalar fields ($\phi_A$) interacting with a reciprocal background ($\phi_B$), structured within the BRTN framework. The analysis compares this structure to standard lattice QFT simulations using TTNs/TEBD.

**Key observables.** Cross-bridge entanglement (criterion for effective description), Effective State Entropy ($S_{	ext{eff}}$), Structure Factor $S(k)$, and momentum non-conservation amplitudes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large volume limit ($N 	o \infty$), fundamental scale ($M^*$), physical mass ($m_{	ext{phys}}$), and the coupling constant ($g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that UV/IR mixing can be formulated as a global Hilbert-space correlation structure, which the BRTN is designed to capture.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate comparisons between Wilsonian tracing (information loss) and TTN representation (global purity), and show the geometric organization of correlations in 1D QFTs using tree structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the failure of standard Wilsonian assumptions due to UV/IR mixing, introducing the BRTN as a Hilbert-space realization, demonstrating its calculational power by analyzing entanglement criteria, and finally showing how normal QFT behavior is recovered in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Renormalisation is traditionally understood to be a Wilsonian memoryless process in which ultraviolet (UV) degrees of freedom gradually decouple, leaving an autonomous infrared (IR) description. However this need not be the case: in UV/IR mixed theories correlations between widely separated scales can persist. In this work I recast UV/IR mixing as a Hilbert-space phenomenon, realised as correlations across renormalisation scales. This formulation is implemented using the Born-Reciprocal Tensor Network (BRTN), a new configuration of tensor network that is globally symmetric under phase-space reciprocity. On this network I prepare the vacuum and reproduce the expected radiative corrections. The resulting renormalisation geometry exhibits memory, with a bridge linking reciprocal representations of IR physics, whose cross-bridge entanglement provides a precise criterion for the viability of an effective description. I analyse when this criterion is met, and show that there is a large-volume limit, with the fundamental scale held fixed, in which the obstruction to a local description scales away: Wilsonian behaviour is restored and renormalisation forgets. The BRTN therefore provides a concrete and calculable platform for UV/IR mixing.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates interacting quantum walks on 1D lattices to control the relationship between particle transport and entanglement. By employing tailored potentials, such as generalized Coulomb-like interactions, the authors map out a phase diagram revealing multiple dynamical regimes. The key finding is the identification of an optimal intermediate regime that maximizes both transport efficiency and quantum correlations simultaneously.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete theoretical tools for designing quantum systems where controlled interactions can steer dynamics toward optimal states that balance coherent spreading with strong non-local correlations, relevant for simulating complex many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is controlling the interplay between particle propagation (transport) and quantum correlation generation in interacting quantum walks.

**Main result.** A rich phase diagram was established using Coulomb-like interactions, identifying a novel intermediate regime that simultaneously optimizes transport efficiency and entanglement for correlated dynamics.

**Method.** Numerical simulations of the two-particle Schrödinger equation were performed, analyzing spectral properties and time evolution across different interaction potentials.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two distinguishable continuous-time quantum walkers on parallel 1D lattices. The interactions are modeled using distance-dependent potentials, including linear and generalized Coulomb-like forms.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy (S), single-particle standard deviation ($\sigma(t)$), meeting probability ($P_M(t)$), and the two-particle band structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($\lambda$), spatial scaling ($\gamma$), decay rate ($\alpha$), and the specific form of the potential (on-site, linear, Coulomb-like).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes a periodic extension of the lattice for band structure calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transition from free to bound states using on-site interactions ($\lambda$ dependence) and show Bloch-like oscillations in single-particle distributions when linear potentials are applied, alongside phase diagrams mapping entropy vs. interaction parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates different interaction models (on-site, linear, Coulomb-like), deriving key observables like entanglement entropy and transport metrics to map out a comprehensive phase diagram of dynamical regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Controlling the interplay between particle propagation and quantum correlation generation is a central challenge in quantum transport. Here, we investigate two distinguishable continuous-time quantum walkers evolving on parallel one-dimensional lattices, interacting via distance-dependent potentials. While on-site interactions reproduce the typical bosonic behaviour, extending the interaction to a linear potential over multiple neighbors introduces controlled Bloch-like oscillations and shifts the bound-pair regime to stronger couplings. More generally, we explore a Coulomb-like interaction parameterized by strength, spatial scaling, and decay rate. This reveals a rich phase diagram including four distinct dynamical regimes: (i) a high-entropy, oscillatory regime akin to a linear potential; (ii) a strongly localized, bound-pair regime; (iii) a novel intermediate regime combining near-ballistic spreading with strong correlations; and (iv) a weakly interacting, free-propagation regime. Notably, regime (iii) achieves concurrent optimization of transport efficiency and entanglement, offering a sweet spot for correlated quantum dynamics. Our results provide a tool for designing interaction-engineered quantum walks with potential applications in quantum information processing and simulations.

</details>
