**Summary.** This theoretical study maps out the complex magnetic phase diagram of monolayer CrTe2, showing that its magnetic order (FM, Z-AFM, DS-AFM) is exquisitely tunable by applying mechanical strain or controlling carrier doping. By calculating exchange interactions and simulating Raman spectra, the authors provide a comprehensive framework for designing 2D spintronic materials where magnetism can be engineered from first principles.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the detailed analysis of spin-lattice coupling (quantifying $\partial^2 J_i / \partial u^2$) and the use of symmetry-breaking signatures in spectroscopy are highly relevant to understanding how external perturbations affect quantum ground states in correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how structural changes (strain) and electronic doping affect the magnetic ground state, phase stability, and transition temperatures in monolayer CrTe2.

**Main result.** The research establishes a rich magnetic phase diagram where the system can exhibit FM, Z-AFM, or DS-AFM phases depending on doping and strain. The magnetism is governed by competing exchange interactions beyond simple conduction electron models.

**Method.** The work combines first-principles DFT calculations with advanced modeling techniques like Heisenberg Monte Carlo simulations and analysis of orbital-resolved hopping parameters.

**Model / system.** The system is monolayer CrTe2, modeled using an effective spin Hamiltonian incorporating nearest, second, and third-neighbor exchange interactions. The strong correlation effects are treated via Hubbard U parameterization.

**Key observables.** Magnetic phase diagram (FM, Z-AFM, DS-AFM), magnetic ordering temperatures ($T_C$, $T_N$), magnetoelastic response, and zone-folded Raman modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice strain/parameter ($a$), carrier doping ($\delta$), Hubbard interaction strength ($U$), and exchange coupling ratios ($J_2/J_1, J_3/J_1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping complex magnetic interactions onto an effective Heisenberg model; some studies use the bilinear framework supplemented by biquadratic terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing transitions between FM and AFM phases based on strain/doping, and simulated Raman spectra are used to spectroscopically distinguish between different magnetic ground states (e.g., Z-AFM vs DS-AFM).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the interplay of structural parameters ($a$) and doping ($\delta$) by calculating exchange interactions ($J_i$), mapping out phase boundaries, and finally predicting unique spectroscopic fingerprints via Raman scattering simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interplay of structural, electronic, and magnetic degrees of freedom governs phase stability and critical temperatures in two-dimensional magnets. Controlling this coupling is essential for advancing fundamental understanding and spintronic applications. Combining first-principles calculations with Heisenberg Monte Carlo simulations, we reveal a rich magnetic phase diagram governed by the interplay of lattice strain and carrier density. These results provide a unified framework that reconciles diverse experimental reports on epitaxial layers and predicts a novel double-stripe antiferromagnetic phase, further stabilized by electron doping. Moreover, structural and electronic perturbations enable room-temperature ferromagnetism and antiferromagnetism. This magnetic evolution arises from competing, highly tunable direct and ligand-mediated exchange interactions in the presence of Ruderman-Kittel-Kasuya-Yosida coupling. By disentangling their individual contributions, we elucidate the underlying microscopic mechanisms, which transcends the conventional conduction electron picture. Finally, we quantify the colossal magnetoelastic response and identify zone-folded Raman modes that serve as unique experimental fingerprints for phase identification. Together, these results establish \ce{CrTe2} as a versatile platform for two-dimensional spintronics, where magnetic order and transition temperatures are tailorable via structural and electrical engineering.

</details>

**Authors:** Jessica Illiano, Caterina De Risi, Angela Sara Cacciapuoti, Marcello Caleffi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17773v1>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an entanglement-based switching fabric, the Edge-Controlled (EC) crossbar, as a solution for quantum routers. It proves that this framework achieves constant forwarding depth by utilizing multipartite graph states and local Pauli measurements. This makes it fundamentally superior to existing methods whose latency scales poorly with network size.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for designing scalable quantum interconnects by shifting complexity from physical state transmission to resource preparation (entanglement generation), which is crucial for realizing large-scale quantum networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a quantum-native switching-fabric framework for quantum routers that circumvents limitations imposed by the no-cloning theorem when relaying quantum information.

**Main result.** The proposed Edge-Controlled (EC) fabrics achieve constant forwarding depth, making them superior to matching-driven EPR-based architectures whose latency scales with the number of required connections.

**Method.** The authors formalize forwarding using multipartite entanglement resources (graph states) and derive an 'edge-controlled' design principle for non-blocking operation. Forwarding is realized through local Pauli measurements on these entangled resources.

**Model / system.** The system models a quantum router utilizing graph states $|	ext{G}angle$ as the forwarding resource, which can be structured monolithically (EC crossbar) or modularly (Clos-type). The core mechanism involves mapping classical switching concepts onto entanglement manipulation via Pauli measurements.

**Key observables.** Forwarding Depth ($D(M)$), Expected Forwarding Latency ($ar{T}(M)$), and resource overhead scaling (e.g., $O(N^2)$ vs. linear).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of ports ($N$), number of available measurement devices ($\sigma$), and the ratio defining modular efficiency.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework is presented as hardware-agnostic, assuming sufficient measurement parallelism for ideal depth-one operation in EC fabrics. The non-blocking property relies on structural conditions derived from graph theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the monolithic and modular structures of the EC crossbar and Clos topology, showing how ports connect to a global switching set $S$. Table I compares latency scaling across different architectures (Star, Memory-relay, EC).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the fundamental problem (no-cloning) to defining entanglement forwarding via graph states. It then derives structural conditions for non-blocking operation, instantiates these with monolithic and modular EC designs, and finally compares the resulting latency scaling against established EPR-based routing methods.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Forwarding in quantum networks cannot be realized by directly transposing classical switching fabrics, since the no-cloning theorem and the quantum measurement postulate constrain the direct relay of quantum information while ruling out copy-based buffering and inspection. In this paper, we propose a switching-fabric framework for quantum routers based on multipartite entanglement. Specifically, we formalize the notion of an entanglement-based switching fabric, in which a graph state acts as the forwarding resource and entanglement forwarding is realized through local Pauli measurements. We translate the classical notions of blocking and non-blocking operation into structural conditions for entanglement-based fabrics, by deriving the \textit{edge-controlled (EC) design principle} for non-blocking operation. We instantiate this principle through a monolithic \textit{EC crossbar} and a modular Clos-type EC fabric, for which we characterize resource scaling and identify the regime where the modular design becomes more resource-efficient than the monolithic one. Finally, a forwarding-latency analysis establishes a fundamental distinction between matching-oblivious and matching-driven forwarding: the proposed EC fabrics realize all requested input-output entanglement links with constant forwarding depth under sufficient measurement parallelism, whereas matching-driven EPR-based fabrics exhibit latency that scales with the number of requested connections. The proposed framework provides a hardware-agnostic foundation for quantum-router switching fabrics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies advanced techniques from spin-glass theory (replica method) to calculate the rate functional governing fluctuations of the spectral distribution in sparse random matrices. By deriving explicit formulas for cumulants and comparing them against numerical data, the authors establish a powerful theoretical framework for analyzing spectral observables in complex disordered systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on random matrices, the use of replica theory and large deviation principles is a powerful mathematical tool applicable to complex systems where mean-field approximations are necessary, providing rigorous statistical descriptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a replica-based framework to determine the rate functional for fluctuations of the empirical spectral distribution function ($i_C$) in diluted Hermitian random matrices.

**Main result.** It derives explicit expressions for the first two cumulants and computes candidate rate functions ($\Psi[\zeta]$) for linear spectral statistics, showing good agreement with numerical diagonalization in the accessible fluctuation regime.

**Method.** The core method involves using replica theory from spin-glass physics to calculate the scaled cumulant-generating functional ($F[\mu]$) via a replica-symmetric saddle-point approximation.

**Model / system.** The system under study is diluted Hermitian random matrices, exemplified by the adjacency matrices of unweighted Erdős–Rényi random graphs with a mean degree $c$. The analysis focuses on spectral statistics derived from these matrices.

**Key observables.** Empirical spectral distribution function ($i_C(x)$), cumulants ($\kappa_1, \kappa_2$), and the rate functional $\Psi[\zeta]$ for linear spectral statistics (e.g., Fourier coefficients).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mean degree $c$, system size $N$ (analyzed in the thermodynamic limit $N 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the replica-symmetric saddle-point ansatz and assumes that a large-deviation principle holds for $i_C$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare theoretical predictions for $\kappa_1$, $N\kappa_2$, and the rate function $\psi$ against results from exact numerical diagonalization, demonstrating quantitative agreement.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds up the formalism by first defining the generating functional using replicas, then deriving explicit formulas for cumulants via functional derivatives, and finally computing the rate function $\psi(u)$ through transformations relating it to the cumulant-generating functional $f(eta)$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a replica-based framework for the scaled cumulant-generating functional of the empirical spectral distribution function $i_C$ of diluted Hermitian random matrices. Within a replica-symmetric saddle-point assumption, this construction yields a candidate rate functional for fluctuations of $i_C$. As an illustrative application, we consider adjacency matrices of unweighted Erdős-Rényi random graphs with mean degree $c$. We derive explicit expressions for the first two cumulants of $i_C$, indicate how higher cumulants can be obtained from further functional derivatives, and compute the rate function of Fourier coefficients, equivalently of selected linear spectral statistics. The replica-symmetric predictions are tested against exact numerical diagonalization and show good agreement in the accessible fluctuation regime. The approach provides a basis for studying rate functionals of spectral observables in sparse random matrix ensembles.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a stochastic thermodynamic framework for diffusion models by introducing the Time-asymmetry Entropy Production (TAEP). It proves that the average TAEP is proportional to the score-matching objective and obeys fluctuation theorems. This offers a deep, physical understanding of why these generative models achieve high sampling diversity.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous physical interpretation of modern generative AI objectives using established tools from non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, linking machine learning loss functions directly to thermodynamic quantities like entropy production.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a stochastic thermodynamic framework for score-based diffusion models, relating their training objective to fundamental principles like entropy production.

**Main result.** The average Time-asymmetry Entropy Production (TAEP) is exactly proportional to the score-matching objective, and TAEP obeys exact fluctuation theorems. This provides a thermodynamic explanation for the superior sampling diversity of diffusion models.

**Method.** The authors introduce TAEP, a trajectory-dependent quantity derived from forward and reverse Langevin dynamics, and apply concepts from non-equilibrium statistical physics, including fluctuation theorems.

**Model / system.** The system is score-based diffusion models, which model data generation via a forward diffusion process (data to noise) and a learned reverse sampling process. The analysis treats these processes as stochastic physical systems.

**Key observables.** Time-asymmetry Entropy Production (TAEP), its fluctuations ($	ext{Var}(\Delta s_{	ext{ta}})$), and the ensemble average $\langle \Delta s_{	ext{ta}} angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The proportionality constant relating $\langle \Delta s_{	ext{ta}} angle$ to the score-matching objective, and the noise strength/curvature of the loss landscape.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on interpreting diffusion models as stochastic physical systems and assumes sufficient network expressivity for the reverse process to approximate the true score function.

**Figures summary.** Figures show TAEP distributions verifying fluctuation theorems, tracking data manifold coverage via $	ext{Var}(\Delta s_{	ext{ta}})$ during training, and demonstrating a positive correlation between SGD noise covariance and the loss landscape Hessian.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the stochastic framework (Langevin equations) to deriving TAEP, proving it obeys fluctuation theorems, establishing its proportionality to the score-matching objective, and finally analyzing how fluctuations quantify sampling unevenness and guide optimization towards flatter minima.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Score-based diffusion models are a powerful class of generative AI systems capable of sampling from complex, high-dimensional probability distributions. Their dynamics consist of a forward diffusion process that transforms data into noise and a learned reverse process that reconstructs data by reversing the probability flow. Here, we develop a stochastic thermodynamic framework for diffusion models and their score-matching objective. We introduce a trajectory-dependent quantity, time-asymmetry entropy production (TAEP), defined from the forward and reverse diffusion dynamics, and show that it obeys exact fluctuation theorems. Remarkably, Hyvärinen's implicit score-matching kernel emerges naturally as a fluctuating component of TAEP, while the average TAEP is exactly proportional to the score-matching objective. We further show that fluctuations of TAEP quantify sampling unevenness and provide a thermodynamic measure of data-manifold coverage. These results yield a quantitative explanation for the superior sampling diversity of diffusion models and reveal a thermodynamic mechanism by which stochastic gradient descent favors flatter, more generalizable solutions. By uncovering the entropic nature of score matching, our work establishes fundamental statistical-mechanical principles underlying diffusion-based generative AI.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes quantum LiDAR performance to determine when entanglement offers an advantage over simple squeezing under realistic noise. By modeling correlated jamming noise, the authors show that twin-beam states provide superior estimation precision compared to classical or locally squeezed light. This establishes a receiver-aware protocol for achieving practical quantum enhancement in remote sensing.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, experimentally relevant roadmap for achieving quantum enhancement in remote sensing by identifying the specific noise structure (correlated interference) that unlocks the full potential of multipartite entanglement over local resources.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether entanglement provides a genuine operational advantage over local squeezing for quantum LiDAR when the dominant noise source is structured, correlated interference rather than independent noise.

**Main result.** Correlated noise establishes an operational regime where twin-beam probes outperform both classical and separable squeezed states, provided the receiver adaptively optimizes its measurement basis.

**Method.** The analysis uses a unified Gaussian framework to compare three quantum probe types (coherent, separable squeezed, twin-beam) by calculating joint estimation performance metrics like Fisher Information and Mean Square Error for range and velocity estimation.

**Model / system.** The system is a Quantum LiDAR setup performing joint range ($	au$) and velocity ($\omega$) estimation using bright two-mode Gaussian probes detected via homodyne measurements. The noise model incorporates both standard loss/thermal background and structured correlated jamming noise.

**Key observables.** Joint range–velocity estimation performance (variance products), Mean Square Error (MSE) of parameter estimates, and the ratio quantifying the advantage between different quantum states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fixed total signal energy ($N=50$), correlated noise strength ($\sigma_c$), and the adaptive optimization of receiver measurement settings (LO phases).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the receiver can adaptively optimize its measurement basis to maximize accessible information, moving beyond simple theoretical bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare joint estimation performance vs. correlated noise strength ($\sigma_c$), showing the hierarchy of advantages; other figures detail reconstruction MSE and covariance ellipses for different probe types under varying conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general problem in quantum sensing to modeling specific noise regimes (loss/thermal vs. correlated jamming). It then compares the performance metrics across coherent, separable squeezed, and twin-beam states, culminating in demonstrating the operational advantage of entanglement under structured noise via simulation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum light promises improved precision in optical remote sensing, but its practical advantage depends critically on whether nonclassical resources remain useful under realistic noise and experimentally accessible detection. This question becomes especially relevant for LiDAR systems, where a quantum advantage has been demonstrated for target detection and joint range-velocity estimation, but mostly under idealized conditions or simple noise models, such as optical loss and thermal background. A key open point is whether entanglement provides an operational advantage when the dominant disturbance is not independent noise, but structured interference across sensing modes. Here, we address this question by studying the joint estimation of target range and velocity with bright two-mode Gaussian probes and homodyne detection, comparing coherent, separable squeezed, and twin-beam states at a fixed resource budget. Our results reveal a hierarchy of quantum resources set by the noise structure: separable squeezing provides a robust advantage over coherent illumination under loss and thermal background, whereas twin-beam probes become superior under correlated jamming when the receiver is adaptively optimized. These results establish correlated noise as the operational regime in which entanglement provides a robustness advantage beyond local squeezing, opening a receiver-aware route to quantum-enhanced LiDAR in realistic and potentially adversarial environments.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on using Second-Harmonic Generation (SHG) spectroscopy as a novel, symmetry-sensitive tool to detect the ferroic-octupolar magnetic order parameter in the altermagnet $	ext{CoF}_2$. By analyzing the temperature and polarization dependence of the SHG signal relative to the Néel temperature, the authors confirm that the observed nonlinear response is directly governed by the hidden antiferromagnetic octupole moment. This establishes a powerful optical pathway for studying complex magnetic ground states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of nonlinear optics (SHG) to probe higher-order magnetic multipoles ($	ext{octupole}$) is highly relevant for understanding exotic quantum phases in condensed matter systems that are not captured by simple magnetization measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to develop a direct experimental method to probe the hidden ferroic-octupolar spin order parameter in altermagnets, which lack net magnetization and are invisible to standard magnetometry.

**Main result.** The observation of electric and magnetic dipole forbidden Second-Harmonic Generation (SHG) below the Néel temperature confirms that the signal is proportional to a ferroic-octupole-induced nonlinear polarization, establishing SHG as a novel diagnostic tool for this order.

**Method.** Nonlinear Optical Second-Harmonic Generation (SHG) spectroscopy was employed, analyzing the symmetry dependence of the resulting polarization ($\mathbf{P}_{2\omega}$) relative to temperature and crystal orientation.

**Model / system.** The system is the altermagnet $	ext{CoF}_2$, which exhibits a collinear Antiferromagnetic (AFM) order below $T_N = 38 	ext{ K}$. The theoretical framework models the nonlinear polarization as arising from an electric-quadrupolar term proportional to the magnetic octupole order parameter ($\mathbf{\mathcal{O}}^M$).

**Key observables.** SHG intensity ($I_{2\omega}$), temperature dependence of $I_{2\omega}$, and polarization anisotropy measurements across different incidence angles.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Néel Temperature ($T_N = 38 	ext{ K}$); the ferroic-octupole order parameter ($\mathbf{\mathcal{O}}^M$); photon energy matching three-photon resonance with $	ext{Co}^{2+}$ $d$-$d$ transitions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the total SHG signal is a sum of crystallographic and spin-induced contributions, and that interference between these two components can be neglected for robust measurement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the AFM spin order; compare polarization-dependent SHG signals above ($T>T_N$) versus below ($T<T_N$) $T_N$; and show the temperature evolution of the spin-induced component confirming its magnetic origin.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the theoretical necessity of probing hidden order parameters, detailing the symmetry analysis linking SHG to the octupole moment ($\mathbf{P}_{2\omega} \propto \chi^{(3)}(\mathbf{\mathcal{O}}^M) : \mathbf{E}_\omega 
abla \mathbf{E}_\omega$), presenting experimental measurements showing temperature and polarization dependence, and concluding with the establishment of the technique's utility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnetism as a new concept in condensed matter physics is currently being thoroughly investigated. Despite the absence of macroscopic magnetization, altermagnets host a hidden spin order whose direct detection remains a challenge. Here we report on the observation of electric and magnetic dipole forbidden optical second-harmonic generation (SHG) in the altermagnet CoF$_2$ with a centrosymmetric lattice and spin order. We demonstrate that below the Néel temperature $T_N = 38$ K the SHG signal is sensitive to the ferrotype magnetic octupole $\mathbf{\mathcal{O}}^M$ which is the order parameter in the antiferromagnetic phase. By combining polarization-resolved SHG experimental data and a phenomenological symmetry analysis, we show that the altermagnetic spin structure of CoF$_2$ enables a ferroic-octupole-induced electric-quadrupolar nonlinear polarization $\mathbf{P}^{2ω} = \mathrm i\varepsilon_{0}{ }^c\mathbfχ^{(3)}(\mathbf{\mathcal{O}}^M) :\mathbf{E}^ω \nabla \mathbf{E}^ω$. The temperature dependence of SHG reveals a phase transition at $T_N$ confirming the spin origin of the observed signal. The SHG response is resonantly enhanced by a coherent three-photon process caused by the electronic $d$-$d$ transitions of the Co$^{2+}$ ion. Model calculations of SHG polarization rotational anisotropies and temperature dependencies give a reasonable agreement with experimental data, proving the disclosed nonlinear contribution. Our results establish SHG as a novel sensitive tool of ferroic-octupolar spin ordering and highlight the potential of CoF$_2$ and other altermagnets for further nonlinear optical investigations and applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes thermal properties of a 3D CFT dual to an interacting scalar field in AdS4. Using advanced techniques like thermal inversion formulas, the authors determine the asymptotic behavior of key observables such as spectral densities and OPE coefficients. The main achievement is demonstrating that these derived asymptotic results maintain high quantitative accuracy even when considering intermediate energy regimes and weak interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on AdS/CFT, the rigorous development of thermal effective field theories for one-point functions and the extraction of universal asymptotic data are powerful analytical tools applicable to understanding critical phenomena in many quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates thermal partition functions and one-point functions of a 3D CFT dual to a massive scalar field in AdS4.

**Main result.** The authors derive asymptotic formulas for spectral density and OPE coefficients that remain quantitatively accurate even at intermediate conformal weights, surviving the inclusion of bulk interactions.

**Method.** They employ thermal inversion formulas applied to correlation functions derived from analyzing the partition function using techniques involving conformal block decompositions and high-temperature expansions.

**Model / system.** The system is a Conformal Field Theory (CFT) in 3D, holographically dual to a massive interacting scalar field theory ($\Phi^3, \Phi^4$) propagating in Anti-de Sitter space (AdS$_4$).

**Key observables.** Thermal partition function, one-point functions of primary fields, spectral density $ho(\Delta, \ell)$, and OPE coefficients ($\lambda_{\phi O}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), chemical potential ($\mu$), conformal dimension ($\Delta$), and spin ($\ell$). The analysis focuses on the high-temperature limit ($eta 	o 0$) and large $\Delta$ regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies fundamentally on the AdS/CFT correspondence, utilizing techniques derived from conformal block expansions and asymptotic analysis in complex variables.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare asymptotic formulas for spectral density and OPE coefficients against exact results obtained through various limits (e.g., GFF theory).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically progresses by first analyzing the non-interacting Generalized Free Field (GFF) theory, then incorporating perturbative corrections from cubic and quartic bulk interactions, applying thermal inversion techniques throughout.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the thermal partition and one-point functions of the three-dimensional conformal field theory dual to a massive interacting scalar field in AdS$_4$. Using thermal inversion formulas, we determine the asymptotic behaviour of the spectral density and OPE coefficients involving heavy operators at fixed spin. We first analyse these CFT data for the generalised free field, corresponding to the non-interacting bulk theory. Then we compute the first-order perturbative corrections induced by the cubic and quartic bulk interactions. The thermal observables considered here probe a sector associated with operators of large dimension and, in the bulk description, a regime dominated by states with large particle number. This regime remains comparatively unexplored even in generalised free field theory. Remarkably, the asymptotic formulas obtained from thermal inversion remain quantitatively accurate far from the asymptotic regime, describing CFT data reliably already at intermediate conformal weights. Our results show that this feature survives the inclusion of bulk interactions and provide new analytic control over heavy-state data in conformal field theories.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops the theory of the Quantum Demultiplexer (Q-DEMUX) to quantify the total amount of generalized information (classical plus quantum) that can be perfectly routed from an input state. It establishes fundamental upper bounds on this demultiplexing strength and characterizes the specific types of underlying quantum channels required to achieve these theoretical maximums.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantum information-theoretic framework for understanding how classical and quantum data streams can be simultaneously processed or separated within a single physical device, which has implications for quantum networking protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether generalized information, encompassing both classical and quantum data encoded in a quantum system, can be perfectly routed or 'demultiplexed' at designated output ports using a Quantum Demultiplexer (Q-DEMUX). It seeks to characterize the fundamental limits of this process.

**Main result.** The maximum total demultiplexing strength is bounded by $\log_2 d_{	ext{in}}$, and optimal performance requires the underlying induced quantum channel ($\Lambda_{	ext{ind}}$) to be a specific combination of Random Isometry (RI) and Classical-Quantum (C-Q) channels.

**Method.** The analysis uses information theoretic tools, defining total demultiplexing strength by summing classical mutual information and quantum coherent information. It involves characterizing the Q-DEMUX via its relationship to instrument realizations and deriving upper bounds using capacity arguments.

**Model / system.** The core model is the Quantum Demultiplexer (Q-DEMUX), which acts as a CPTP map taking an input state $ho^A_{in}$ and routing information to separate quantum ($Q$) and classical ($C$) output ports based on a selector $s$. The process is mathematically linked to induced quantum channels ($\Lambda_{	ext{ind}}$) acting on the system.

**Key observables.** Total Demultiplexing Strength ($T_s(D)$), Classical Mutual Information, Quantum Coherent Information, and the channel's capacity bounds ($C$ and $Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The input dimension ($d_{	ext{in}}$) sets the fundamental upper bound on achievable information transfer ($\log_2 d_{	ext{in}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include relating the Q-DEMUX to instrument realizations and using techniques like Stinespring dilations and bounding arguments based on entropy measures.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates a circuit diagram for a pure-perfect Q-DEMUX, involving controlled unitary gates and measurements on an auxiliary system $E_2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the Q-DEMUX concept (Chunk 1), then formalizing the total demultiplexing strength by combining classical and quantum components (Chunk 2). Subsequent sections characterize the necessary structure of the underlying channel ($\Lambda_{	ext{ind}}$) required to achieve maximal strength, culminating in theorems that specify these optimal channel forms (Chunks 3, 4, 5).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Demultiplexers are the fundamental primitives of network architecture, enabling perfect routing of an input classical signal to a designated one, among multiple output ports. Quantum transmission lines, having access to the quantum systems directly, are able to transmit both the classical and quantum information encoded in quantum systems. A natural question therefore emerges that whether the scrambled classical and quantum information in a quantum system can be perfectly demultiplexed in the designated classical and quantum output ports? Here we answer this question by introducing a quantum to quantum-classical device, namely the quantum demultiplexer (Q-DEMUX). We characterize the class of Q-DEMUXs enabling perfect routing of both the classical and the quantum information along with their simple circuit realizations. Our results highlight an explicit connection between the strength of a Q-DEMUX with the incompatibility of quantum instruments. Finally, we extend the notion in a stronger variant where the sender is oblivious regarding the nature of the data to be transmitted through the Q-DEMUX.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that Dirac electrons confined in cylinders inherently possess a helical current texture even at zero orbital angular momentum. When this system interacts locally with a spatially chiral scalar potential, the interaction is highly selective, causing diagonal spin channels to vanish while only off-diagonal, spin-flipping channels survive. This establishes a fundamental, geometry-driven mechanism for spin polarization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a fundamental, purely geometric mechanism for spin-selective interactions in low-dimensional systems, establishing a baseline theory applicable to understanding phenomena like CISS without invoking external magnetic fields or explicit SOC terms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the intrinsic helical conserved-current texture carried by Dirac eigenstates confined cylindrically, and how this structure couples locally to an external chiral scalar potential.

**Main result.** This coupling enforces a geometric selection rule: diagonal spin channels vanish while off-diagonal, handedness-converting channels survive. This mechanism is purely local and relies only on the inherent current texture, not external fields or SOC.

**Method.** The authors derived exact spinor solutions for confined Dirac modes and calculated the resulting conserved current density. They then computed a channel-resolved matrix element kernel describing the interaction with a chiral scalar potential.

**Model / system.** The model is a Dirac electron confined in cylindrical geometry (e.g., a nanotube) described by an effective Hamiltonian incorporating confinement potentials. The analysis focuses on the $l=0$ mode and its associated helical current structure.

**Key observables.** Conserved Dirac four-current density components ($j_\phi, j_z$), local helical pitch $Z(ho)$, and the channel-resolved matrix element kernel $M_{s's}(k', k)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cylinder radius $R$, energy scale $E_k$, chiral potential strength $V_0$, and momentum transfer $q$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The coupling calculation assumes the chiral potential has support only inside the cylinder, and the analysis focuses on the minimal static local-geometric vertex.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the intrinsic helical conserved current texture at a characteristic radius $ho^*$, emphasizing that the helix is defined by the current density itself.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the existence and properties of the helical current in confined Dirac modes. It then derives the matrix-element kernel resulting from coupling this current to a static chiral potential, culminating in the selection rule analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that exact Dirac eigenstates in cylindrical confinement carry a definite helical conserved-current texture even in the zero orbital angular momentum channel l = 0. For the lowest confined mode, the Dirac current contains a nonvanishing azimuthal component together with longitudinal transport and exhibits opposite handedness in the two spin-resolved sectors. The structure also persists into the evanescent region.   We further derive the channel-resolved matrix-element kernel generated by a static chiral scalar potential acting on the confined l = 0 Dirac modes. The resulting spin-selective coupling arises from the Dirac current texture and the scalar chiral potential, and yields a geometric selection rule in which diagonal channels vanish while off-diagonal conversion channels survive. The coupling strength is governed by an internal sampled-current overlap Jchi(k), defined as the integral from 0 to R of f(rho) times jphi_up(rho, k) times rho d rho.   This quantity measures the spatial overlap between the chiral radial profile and the spin-up azimuthal Dirac-current density. The mechanism is fully local and texture-based, without external magnetic fields or spin-orbit coupling.   Within standard Dirac theory, this work identifies the minimal static Dirac-geometric kernel underlying spin-selective response, establishing a baseline structure from which dynamical-medium, scattering, and transport formalisms can be systematically developed toward a complete description of spin-polarization phenomena such as CISS.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates monolayer CrC2N4, demonstrating its potential as a tunable 2D semiconductor. Key findings include strong spin-orbit coupling leading to valley-dependent spin splitting and the ability of mechanical strain to precisely control the band gap and optical response. This makes it highly promising for developing next-generation valleytronic and optoelectronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on solid-state physics, the detailed theoretical modeling of spin-valley coupling and Berry curvature provides a rich platform for understanding topological effects that are relevant to quantum transport phenomena studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the electronic, valley, charge-transfer, and optical properties of monolayer CrC2N4, focusing on how spin-orbit coupling (SOC) and external strain tune its behavior for advanced device applications.

**Main result.** CrC2N4 exhibits strong spin-valley locking due to SOC, and biaxial strain can significantly tune the band gap from 1.987 eV down to 1.421 eV while enhancing Berry curvature for valleytronic use.

**Method.** The research relies heavily on first-principles calculations using DFT (PBE/HSE06) combined with advanced techniques like fully relativistic noncollinear calculations, Bader analysis, and RPA for optical properties.

**Model / system.** The physical system is monolayer CrC2N4. The theoretical framework involves modeling the low-energy states near K/K' valleys using a two-band Dirac-type Hamiltonian to capture spin-valley coupling effects.

**Key observables.** Band gap ($E_g$), SOC-induced spin splittings ($\Delta_v, \Delta_c$), Berry curvature ($\Omega_z$), optical absorption spectrum ($\alpha(\omega)$), and strain dependence of these properties.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Biaxial strain range ($-4\%$ to $+4\%$), Spin splitting values ($51.9 	ext{ meV}$ for VB, $1.7 	ext{ meV}$ for CB).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses DFT/ab initio methods; optical spectra are calculated at the RPA level without explicitly including electron-hole interactions via the Bethe–Salpeter equation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate optimized geometries, band structures under SOC and strain, charge density distributions showing Cr-N bonding, and plots detailing the strain dependence of Berry curvature and optical coefficients ($\alpha, n, k$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the material's intrinsic properties (SOC effects, bonding) first, then details how biaxial strain modulates these electronic and optical responses, culminating in device-relevant predictions for valleytronics and optoelectronics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Monolayer CrC2N4 recently emerged as a promising two-dimensional semiconductor, yet its spin-orbit-coupled (SOC) physics and strain-tunable optical response remained largely unexplored. Here, we investigated the electronic, valley, charge-transfer, and optical properties of pristine and biaxially strained monolayer CrC2N4 using first-principles calculations. The monolayer exhibited a direct band gap at the K/K' valleys. SOC produced valley contrasting out-of-plane spin polarization, yielding a moderate valence band spin splitting of 51.9 meV and a small conduction band spin splitting of 1.7 meV. Orbital-resolved analysis showed that the edge states were mainly governed by Cr-d and N-p hybridization, while Bader analysis indicated polar-covalent bonding through charge transfer toward N atoms. Biaxial strain in the range of -4% to +4% tuned the band gap from 1.987 to 1.421 eV and drove an indirect-to-direct gap transition near -1% strain. Tensile strain enhanced the Berry curvature and red-shifted the optical response toward the visible-near-infrared region. These results suggested monolayer CrC2N4 as a promising platform for strain-engineered valleytronic and optoelectronic device applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically predicts photon anti-bunching in High Harmonic Generation (HHG), providing the first evidence of single-photon temporal correlations in this process. Using advanced quantum field techniques, the authors calculate the intensity correlation function $g^{(2)}(	au)$, demonstrating that the emission violates classical bounds when considering only one emitted photon. This work is crucial for understanding non-classical light sources generated in intense laser fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems because it provides a rigorous theoretical pathway to observe fundamental non-classical light sources using an established, high-intensity physical process like HHG.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to theoretically predict and demonstrate photon anti-bunching—a signature of single-photon emission—within the complex process of High Harmonic Generation (HHG). This marks a first theoretical discovery of non-classical temporal correlations in HHG.

**Main result.** The authors successfully predicted clear evidence of photon anti-bunching ($g^{(2)}(0) < 1$) across the entire harmonic spectrum using quantum field theory methods. They also distinguished this single-photon signature from ensemble statistics like sub-Poissonian distributions.

**Method.** The analysis relies on calculating high-order time correlation functions, specifically $G^{(2)}(	au)$, derived from the Heisenberg picture approach applied to the dipole moment operators within the HHG process.

**Model / system.** The system is High Harmonic Generation (HHG), modeled quantum mechanically using a Hamiltonian incorporating field and interaction terms. The analysis progresses from single-atom calculations to many-emitter scenarios, treating the emission as an upconversion of multiple driving photons into one high-energy harmonic photon.

**Key observables.** Normalized second-order intensity correlation function ($g^{(2)}(	au)$) and the Mandel Q-parameter ($Q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Harmonic order ($q$), time delay ($	au$), and the number of emitters ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations assume an initial vacuum state for the field and utilize approximations that allow separating correlations into coherent and incoherent parts, especially in the many-emitter limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $g^{(2)}(	au)$ violating classical bounds (showing anti-bunching) for various harmonics and show how this correlation function approaches unity as the number of emitters ($N$) increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from establishing the theoretical framework using quantum field operators in the Heisenberg picture, calculating $g^{(2)}(	au)$ for single atoms to confirm anti-bunching, and finally extending the analysis to many emitters while distinguishing between temporal correlations (anti-bunching) and number statistics (sub-Poissonian).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photon anti-bunching is the direct evidence for the existence of photons without having a classical counterpart. Unlike bunching of photons, which can have a semi-classical description, the effect of photon anti-bunching can only be understood with quantized electromagnetic fields. However, for the process of high harmonic generation (HHG), where many photons of the driving field are upconverted to a single photon of higher energy, there is yet no clear evidence for the presence of individual photon emission. The key result of this work is the prediction of photon anti-bunching in the process of HHG, marking it the first theoretical discovery of non-classicality in the temporal correlations of HHG photons. While other non-classical signatures in HHG, such as sub-Poissonian statistics or squeezing, have been discussed for an ensemble of photons, the anti-bunching signature reported here is a signature of a single photon. This is achieved by using the recently developed Heisenberg picture approach for quantum optical HHG, revealing clear anti-bunching signatures in the intensity correlation function across the entire harmonic spectrum.

</details>

### [Hydrogen s-electrons as the origin of crystal magnetism beyond spin-orbit coupling](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17902v1)

**Authors:** Baiqiang Liu, Zhen Gong, Rui Liu, Siyang Liu, Yue Feng, Zhigang Wang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17902v1>

**Summary.** This work reports on the discovery of intrinsic ferromagnetism in $	ext{H}_{13}@(	ext{BN})_{12}$, demonstrating that magnetism can arise from hydrogen's $1s$ s-electrons without relying on spin-orbit coupling. Using first-principles calculations, they show this magnetism is stabilized by the cage structure and can be tuned—specifically quenched under high compression—providing a blueprint for low-energy spintronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete theoretical framework for engineering magnetic materials where conventional $d$ or $f$ electron physics is not required, opening avenues for novel spintronic devices relevant to condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the challenge of achieving robust magnetism derived from s-electrons, such as those in hydrogen, which are typically expected to have quenched spins.

**Main result.** A ferromagnetic crystal $	ext{H}_{13}@(	ext{BN})_{12}$ was shown to exhibit intrinsic magnetism originating solely from the central hydrogen $1s$ electron, establishing a pathway for low-SOC magnetic materials.

**Method.** The research relies heavily on first-principles Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations, supplemented by advanced analyses like Electron Localization Function (ELF), Crystal Orbital Hamilton Population (COHP), and phonon dispersion calculations.

**Model / system.** The model system is the low-Z ferromagnetic crystal $	ext{H}_{13}@(	ext{BN})_{12}$, where thirteen hydrogen atoms are encapsulated within a $(	ext{BN})_{12}$ cage. The magnetism is localized to the central H atom's $1s$ orbital.

**Key observables.** Magnetic moment ($\mu_B$), spin-resolved band structure, Electron Localization Function (ELF) maps, and magnetic energy comparison under stress.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ambient pressure vs. 16 GPa compression; the critical role of the negative electrostatic potential within the cage.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The stability mechanism is governed by the localization of the central hydrogen $1s$ electron, and SOC effects are negligible due to the s-electron nature.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate structural properties, phonon dispersion confirming stability, band structures showing spin splitting, and ELF maps demonstrating electron localization changes under applied stress.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically establishes the magnetic origin from H $s$-electrons, confirms the ground state magnetism using multiple computational analyses (COHP, ELF), demonstrates the tunability of this magnetism via external pressure/stress, and concludes by outlining its potential for low-energy spintronics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetism has long been attributed to localized d, f, and even p electrons with strong correlations, whereas s electrons exemplified by hydrogen are reactive and tend to have their spins quenched, making s-electron-derived magnetism and long-range ordered magnetic crystals seem unattainable. Here we report a low-Z ferromagnetic crystal H13@(BN)12 using first-principles calculations, where thirteen hydrogen atoms are encapsulated within a (BN)12 cage and magnetism originates from the 1s electron of the central hydrogen atom. The crystal remains stable under ambient pressure owing to chemical precompression. Notably, the central hydrogen atom retains a magnetic moment of 1 μB, with long-range magnetic order established through multicenter bonding within the H13 aggregate and the intercell B-B network, while the zero orbital angular momentum of s electrons renders spin-orbit coupling (SOC) negligible as expected. Electronic structure analyses reveal that the large cavity and central negative electrostatic potential of the (BN)12 cage localize the hydrogen 1s electron, preventing spin quenching. Interestingly, under 16 GPa compression, the system transforms into a nonmagnetic metallic state driven by delocalized electrons of the central hydrogen atom. This study opens a pathway for constructing s-electron-driven magnetic materials and lays the foundation for developing low-energy consumption magnetic devices without SOC.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a quantum critical Eliashberg theory for superconductivity in 2D ferroelectric quantum materials. It shows that an interband 'Stark'-like coupling generates an effective two-phonon interaction that significantly enhances the superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$) beyond standard BCS predictions. The results are highly relevant to understanding unconventional pairing mechanisms in layered oxides and TMDs.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on strong electron-phonon coupling mediated by quantum critical fluctuations provides a rich theoretical framework relevant to understanding unconventional superconductivity in layered materials, which often involves complex electronic correlations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates superconductivity arising from ferroelectric quantum critical fluctuations in two dimensions, addressing the puzzle of enhanced SC when conventional electron-phonon coupling is suppressed.

**Main result.** The superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$) is strongly enhanced relative to BCS expectations due to an effective two-phonon pairing mechanism mediated by interband coupling near a Quantum Critical Point (QCP).

**Method.** The authors construct and solve the quantum critical Eliashberg theory for this 2D system, analyzing different limits of the interband gap.

**Model / system.** The model uses a minimal two-band Hamiltonian describing electrons coupled to a 2D TO phonon mode in a Quantum Ferroelectric Metal (QFEM) near its QCP. The coupling mechanism is an interband 'Stark'-like interaction leading to effective quadratic (two-phonon) pairing.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), pairing kernel, and the gap equation solution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interband gap magnitude ($\delta$), coupling constant ($\lambda$), dimensionality (2D vs 3D), and frequency cutoffs ($\Lambda\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations in the large-gap and small-gap limits, treating the system near criticality, and assuming parabolic bands.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate diagrammatic representations of the two-phonon process and plot $T_c$ versus coupling strength ($\lambda$) or inverse coupling strength ($1/\lambda$), comparing results to BCS scaling.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by constructing the Eliashberg equations, analyzing the pairing vertex in both large-gap and small-gap regimes, and deriving explicit forms for $T_c$ that show parametric enhancement over standard BCS predictions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Soft critical fluctuations associated with ferroelectric quantum phase transitions are typically transverse owing to their polar nature. This implies that the conventional density--density electron--phonon coupling to these modes is strongly suppressed, which is puzzling as a variety of materials exhibit enhanced superconductivity in the vicinity of ferroelectricity. An alternative coupling mechanism is an interband ``Stark''-like coupling that connects bands of opposite parity. In the limit where one of the bands is far in energy, these processes generate an effective quadratic (two-phonon) coupling. In contrast, when both bands lie close to the Fermi energy, the resulting interaction develops singular behavior due to the additional gapless electronic states, motivating a detailed study into the dynamics of this effective two-phonon coupling. To this end, we construct the quantum critical Eliashberg theory for a two-dimensional system across a wide range of interband gap magnitudes, near the quantum critical point. We find that the critical temperature $T_c$ is strongly enhanced relative to conventional BCS expectations. In the large-gap limit, the pairing kernel acquires higher-order logarithmic contributions, leading to a parametrically enhanced $T_c$ governed by cubic and quadratic logarithmic terms. In the small-gap regime, the pairing scale exhibits a modified BCS-like form with an enhanced dependence on the inverse square root of the dimensionless coupling constant. The enhancement is due to the dynamics of the two-phonon pairing whose infrared cutoff is set by $T_c$, resulting in a significant enhancement of superconductivity compared to three-dimensional systems, where it is set by the Fermi energy. Our results elucidate the unique dynamical properties of effective two-phonon interactions, and may be relevant to layered compounds like Td-MoTe$_2$ and doped SrTiO$_3$ membranes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies the strong-coupling diagram technique to analyze the phase diagram of the 3D repulsive Hubbard model. It determines critical boundaries between paramagnetic and antiferromagnetic phases while investigating how the density of states evolves across different interaction strengths ($U$). The results highlight complex doping effects, such as plateaus in $T_N$ linked to spectral reconstruction.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides detailed theoretical insights into fundamental many-body physics in strongly correlated electron systems, particularly concerning Mott transitions and magnetic ordering in 3D lattices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the phase diagram of the three-dimensional repulsive Hubbard model, focusing on the transition between paramagnetic and antiferromagnetic states.

**Main result.** The analysis reveals a Mott transition in the density of states around $U\approx9t$ and shows that doping dependence of the Néel temperature exhibits plateaus linked to changes in electron coupling strength.

**Method.** The strong-coupling diagram technique (SCDT) is employed, involving series expansion in kinetic energy and solving coupled equations for spin and charge susceptibilities.

**Model / system.** The system is the 3D repulsive Hubbard model on a simple cubic lattice, described by the Hamiltonian $	ext{H} = \sum_{\langle l l' angle\sigma} t_{ll'} (a^\dagger_{l'\sigma} a_{l\sigma} + h.c.) + U \sum_l n_{l\uparrow} n_{l\downarrow}$.

**Key observables.** Néel temperature ($T_N$), Density of States (DOS), magnetic critical exponent ($\gamma$), and spin/charge susceptibilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $U$ (Hubbard repulsion) relative to $t$ (hopping integral); electron concentration $ar{n}$; regimes like $4t \le U \le 12t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary method relies on the strong-coupling diagram technique, which is most accurate for the limit where $U \gg t$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the variation of $T_N$ with doping ($ar{n}$) showing plateaus (e.g., Fig. 6), and demonstrate how the DOS changes from weak-coupling quasiparticle features to strong-coupling Mott gaps depending on $ar{n}$ and $U$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes phase boundaries ($T_N(U)$) using SCDT, calculates susceptibilities via determinant analysis ($\Delta(k, 
u)$), and examines how the DOS and critical exponents change as a function of both interaction strength ($U$) and doping ($ar{n}$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The three-dimensional repulsive Hubbard model is investigated using the strong-coupling diagram technique. For half-filling, the boundary between paramagnetic and antiferromagnetic states is determined for the range of the Hubbard repulsion $4t\leq U\leq12t$, where $t$ is the hopping integral between neighboring sites. Along this boundary, the density of states is calculated, and it demonstrates the Mott transition at $U\approx9t$. For $U\geq6t$ and half-filling, the density of states has the shape inherent in the strong electron repulsion, while for $U=4t$, its shape points to weak coupling. The dependence of the Néel temperature $T_{\rm N}$ on the electron concentration $\bar{n}$ is investigated for the cases $U=4t$ and $12t$. In the former case, $T_{\rm N}$ decreases monotonously with $\bar{n}$, while in the latter case, there is a plateau in the dependence near $\bar{n}=0.87$. The plateau is connected to a reconstruction of the density of states caused by an effective weakening of electron coupling due to electron depopulation. For $U=12t$ and half-filling, the magnetic critical exponent $γ\approx1.4$, which is close to the value in the Heisenberg model. Some features resembling the first-order phase transition, revealing themselves in a finite crystal, are discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the role of Crystal Electric Field splitting in understanding Quantum Critical Points within Yb-based compounds. By analyzing low-temperature specific heat measurements across doping series, the authors refine existing phase diagrams. They conclude that the QCP is better understood as a crossover dominated by quantum fluctuations rather than a singular point defined by diverging thermodynamic quantities.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides detailed experimental evidence on how crystal field effects modify the expected critical behavior near magnetic quantum criticality in heavy fermion systems, offering insights into the nature of quantum fluctuations beyond simple mean-field models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study explores how Crystal Electric Field (CEF) splitting modifies the understanding of Quantum Critical Points (QCPs) when a system transitions between magnetic and Fermi-liquid ground states.

**Main result.** The analysis suggests that the QCP region is better described as a crossover dominated by low-lying quantum fluctuations, rather than a singularity characterized by divergences in specific heat measurements.

**Method.** The research analyzes very low temperature thermodynamic properties, particularly the specific heat divided by temperature (C4f/T), across various doping levels within the YbT4M family of compounds.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is the YbT4M family of intermetallic compounds. The theoretical framework improves upon the Doniach-Lavagna phase diagram by explicitly incorporating CEF splittings to model the competition between magnetic ordering, Kondo screening, and valence fluctuations.

**Key observables.** Specific heat divided by temperature (C4f/T), which exhibits power law dependencies, logarithmic divergences ($\propto \ln(T/T_0)$), or near-constant behavior depending on the electronic regime.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters include the Kondo temperature ($T_K$), the magnetic ordering temperature ($T_m$), and the CEF splitting energy ($\Delta_{	ext{CEF}}$). The doping level acts as a control parameter mapping out different regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Early models' predictions of divergences at the QCP are revised; instead, the transition is characterized by a clear drop in $C_{4f}/T$ towards zero temperature.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams (e.g., magnetic vs. non-magnetic regions) and plot C4f/T versus temperature for various doped alloys to map out distinct physical regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the low-temperature behavior across three identified regimes—magnetic, non-Fermi liquid, and valence fluctuation—using doping as a tuning parameter to construct a more realistic phase diagram around the QCP.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Very low temperature thermdynamic properties of the Yb$T_4M$ family of compounds are analyzed in a broad range of behavior including a quantum critical point QCP between magnetic and Fermi-liquid ground states GS. Doniach-Lavagna phase diagram limitations are improved by taking into account crystal electric field CEF splittings. The studied alloys: Yb$T_{5-x}M_x$ (with $T$= Ni, Cu, and $M$= Cd, Mg, Pd, Au, Zn, Ag), allow to gain insight into the evolution of the GS behavior undergoing the QCP region as a function of chemical doping as control parameter. Three types of behaviors are recognized in this system: i) a magnetic one, with weak interactions at $T_m\leq 1$\,K and very low Kondo temperature of their respective doublets GS. Between $T_m$ and $T_q\approx 0.3$\,K, quantum fluctuations start to dominate the scenario with the specific heat $C_{4f}/T(T\geq T_q)$ showing $T$ power law dependencies and a very heavy-fermion {\it plateau} below $T_q$. ii) beyond the QCP the typical non-fermi-liquid logarithmic $T$ dependence: $C_{4f}/T \propto \ln(T/T_0)$ and iii) at the non-magnetic limit, the alloys behave as valence-fluctuation systems with $T_K$ overcoming the CEF splitting. With this experimental information, a more realistic phase diagram can be drawn around the QCP where the scenario is dominated by low lying quantum fluctuations, without $C_{4f}/T|_{Lim T\to 0}$ divergences but a clear drop of its value.

</details>

### [The Helical SYK Model and Emergent Infrared Integrability](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17161v1)

**Authors:** Gustavo Valdivia-Mera, Bhavay Tyagi, Eric R. Bittner, Pavan Hosur  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17161v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

</figure>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a helical generalization of the SYK model, exploring how its random quartic interactions behave under renormalization group flow. While generic couplings destroy initial integrability, the disorder-averaged large-$N$ limit forces the system back to an exactly solvable, free, and integrable state in the infrared regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between random interactions (disorder) and emergent exact solvability in the deep infrared limit is a powerful concept relevant to understanding thermalization and quantum chaos in many-body systems, which has parallels in AMO physics when studying complex interacting regimes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates the emergence of infrared (IR) integrability in a generalized, random-coupled quantum chaotic model.

**Main result.** In the disorder-averaged large-$N$ IR limit, the theory re-emerges as integrable because all quartic interactions flow to zero due to conformal perturbation theory analysis. The system is exactly solvable when restricted by symmetries or in this deep IR limit.

**Method.** The study employs bosonization, Conformal Perturbation Theory (CPT), and Renormalization Group (RG) flow analysis on a generalized Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model.

**Model / system.** The system is a $1+1$ dimensional helical generalization of the SYK model built from left- and right-moving Majorana fermions with random quartic couplings in flavor-chirality space. The Hamiltonian structure is analyzed across various symmetry constraints, ranging from maximally symmetric to fully general.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the RG flow coefficients (beta functions) determining coupling strength evolution, and the resulting physical energy density ($\epsilon_{	ext{phys}}$) in solvable limits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the system size $N$, the disorder strengths $J_S$, and the dimensionality ($1+1$ dimensions). The analysis focuses on the large-$N$ limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes Gaussian random couplings for disorder averaging, relies on separating symmetry-constrained sectors from the full interaction space, and uses CPT around a free fixed point.

**Figures summary.** Figures likely summarize the hierarchy of symmetries constraining interactions (Table 2) and illustrate the flow equations or diagonalization results for normal modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the highly symmetric, exactly integrable sector via bosonization. It then analyzes how relaxing symmetries expands the interaction space. The core is the RG analysis showing that disorder averaging forces the system back to an IR fixed point where integrability is recovered through marginal irrelevance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a helical generalization of the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model in $1+1$ dimensions, built from left- and right-moving Majorana fermions with local quartic interactions and random couplings in flavor-chirality space. These interactions organize into a symmetry-controlled hierarchy of quartic chirality sectors. At the most restrictive end of this hierarchy, symmetry forces the quartic structure into a density-density form, which admits an exact solution using bosonization, rendering the theory integrable. Once the full quartic helical interaction space is allowed, including purely chiral, chirality-balanced, and chirality-imbalanced sectors, this symmetry-protected integrable structure is lost. Nevertheless, the large-$N$ infrared limit remains analytically tractable through short-distance selection rules and disorder averaging. Using conformal perturbation theory about the free fixed point, we show that the entire interaction space is marginally irrelevant, and the theory thus becomes free and integrable in the IR.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Closest Accessible Symmetry (CAS) reduction method to analyze how the energy spectrum of many-body quantum systems changes during Hamiltonian interpolation. By systematically projecting the system onto symmetry-constrained subspaces, it avoids numerical discretization while accurately predicting critical points and characterizing phase transitions via pseudo-spectra and hybridization analysis.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical tool for analyzing quantum dynamics across phase transitions, offering insights into spectral reorganization that are complementary to standard time-stepping or local approximation methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a systematic framework for analyzing the energy spectrum of Hamiltonian interpolations between two Hamiltonians without relying on discretizing the interpolation parameter.

**Main result.** The Closest Accessible Symmetry (CAS) reduction provides a hierarchical, non-discretized method to approximate the pseudo-spectrum and locate quantum phase transitions by tracking spectral reorganization across the entire path.

**Method.** The core technique involves projecting the interpolating Hamiltonian onto subspaces defined by accessible symmetries, yielding weakly coupled pseudo-eigenspaces whose coupling strengths are analyzed using hybridization criteria.

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to general many-body quantum systems undergoing adiabatic evolution, exemplified by the frustrated Ising ring model and linear interpolations between Hamiltonians A and B.

**Key observables.** Quantum phase transition locations (gap closing), pseudo-spectrum $\{\mu_k\}$, hybridization strength $\chi_{kj}$, and spectral gap bounds ($\Delta$ vs. $	ilde{\Delta}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interpolation parameter $s \in [0, 1]$, the structure of accessible symmetries (e.g., single-Pauli string symmetries), and the cost function used to select the optimal symmetry reduction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quality of the approximation depends on the compatibility between the chosen accessible symmetry family and the physical problem instance; the method relies on restricting the search space to tractable symmetry generators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how different types of symmetries ($\mathbb{Z}_2$) can be generated by removing terms from $H(s)$, show fidelity comparisons between pseudo-eigenstates and true eigenstates, and plot spectral gap behavior across phase transitions for the Ising model.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the CAS framework, details its application to linear interpolations using symmetry constraints (Types I-IV), develops diagnostic tools like hybridization criteria ($\chi_{kj}$), and compares different optimization strategies (max-cost vs. average cost) on benchmark models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a framework for analysing the spectrum of Hamiltonian interpolations without heavily relying on discretising the interpolation parameter. The method is based on the concept of accessible symmetries: a problem-class-dependent family of certifiable reflections that induce bipartitions of the Hilbert space. At each step, the interpolation Hamiltonian is projected onto the sectors of the accessible symmetry that is closest to being satisfied, yielding a hierarchy of weakly coupled pseudo-eigenspaces together with explicit residual couplings between them. We show that this representation captures qualitative signatures of quantum phase transitions, provides estimates of their location, and offers insights into their nature. The quality of the approximation is controlled by the compatibility between the accessible symmetry family and the problem instance. Although motivated in spirit by adiabatic quantum computation, our approach applies more broadly to the study of Hamiltonian phase diagrams, providing a new perspective on the spectral reorganisation of many-body quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies the concept of Hyperuniformity (HU) to analyze charge distributions in the generalized Aubry-André model on aperiodic lattices. It demonstrates that while all distributions are hyperuniform, their specific HU class acts as a fingerprint revealing whether the system is gapped or gapless and how single-particle states localize near the Fermi energy. This framework successfully identifies third-order phase transitions between different insulating many-body states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of Hyperuniformity as a universal diagnostic tool to classify quantum phase transitions—especially distinguishing between spectral and mobility gaps—provides a powerful, non-traditional metric for condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To characterize phase transitions in inhomogeneous electron distributions within aperiodic systems, moving beyond classification based solely on symmetry or topology.

**Main result.** The many-body charge distribution is always hyperuniform, but its specific class (I, II, or III) changes across different phases. This change signals a third-order phase transition linked to the localization properties of single-particle states near the Fermi energy.

**Method.** Analysis relies on quantifying global uniformity using Hyperuniformity (HU), derived from scaling behaviors of charge density correlation functions ($ar{B}(R)$) and structure factors ($S(k)$).

**Model / system.** The study uses the Generalized Aubry-André (GAA) model, a tight-binding Hamiltonian applied to an aperiodic lattice. This model is chosen because it supports both localized and extended single-particle states via a mobility edge.

**Key observables.** Hyperuniformity class (Class I vs. Class II), charge density ($\mathbf{n_i}$), Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR), and fractal dimension ($D_f$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fraction ($
u$), potential strength ($\lambda$), and the location of the Fermi energy ($E_F$) relative to mobility edges.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the HU class provides a robust diagnostic tool for distinguishing between spectral gap and mobility gap origins of insulation. The framework is noted to neglect full quantum fluctuations in certain regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of $ar{B}(R)$ on localization scenarios, showing distinct Class-I (extended) vs. Class-II (localized) behavior; they also map out $D_f$ landscapes and confirm third-order transitions via energy derivative analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the GAA model and HU diagnostics, first establishing that charge distributions are always hyperuniform. It then contrasts gapped phases (always Class-I) with gapless phases, where the class depends critically on whether the Fermi level intersects localized or extended single-particle states, culminating in a third-order transition diagnosis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show the existence of distinct inhomogeneous charge distributions and a phase transition between them in aperiodic fermion systems. Using a generalized Aubry-André model as an example, we obtain various types of charge distributions, which we classify by means of hyperuniformity, a general mean to quantify and classify the global uniformity of spatial distributions. Examining various cases of filling fraction and potential strength and shape, we find that the many-body charge distribution in this model is always hyperuniform, i.e., showing an anomalously suppressed long-range fluctuation, while its hyperuniformity class depends on the localization properties of the states around the Fermi energy, irrespective of those of high-energy states. Namely, the change in the localization properties of these single-particle states leaves a signature in the hyperuniformity class of many-body charge distribution. We further show a change of the hyperuniformity class corresponds to a phase transition between inhomogeneous many-body states, and that it is of the third order.

</details>
