**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the optimal information-disturbance tradeoff for estimating direction using two coupled quantum spins. By comparing antiparallel versus parallel spin encodings, the authors prove that the antiparallel configuration yields superior performance in both maximizing directional information and minimizing measurement backaction (disturbance). The results are generalized to arbitrary spin $j$ and analyzed asymptotically.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous quantitative bounds on fundamental resource trade-offs (information vs. disturbance) in quantum metrology, offering concrete advantages of specific physical encoding schemes (antiparallel spins) over simpler ones.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the optimal trade-off between extracting directional information and disturbing the quantum state when estimating an unknown spatial direction encoded in two antiparallel spins.

**Main result.** For antiparallel encoding, the system consistently achieves both higher directional information and lower output disturbance compared to parallel spin encodings across all analyzed spin values ($j$).

**Method.** The problem is formulated using covariant quantum instruments and solved via semidefinite programming (SDP) techniques applied to a Choi seed operator. The analysis extends from finite-dimensional matrix calculations to asymptotic limits for large spins.

**Model / system.** The system involves two physical spin-$j$ particles whose state encodes directional information, analyzed by comparing antiparallel and parallel spin-coherent pairs. The mathematical framework utilizes group averaging over covariant orbits on the sphere.

**Key observables.** Directional Information ($I$), Full-state Operation Fidelity ($F$), and Disturbance ($D = 1 - F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spin quantum number $j$, which is generalized from spin-1/2 to arbitrary $j$. The large-$j$ limit provides asymptotic bounds for the tradeoff.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on rotational invariance, restricting the optimal measurement strategy to covariant instruments. For endpoint calculations, specific POVMs are fixed, simplifying the optimization compared to the full continuous problem.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the optimal I-D tradeoffs for antiparallel vs. parallel encodings as a function of spin $j$, showing quantitative advantages for the antiparallel case in both information gain and disturbance reduction.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from foundational concepts (covariant instruments, SDP formulation) to specific calculations for spin-1/2, then generalizes these results to arbitrary spin $j$. The structure culminates in deriving asymptotic limits using advanced mathematical techniques like Bessel functions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We determine the optimal information--disturbance tradeoff for estimating an unknown spatial direction encoded in two antiparallel spins. Rotational covariance reduces the optimization over all instruments to a finite-dimensional Choi problem: a positive seed operator obeys one trace constraint for each irreducible sector of the input representation, while both the directional score and the operation fidelity are linear functionals of this seed. For two antiparallel spin-$1/2$ particles, whose physical representation decomposes as $0\oplus1$, we derive the two-multiplier dual problem and characterize the optimal instrument from the kernel vectors of the dual slack operator. The optimal operation is a covariant filter with scalar--vector coherence and is generally not a convex interpolation between the identity channel and a measure-and-reprepare strategy. At maximum information we recover the Gisin--Popescu score, but the least disturbing output state is optimized independently, giving a smaller disturbance than both the parallel-spin benchmark and antiparallel measure-and-reprepare. We also formulate the parallel benchmark and, as a central extension of the method, treat antiparallel spin-coherent states of arbitrary spin $j$. In this case the signal coherently occupies all sectors $\ell=0,\ldots,2j$ of $j\otimes j$, the endpoint information is governed by nearest-neighbor sector coherences, and the endpoint disturbance is obtained from an explicit finite block-diagonal eigenvalue problem.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive simulation framework to analyze fault-tolerant distributed quantum computing across modular QPUs. It jointly models error correction overheads with realistic network constraints, such as limited entanglement generation rates and bandwidth contention. The key finding is that system design requires careful tradeoff analysis, as communication bottlenecks can impose performance penalties that outweigh the benefits of higher code distances.

**Why it may be interesting.** For AMO physicists working on quantum networking or trapped ions, this work provides quantitative metrics showing how physical interconnect limitations (like photon loss rates) translate directly into fundamental limits on achievable logical qubit fidelity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work addresses how network constraints fundamentally impact the performance and feasibility of fault-tolerant quantum computation when scaling to modular, distributed architectures.

**Main result.** The study reveals critical tradeoffs where communication overheads (like entanglement generation latency) can negate the error suppression gains achieved by increasing code distance in distributed systems.

**Method.** It employs an end-to-end simulation framework that jointly models surface-code operations, internal QPU connectivity, and realistic network limitations like finite entanglement rates and bandwidth contention.

**Model / system.** The system is a Quantum Data Center (QDC) composed of multiple interconnected QPUs utilizing surface codes for fault tolerance. The model incorporates lattice surgery protocols to realize logical gates across the network.

**Key observables.** Execution latency, estimated logical error rates (TLER), and circuit makespan ($t_{total}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), Entanglement-to-Photon Rate (EPR rate), Syndrome extraction time ($T_{syn}$), Idle noise multiplier ($k$), and Seam noise factor ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes uniform entanglement generation rates across links and simplifies the complex stochastic nature of error events into mean-field approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate architectural comparisons (monolithic vs. distributed), lattice surgery protocols for local/remote CNOTs, network topologies like Fat-trees, and plots showing TLER scaling versus physical error rate under varying noise regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a systematic framework by first compiling logical circuits to lattice-surgery primitives, then scheduling these operations considering resource contention (ancilla assignment), and finally calculating the total time/error based on network timing models (e.g., slow vs. fast EPR regimes).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

As we move towards scalable and modular quantum computing, quantum data centres become imperative. Existing analyses typically treat network constraints in isolation or through simplified models, leaving the interplay between error correction operations and communication resources underexplored. In this work, we present an end-to-end simulation framework that jointly models surface-code operations, internal QPU connectivity, and realistic network constraints including finite entanglement generation rates, limited communication qubits, and bandwidth contention, producing execution latency, from which logical error rate estimates are obtained. The framework is modular by design, allowing individual components such as routing heuristics, scheduling policies, and network topologies to be independently replaced. Numerical evaluation reveals distinct operating regimes in which the optimal resource allocation and code distance selection shift depending on the network characteristics. These results point to tradeoffs in the design of distributed quantum computing architectures that are not visible when computation and communication are modeled separately.

</details>

### [Operator ordering as an emergent gauge field in twisted bilayer graphene: singular spectral signatures at the magic angle](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17349v1)

**Authors:** C. A. S. Almeida  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17349v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `Kondo & dissipative impurity` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2606.17349_figures/2606.17349_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. Spectral signatures of operator ordering in TBG near the magic angle. (a) Adiabatic evolution of ε0 = E0/E∗for j = ± 1</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This theoretical work predicts that the spectral features of magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene are governed by an operator ordering correction term. This correction acts like an emergent Aharonov–Bohm flux at stacking boundaries, lifting degeneracy and causing a measurable splitting ($\approx 171$ meV) in the electronic density of states. The finding provides a unique, geometrically derived signature for experimental verification using STM spectroscopy.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism relies on subtle mathematical requirements (self-adjointness, current conservation) applied to a condensed matter system, linking geometric topology (emergent flux) directly to observable electronic structure in strongly correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the spectral signatures at the AB/BA stacking points of magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene (TBG) that are absent from standard continuum models.

**Main result.** The required Hermitian ordering correction to the Dirac Hamiltonian generates an emergent Aharonov–Bohm flux, splitting the zero-energy degeneracy into two asymmetric Van Hove peaks separated by $\Delta_{	ext{split}} \approx 171$ meV.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving the necessary self-adjoint extension of the continuum Dirac operator and solving the resulting radial equations near mass zeros using adiabatic interpolation techniques.

**Model / system.** The system is magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene (TBG) modeled by a continuum Dirac Hamiltonian. The key theoretical development is incorporating an ordering term, $H_{	ext{ord}}$, which restores functional-analytic Hermiticity due to spatially varying mass profiles.

**Key observables.** Two asymmetric Van Hove peaks separated by $\Delta_{	ext{split}} \approx 171$ meV; the scaling of this splitting as $\sqrt{	heta}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magic angle ($	heta$), effective mass $m_{	ext{eff}}(\mathbf{r})$, and the emergent flux quantum $h/(2e)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that spectral structure is governed by the self-adjoint extension of the Dirac operator, requiring adherence to current conservation and square-integrability of zero modes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate adiabatic evolution showing gap splitting; LDOS maps show two distinct peaks at AB/BA points; and a plot confirms $\Delta_{	ext{split}}$ scales with $\sqrt{	heta}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the failure of naive Hamiltonians to conserve current, deriving the necessary ordering correction ($H_{	ext{ord}}$), mathematically proving its physical necessity via self-adjoint extensions, and finally predicting measurable spectral signatures ($\Delta_{	ext{split}}$) at specific stacking points.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scanning-tunnelling spectroscopy at the AB and BA stacking points of magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene should reveal two asymmetric Van~Hove peaks separated by $Δ_{\mathrm{split}}\approx 171$~meV -- a splitting absent from the standard Bistritzer--MacDonald spectrum. We show this signature arises naturally from the Hermitian ordering correction of the Dirac Hamiltonian with spatially varying mass, which generates an emergent Aharonov--Bohm flux of $h/(2e)$ at each zero of the effective mass $m_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{r})=w|f(\mathbf{r})|$. In the chiral limit, the interlayer coupling is locally diagonalised by a spatially dependent unitary transformation; the ordering term $H_{\mathrm{ord}}=-\tfrac{i}{2}\boldsymbolσ\cdot \nabla\ln m_{\mathrm{eff}}$ then develops a $1/r$ singularity at the AB/BA stacking points, where $m_{\mathrm{eff}}$ vanishes. The splitting scales as $\sqrtθ$ -- distinguishing it from correlation-driven gaps ($\proptoθ$ or $\propto 1/θ$) -- is gate-voltage independent, and is spatially localised within $r_c\approx 2.1$~nm of each AB/BA point. Within the local asymptotic theory near the zeros of $\mathrm{eff}$, the ordering-corrected zero mode acquires parabolic-cylinder character $D_{-1/2}$. The spatially resolved AB/BA spectrum reported in recent STM studies of magic-angle TBG has not been analysed for two-peak structure and constitutes an immediate experimental test; the predicted cell-averaged broadening of $\approx 14$~meV is consistent with the $16$~meV discrepancy between existing STM data and the BM tight-binding prediction.

</details>

The depicted yellow range is the end-to-end fidelity thresholds set with panel (a) F th ete ∈[0.90, 0.94]; panel (b) F th ete ∈[0.70, 0.94]; panel (c) F th ete ∈[0.50, 0.94]. REF shows a similar pattern across different ranges since it does not account for end-to-end fidelity thresholds, while other approaches all deliver, considering the end-to-end fidelity thresholds.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper develops optimal scheduling protocols to manage the trade-off between link quality and availability in quantum networks. By modeling fidelity decay over time, it derives analytical solutions for simple chains and proposes advanced heuristics for complex topologies. The work is crucial for designing robust, high-throughput quantum communication infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical framework for resource allocation in realistic quantum communication channels, directly addressing the operational limitations of current quantum network designs by integrating maintenance downtime into the core optimization loop.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing an optimal scheduling protocol for quantum network links that balances link quality (fidelity, which decays over time) against availability (which is reduced by necessary calibration downtime). This must be done to maximize overall end-to-end throughput.

**Main result.** For linear chains, the paper derives analytical protocols (QLO) showing optimal scheduling based on fidelity thresholds. For general networks, it proposes heuristics (MIN/GRO) due to non-convex optimization challenges.

**Method.** The work employs network optimization theory, transforming time-dependent fidelity decay into log-fidelity space ($L$-space). It uses analytical derivations for linear chains and heuristic/numerical approaches for complex topologies.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum network composed of links (modeled as optical fibers) connecting quantum repeaters. Entanglement distribution requires periodic recalibration to counteract environmental drift, creating an operational trade-off.

**Key observables.** End-to-end fidelity ($F_{ete}$), effective throughput ($T$), and the required activation/calibration time periods ($a_e$ vs $c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Individual link fidelity thresholds ($L_{th, e}$) and the global end-to-end fidelity constraint ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes exponential decay of fidelity with activation time. For general networks, the objective function becomes non-convex, necessitating heuristic approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate fidelity decay curves (showing single/stretched exponentials) and allowed regions in fidelity space ($	ext{F}_{m0}$ vs $	ext{F}_{m1}$) that define feasible operating points for maximum throughput.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from characterizing link degradation via experiment (polarization drift), to developing optimal scheduling for simple linear chains using analytical theorems, and finally extending the methodology to complex, shared-resource general quantum networks using heuristics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The reliable distribution of entanglement is essential for the effective operation of quantum networks. Due to fundamental differences between quantum and classical communication systems, it is necessary to develop specialised algorithms and protocols that also account for quantum-specific constraints. In this work, we focus on the issue of recalibration. As suggested by recent experimental studies, the process of local entanglement generation in a quantum link degrades over time due to environmental changes that have to be estimated and compensated via a calibration operation, during which the link is not available. Therefore, in such a quantum network, every link alternates between an activation period, during which it operates normally, and a calibration period, during which it cannot participate in the end-to-end entanglement distribution, thereby creating a trade-off between link quality (the fidelity of generated pairs, which decays during activation) and availability (the fraction of time the link is usable, which calibration reduces). We develop analytically a protocol for optimally assigning activation periods to each link in linear quantum repeater chains, subject to any general end-to-end fidelity requirements and local initial fidelity thresholds. Building on this foundation, we extend to general quantum networks, where multiple paths may cross at common links, proposing a heuristic approach evaluated in simulations and compared with a benchmark, numerical approach, and theoretical bounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates Quantum Error Correction codes designed for biased noise, finding that idealized theoretical advantages vanish under realistic circuit noise. The authors introduce a 'bias-filtering gadget' into the XZZX code structure, demonstrating that this lightweight hardware modification can recover a measurable portion of the lost bias advantage by protecting the physical noise asymmetry during syndrome measurement.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses the gap between theory (ideal code capacity) and practice (noisy circuits), providing concrete mitigation techniques for hardware-specific error biases relevant to trapped ion or superconducting qubit platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study evaluates how much the theoretical advantage of bias-tailored Quantum Error Correction (QEC) codes diminishes when moving from idealized noise models to realistic circuit-level noise.

**Main result.** Under realistic circuit noise, the predicted advantages are significantly reduced because syndrome extraction itself degrades the physical noise asymmetry; however, a lightweight 'bias-filtering gadget' can partially recover this lost advantage.

**Method.** The authors compare code performance using both idealized code-capacity simulations and detailed circuit-level noise models incorporating gate errors and ancillary qubit encoding/decoding gadgets.

**Model / system.** The work compares XZZX codes against anisotropic rectangular surface codes, analyzing their resilience to partially-biased dephasing noise common in physical quantum hardware platforms like trapped ions or spin qubits.

**Key observables.** QEC error thresholds (p_th), entanglement infidelity ($	ext{Fe}_L$), and the relative improvement factor provided by the bias-filtering gadget.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise bias ($\eta$), circuit complexity, code distance ($d$), and the ratio of idle to gate errors ($p_{id}/p_{2q}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that syndrome extraction circuits introduce noise that degrades the initial physical noise asymmetry, which is the central limitation being overcome.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare code performance under ideal vs. circuit-level noise; one figure illustrates the bias-filtering gadget using a repetition code structure during syndrome measurement.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical advantage in idealized models, then demonstrating how this advantage collapses under realistic circuit noise due to syndrome extraction errors, and finally proposing and analyzing a specific 'bias-filtering CNOT gadget' to mitigate this degradation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We find that the substantial advantages predicted for bias-tailored quantum error correction (QEC) under code-capacity noise are strongly reduced once realistic syndrome extraction and circuit-level noise models are considered. We start by comparing XZZX codes to rectangular surface codes with a bias-dependent optimised anisotropy. Although code-capacity simulations predict an advantage of rectangular surface codes in the limit of high noise bias, this actually disappears under circuit-level noise, making the XZZX codes the preferred and simplest choice even for platforms that allow for a flexible variation of the code layout adapted to changes in noise calibration. Our results identify bias degradation during syndrome extraction under circuit-level noise as the central limitation of biased-tailored QEC. To partially mitigate this effect, we introduce a bias-filtering CNOT gadget that temporarily encodes the ancillary target qubit during syndrome extraction in a repetition code and, upon measurement and feed forward, manages to reduce the bias degradation. In a regime of high-bias and low-idle errors, this bias-filtering gadget yields a few-percent relative improvement of the XZZX code error threshold, demonstrating that lightweight bias-filtering strategies can recover part of the lost bias-tailoring advantage for realistic circuit-level noise.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the kinematics of the Pauli equation using advanced formalisms like Wigner-Vlasov. It demonstrates that the spinorial nature leads to a splitting of probability flux and derives coupled Hamilton-Jacobi equations for both the Pauli and Schrödinger systems. The paper is significant for establishing deep mathematical consistency between these fundamental quantum descriptions in electromagnetic environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical connection drawn between the spinorial nature of the Pauli equation and classical/semi-classical Hamilton-Jacobi mechanics is highly relevant for understanding quantum dynamics in external fields, bridging foundational QM theory with semi-classical methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the kinematic properties of quantum systems governed by the Pauli equation, comparing its dynamics to those described by the Schrödinger equation.

**Main result.** It establishes that the probability flux associated with the Pauli equation splits into two components corresponding to its spinor structure, and derives analogous Hamilton-Jacobi equations for both systems.

**Method.** The analysis heavily utilizes the Wigner-Vlasov formalism, employing Helmholtz decomposition and deriving coupled sets of differential equations for potentials and fluxes.

**Model / system.** The primary model is a spin-1/2 particle governed by the Pauli equation in external electromagnetic fields. The comparison system is the standard Schrödinger equation counterpart.

**Key observables.** Probability current flux ($\mathbf{v}_1, \mathbf{v}_2$), quantum potential ($Q$), Hamilton-Jacobi functions ($H_n$), and magnetic moment ($\mathbf{\mu}_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Uniform magnetic fields, asymmetric quadratic potentials, and the spin coupling constant (related to the Bohr magneton).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on specific gauge choices (e.g., Lorenz $\Psi$-gauge) and approximations within the Wigner-Vlasov expansion.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the probability flux distribution ($v_\lambdaho$) for different spin components ($\lambda$), showing directional flow relative to color coding.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first introducing the Wigner-Vlasov formalism, deriving analogues of H-J equations and motion equations (Theorem 1), then constructing a formal link between Pauli and Schrödinger dynamics (Theorem 2), establishing field consistency with Maxwell's equations (Theorem 4), and concluding with explicit solutions for specific potentials.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Based on the Wigner-Vlasov formalism, this paper investigates the kinematic properties of the Pauli equation. It is shown that the probability current associated with the Pauli equation can be represented as a superposition of two currents with certain expansion coefficients. Each of these currents corresponds to a particular component of the spinor. The expansion coefficients effectively serve as weighting functions that determine the probability contribution of the corresponding spinor component. Therefore, each spin projection corresponds to its own probability flux. A new system of the Hamilton-Jacobi equations and also a system of motion equations in electromagnetic fields are obtained, taking into account the interaction between the spin and the magnetic field. To illustrate how these equations can be applied we have investigated the quantum system kinematics in detail using an exact solution of the Pauli equation in the presence of a uniform magnetic field and an asymmetric quadratic potential.

</details>

**Authors:** Hirokazu Maruoka  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17179v1>

**Summary.** This work provides a deep theoretical framework showing that self-similarity arises from scale invariance. It unifies dimensional analysis by proving it works because physical units and parameters share underlying scaling symmetries. The paper classifies all such solutions into three types based on the mathematical structure of their associated scale functions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum systems, the rigorous mathematical machinery developed for classifying scale invariance and dimensional reduction is highly relevant to analyzing critical phenomena or asymptotic regimes in complex many-body statistical mechanics models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a unified framework for understanding dimensional analysis by rigorously formulating self-similarity from the perspective of scale invariance.

**Main result.** The paper establishes that there are three distinct kinds of self-similar solutions (Type I, II A, and II B) based on how intrinsic scale functions relate to unit transformations. It proves that dimensional analysis works because it exploits the partial sharing of scale invariance between physical units and parameters.

**Method.** The core method involves applying group theory concepts related to scale transformations ($\sigma$) to governing equations, comparing these mathematical invariances with those derived from physical units, and classifying the resulting solutions hierarchically.

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to various physical models, including one-dimensional diffusion processes, viscoelastic impact dynamics, and modified groundwater flow problems governed by non-linear partial differential equations.

**Key observables.** Self-similar profiles ($\Phi$), data collapse plots, and the structure of similarity parameters ($\Pi, Z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Scale factors ($A$), power exponents ($\alpha_{ik}$), dimensionless numbers (e.g., $c$ in groundwater flow).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes that scale functions derived from unit structures can be applied to physical parameters due to the indistinguishability of their numerical transformations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate data collapse for diffusion processes and show hierarchical relationships between different classes of similarity parameters (First Class $ightarrow$ Second Class).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining scale invariance, then applying it to specific PDEs (diffusion, groundwater flow) to derive initial similarity forms. It then develops a general mathematical framework comparing unit scaling vs. parameter scaling, leading to the final three-fold classification of self-similarity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we formulate self-similarity from the perspective of scale invariance, where a self-similar form is understood as the transformation of a function into a form invariant under scale transformations. By applying this formulation to physical parameters, which consist of numerical values and units, it is demonstrated that dimensional analysis works for physical problems because scale invariance is partially shared between units and physical parameters. This naturally leads to the distinction between similarity of the first kind and similarity of the second kind according to whether the scale functions induced by units and those associated with physical parameters are equivalent or not. Self-similar solutions of the second kind can be further classified according to whether the power exponents of the similarity parameters include functions of dimensionless numbers. This leads to the conclusion that there are three kinds of self-similar solutions. The present work provides a unified framework for understanding dimensional analysis and a universal classification of self-similarity in physical problems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the performance of Adiabatic Quantum Flux Parametron gates, crucial for cryogenic superconducting electronics. It models how non-ideal Josephson junctions (SNS and WL) deviate from ideal sinusoidal behavior by using polynomial expansions in circuit simulations. The results quantify these deviations, showing that junction type dictates critical trade-offs between switching speed, current swing, and operational margin.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware limitations in superconducting quantum computing by analyzing how material imperfections (non-sinusoidal CPRs) impact fundamental logic gate performance metrics like speed and energy dissipation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how non-sinusoidal current-phase relations (CPR) in Josephson junctions affect the performance of Adiabatic Quantum Flux Parametron (AQFP) gates.

**Main result.** Non-ideal junction types (SNS and WL) significantly alter the switching dynamics, delay, and stability margins compared to ideal SIS junctions, demonstrating trade-offs between speed and current swing for cryogenic digital processing.

**Method.** The authors used symbolic device modeling within the Keysight ADS simulator to analyze transient behavior of AQFP circuits under various junction models.

**Model / system.** The system is based on AQFP logic gates, superconducting circuits utilizing SQUID loops containing Josephson Junctions (JJ). The model compares ideal SIS junctions against non-ideal SNS and WL junctions by incorporating their respective CPRs into the circuit simulation.

**Key observables.** Switching speed, delay, output current swing ($\mu$A), and signal integrity in a 3-stage shift register.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical currents ($I_{c1} = I_{c2} = 50\ \mu	ext{A}$), operating frequency (e.g., $5 	ext{ GHz}$), and the nature of the CPR deviation from sinusoidal.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that using polynomial models via SDD accurately captures the behavior of non-ideal junctions for circuit simulation purposes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the AQFP buffer schematic, potential energy profiles, comparisons of $I-\phi$ curves (SIS vs. SNS vs. WL), and transient simulations showing signal propagation through a 3-stage shift register.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the AQFP concept using ideal junctions, then models non-ideal junctions (SNS/WL) via polynomial expansions, simulates the basic buffer gate operation, and finally demonstrates functionality using a multi-stage shift register.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Adiabatic quantum flux parametron (AQFP) gate is a promising approach to scale up the cryogenic microwave electronics for superconducting qubit multiplexed control. However, the performance of these circuits depends on the quality of the Josephson junctions which are ideally superconductor-insulator-superconductor (SIS) type following the ideal sinusoidal relation between current and quantum phase. We demonstrate how the non-sinusoidal current-phase relation in Superconductor-Normal metal-Superconductor (SNS) and weak link (WL) junctions affects the speed, delay, and margin of the AQFP gates. The JJ models are defined in the Keysight ADS simulator using symbolically defined device (SDD) method.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces SPICE-Q, a comprehensive framework designed to industrialize superconducting quantum chip design. It solves the problem of fragmented simulation tools by creating a unified data chain connecting electromagnetics, device physics, Hamiltonian dynamics, and manufacturing statistics. This shift treats quantum hardware development as a rigorous engineering workflow necessary for scaling fault-tolerant computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial engineering roadmap for applying advanced open-system dynamics and many-body techniques by formalizing the interface between electromagnetic field theory and quantum information processing models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The design of large-scale, fault-tolerant superconducting quantum processors requires a unified engineering workflow that connects device physics to system performance.

**Main result.** SPICE-Q proposes a Design-Technology Co-Optimization (DTCO) framework that treats quantum chip design as an industrial engineering process rather than isolated simulations.

**Method.** The framework unifies disparate simulation tools (EM solvers, Hamiltonian dynamics, classical circuit simulators) through standardized model interfaces and a traceable data chain.

**Model / system.** The system is superconducting quantum chips built from Josephson junctions, modeled by coupling electromagnetic field solutions to effective Hamiltonians describing qubit dynamics and noise channels.

**Key observables.** Frequency, coupling strength, anharmonicity, decoherence times ($T_1/T_2$), readout performance, and overall manufacturing yield.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Process constraints (PDK), Josephson junction variability ($\delta I_c$), resonator quality factors ($Q$), and 3D interconnect parasitics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that scaling requires treating the entire design process—from physics to fabrication statistics—as a single, continuous, model-driven engineering workflow.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds an analogy with classical SPICE development (moving from physical equations to parameterized models) and applies this structure to quantum computing, detailing the necessary integration points between EM simulation, Hamiltonian extraction, and statistical yield analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose SPICE-Q, a SPICE-inspired design-technology co-optimization framework for superconducting quantum processors. Rather than replacing tools such as HFSS, Qiskit Metal, pyEPR, SQcircuit, SQuADDS, scqubits, or QuTiP, SPICE-Q aims to connect them through a unified, traceable data chain spanning process rules, layout, electromagnetic simulation, energy-participation-ratio and circuit quantization, Hamiltonian extraction, noise analysis, cryogenic test, and manufacturing feedback. The central mapping is from process and PDK constraints to layout geometry, electromagnetic modes, equivalent circuit parameters, effective Hamiltonians, and finally metrics such as frequency, coupling, anharmonicity, decoherence, readout performance, and yield. This flow must capture Josephson-junction variability, transmon frequency allocation, resonator and Purcell constraints, coupler crosstalk, microwave routing, 3D interconnects, material/interface loss, package modes, and wafer-scale process statistics. By introducing standardized model interfaces, statistical parameter models, model cards, version governance, and closed-loop calibration from cryogenic and fabrication data, SPICE-Q frames superconducting quantum-chip design as an engineering workflow rather than a collection of isolated simulations. We argue that scalable and fault-tolerant quantum processors will require such a continuous model chain from device physics and electromagnetic fields to quantum dynamics, noise, manufacturability, and system-level yield.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies statistical mechanics principles, specifically the Wealth Thermalization Hypothesis (WTH), to model global wealth inequality. It shows that the Rayleigh-Jeans thermal distribution accurately describes empirical data for household wealth and national GDP across decades. The work argues that this thermodynamic condensation mechanism provides a universal physical law governing social stratification.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful analogy between condensed matter physics concepts like thermalization and condensation (RJ phenomenon) and macroscopic socio-economic phenomena like global wealth distribution, suggesting universal physical laws govern social structures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide a thermodynamic description of global wealth inequality using the Wealth Thermalization Hypothesis (WTH).

**Main result.** The Rayleigh-Jeans (RJ) thermal distribution provides a universal and good description for various real-world curves related to wealth inequality, such as Lorenz curves for household wealth, GDP, and stock markets.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving analytical expressions for the Lorenz curve and Gini coefficient using continuous models like RJS and RJE spectra, and fitting these theoretical predictions to empirical economic data.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as 'Social Stratification in a Society' (SSS) where wealth layers are associated with energy levels governed by a nonlinear dynamical system conserving total energy and probability norm. The core model uses the RJS and RJE spectra derived from statistical mechanics principles.

**Key observables.** Lorenz curve ($w$ vs $h$), Gini Coefficient ($G$), Pareto Probability Distribution ($C(w_m)$), Temperature ($T$), Chemical Potential ($\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The energy parameter $\epsilon$, the spectral parameters $a$, and the regime of condensation (e.g., low $\epsilon$ for strong inequality).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system undergoes dynamical thermalization governed by weak nonlinear perturbations, allowing the use of a grand canonical ensemble description.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare theoretical Lorenz curves (RJS/RJE) and Pareto distributions against real-world data for household wealth (World 2021), GDP evolution (UN data 1973–2023), and stock markets, demonstrating quantitative agreement.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the WTH framework to deriving analytical solutions for key diagnostics using RJS/RJE models. It then systematically applies these models by fitting parameters to diverse datasets (household wealth, GDP, etc.) to argue for universality.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

According to the recent Wealth Thermalization Hypothesis (WTH) the wealth inequality in the world is described by the Rayleigh-Jeans (RJ) thermal distribution of interacting agents in a society with social stratification. In this concept, the wealth layers of society are associated with energy levels from a nonlinear dynamical system conserving two integrals of motion being total energy and probability norm. This leads to RJ condensation and the formation of a huge poverty phase of low wealth and a tiny oligarchic phase that captures a main part of total society wealth. This RJ phenomenon has similarities with self cleaning in multimode optical fibers and constraint driven condensation in various physical systems. We analyze real Lorenz and Pareto curves for wealth of households in countries and the world, Gross Domestic Product of countries, market capitalization of companies at stock exchange of Hong Kong, Shanghai, London, bitcoin transactions, world trade between countries and show that the WTH theory gives a good description of these curves. On the basis of this comparison we argue that the RJ thermal distribution provides a universal description of wealth inequality in the world.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper outlines the 'Quantum Chip Paradigm Framework' necessary to industrialize superconducting quantum computing. It argues that Q-EDA must adopt a bottom-up, physical modeling approach, mirroring classical EDA advancements. The framework establishes a staged roadmap for achieving scalable, fault-tolerant quantum processors by standardizing design knowledge from physics down to system integration.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial engineering blueprint for scaling quantum computation, framing the theoretical physics problem within the practical constraints of semiconductor manufacturing and system integration.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The development of Quantum Electronic Design Automation (Q-EDA) is required to scale superconducting quantum chips from laboratory prototypes to reliable, fault-tolerant engineering systems.

**Main result.** The paper proposes a comprehensive 'Quantum Chip Paradigm Framework' that mandates treating Q-EDA as a full development paradigm, necessitating a bottom-up approach starting from physical device models.

**Method.** It draws an analogy between the current quantum design challenges and the historical 'SPICE moment' in classical EDA, advocating for a structured, hierarchical methodology.

**Model / system.** The system is superconducting quantum chips, modeled using underlying physical components like Josephson junctions, resonators, and couplers. The framework proposes a hierarchy from physical structures $\rightarrow$ qubit PCells $\rightarrow$ logical qubits $\rightarrow$ functional IP $\rightarrow$ Quantum SoC.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelities, coherence time, crosstalk structures, resource overhead (T gates/Toffoli), and overall system error budgeting.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit frequency planning, control complexity, packaging interconnects, and the need for Design-Technology-Measurement Co-optimization (DTCO).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that progress requires standardizing design knowledge into reusable, auditable engineering objects across all abstraction levels.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but the structure implies a progression through different layers of abstraction for quantum chip design.

**Paper structure.** The paper traces the evolution of EDA from classical IC design (SPICE moment) to propose a multi-stage Q-EDA roadmap (1.0 $\rightarrow$ 2.0 $\rightarrow$ 3.0), emphasizing physical modeling and closed-loop optimization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Electronic Design Automation (Q-EDA) is emerging as quantum chips move from laboratory prototypes to scalable engineering systems. This paper argues that superconducting quantum chip design is approaching a "SPICE moment" similar to early classical EDA, where growing qubit scale, control complexity, frequency planning, packaging, process variation, and cryogenic measurement feedback require a shift from experience-based design to model-driven engineering. We propose a Quantum Chip Paradigm Framework that treats Q-EDA not only as software, but as part of the quantum chip development paradigm. Unlike classical HDL-first design, quantum chip design must begin with physical structures such as Josephson junctions, resonators, couplers, readout elements, control lines, and packaging environments. The framework emphasizes PCell-based modeling, SPICE-Q simulation, Quantum PDKs, and design-technology-measurement co-optimization. We further outline a hierarchical Q-EDA system spanning physical structures, qubit PCells, logical qubits, quantum arithmetic, functional quantum IP, and Quantum SoC systems. The key goal is to turn physical models, layout rules, simulation results, fabrication data, and measurement feedback into reusable and auditable engineering objects for large-scale quantum processors and fault-tolerant quantum computing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Quantum Computing Algebra (QCA), a novel formalism built on real geometric algebra with a split signature to simplify quantum computation implementation. It successfully maps complex concepts like multi-qubit gates and entanglement into algebraic products, demonstrating its utility by simulating payoffs in quantum game theory models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a concrete, computationally efficient algebraic framework (QCA) that bypasses complex matrix exponentiation by using geometric algebra is highly relevant. It offers a novel mathematical toolset for simulating quantum dynamics and entanglement in systems where direct Hamiltonian simulation becomes intractable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a practical framework for quantum computation by bridging abstract Dirac formalism with efficient computational implementation using Geometric Algebra (GA). It specifically addresses the need for an algebraic structure suitable for real-number computation.

**Main result.** A Quantum Computing Algebra (QCA) based on a split-signature construction is proposed, successfully translating quantum mechanics into a real geometric algebra framework. This allows for efficient representation and calculation of multi-qubit systems and entangled strategies in applications like quantum game theory.

**Method.** The authors utilize Geometric Algebra (GA) to represent qubits and operators, employing a split-signature approach within a real Clifford algebra structure. They derive algebraic rules for tensor products and map fermionic creation/annihilation operators to qubit gates.

**Model / system.** The model centers on multi-qubit quantum systems, initially described by the spinor $\psi \in \mathbb{C}^2$ for single qubits. The theory extends this using an algebraic structure derived from Witt generators and idempotents, allowing simulation of complex interactions like those in quantum game theory.

**Key observables.** Payoffs ($\pi_A, \pi_B$) calculated in quantum game theory scenarios (e.g., Prisoner's Dilemma), and the probabilities ($P_{AA}, P_{AB}$, etc.) describing the final entangled state $|\psi_fangle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Entanglement parameter $\gamma$, individual player strategy parameters ($\psi_1, \psi_2$), and the algebraic structure defined by the split signature.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that using real geometric algebras via a split-signature construction provides a significant computational advantage over previous positive-definite methods for practical implementation on real computers.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Bloch sphere basis states, the EWL quantization protocol scheme for two qubits, and 3D plots showing calculated payoffs ($\pi_A$ vs $\psi_A$, $\pi_B$ vs $\psi_B$) under varying entanglement conditions ($\gamma$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the QCA framework using split-signature GA (Chunk 1). It then details how to represent multi-qubit gates and tensor products algebraically, including mapping fermionic operators via Jordan-Wigner transformation (Chunk 2). Finally, it applies this machinery to model quantum game theory payoffs for systems like the Prisoner's Dilemma (Chunks 3 & 4).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a real geometric algebra framework designed for the direct translation of the Dirac formalism into geometric algebra representations. Unlike previous approaches based on positive-definite signatures, QCA employs a split-signature construction that enables a natural realization of quantum states and operators while simplifying computational implementation. We further present an implementation of QCA using the \textit{GAALOP} software and show how quantum gates and multi-qubit systems can be efficiently represented and generated computationally. As an application, we demonstrate the use of QCA in quantum game theory, where the real-algebraic formulation provides computational advantages for modeling entangled strategies and quantum interactions. The proposed framework establishes a practical bridge between the abstract formalism of quantum computation and efficient geometric algebra implementations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the complex Conformal Field Theory associated with the 2D Q-state Potts model, extending its analysis beyond the standard physical regime ($Q \le 4$). By treating $Q$ as a continuous or complex variable, the authors propose that CFT data can be found through analytic continuation of known results. This work is significant for mapping out the full theoretical landscape of this critical system.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on statistical mechanics, the rigorous mapping to CFTs provides deep insights into universality classes and non-unitary theories, which share mathematical structures relevant to open quantum systems and boundary physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to extend the understanding of Conformal Field Theories (CFTs) associated with the two-dimensional Q-state Potts model into regimes where the number of states, Q, is greater than 4 or complex.

**Main result.** The authors conjecture that all conformal data for this extended regime can be obtained by analytic continuation from known exact results for $Q \le 4$, establishing a framework for complex CFTs in this model.

**Method.** The research combines analytical derivations, numerical transfer-matrix computations, and advanced techniques like minigrid extrapolation to map out the parameter space of the associated CFTs.

**Model / system.** The system is the two-dimensional Q-state Potts model defined on a triangular lattice, which is reformulated into an equivalent loop model whose partition function allows for continuous variation of $Q$.

**Key observables.** Central charge ($c$), critical exponents, three-point structure constants ($\omega$), and the location of fixed points ($z_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of states, Q (treated as a complex variable), and the lattice size $L$ (used for extrapolation).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the CFT data can be obtained via analytic continuation from the well-understood regime where $Q \le 4$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate paths in the complex Q-plane, convergence of extrapolated parameters ($z_c, c, \omega$) as $L 	o \infty$, and stability domains defined by conformal dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the model's fixed points across different Q regimes (real vs. complex), develops mathematical tools using transfer matrices and CFT techniques (like Dotsenko-Fateev formulas), and concludes with extensive numerical extrapolation of critical parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The two-dimensional $Q$-state Potts model with real couplings has a first-order transition for $Q>4$. Starting from a triangular-lattice Potts model with two- and three-spin interactions, we study an equivalent loop model in which $Q$ is a continuous parameter. By a combination of analytical and numerical arguments, we show that this loop model allows for the collision of a critical and a tricritical fixed point at $Q=4$. These then emerge as a pair of complex conformally invariant theories at $Q>4$, or even complex $Q$, for suitable complex coupling constants. We conjecture that all conformal data (such as the central charge, critical exponents, and three-point structure constants) can be obtained by analytic continuation of known exact results for the loop model with $Q \le 4$. This conjecture is checked, both for real $Q>4$ and for $Q \in \mathbb{C}$, by extensive transfer-matrix computations and comparison to previous studies for $Q=5$.

</details>

### [Acceleration-induced spectral blind spots in stimulated atomic transitions](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17396v1)

**Authors:** Jiawei Hu, Hongwei Yu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17396v1>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically demonstrates that uniform acceleration can induce spectral blind spots in atomic transitions by modifying light-matter coupling. These blind spots manifest as frequencies where both normal stimulated absorption and Unruh excitation are simultaneously suppressed, providing a unique signature of the Unruh effect through signal absence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel signature of the Unruh effect—a frequency-dependent *absence* of response rather than an expected enhancement—which is highly relevant for quantum optics and open system dynamics in non-inertial frames.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how uniform acceleration fundamentally alters the stimulated light-matter interaction response of an atom, potentially leading to frequency-selective spectral blind spots.

**Main result.** Spectral blind spots occur at discrete frequencies where both ordinary stimulated absorption and acceleration-induced Unruh excitation simultaneously vanish for photons with transverse momentum.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum electrodynamics formalism applied to a two-level atom coupled to a massless scalar field along an accelerated trajectory, involving Bessel functions.

**Model / system.** The system is a uniformly accelerated two-level atom interacting weakly with the quantized electromagnetic vacuum field. The calculations distinguish between oblique ($k_\perp 
eq 0$) and collinear ($k_\perp = 0$) driving fields.

**Key observables.** Stimulated transition probability, spectral blind spots (frequencies where absorption and excitation vanish).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Proper acceleration ($a$), atomic transition frequency ($\omega_0$), transverse photon momentum component ($k_\perp$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Weak coupling ($\lambda$) is assumed, and the analysis focuses on uniform acceleration trajectories in natural units.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 plots a function involving modified Bessel functions of the second kind, $K_2 i\omega_0/a(k_\perp/a)$, which governs both transition probabilities for transversely propagating photons.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by calculating transition amplitudes using field mode expansions along an accelerated path. It then derives and analyzes the resulting stimulated absorption and Unruh excitation probabilities, contrasting the results for oblique versus collinear driving fields to pinpoint the spectral suppression mechanism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Stimulated transitions are among the most fundamental processes in light-matter interaction, underlying resonant absorption and emission in atomic systems. Here we show that uniform acceleration can convert this familiar response into a frequency-selective absence of response. Specifically, when an incident photon has a nonzero momentum component transverse to the acceleration, the stimulated transition probability vanishes at a discrete set of frequencies fixed by the acceleration, the atomic transition frequency, and the photon propagation angle. At these spectral blind spots, both ordinary stimulated absorption and acceleration-induced excitation are simultaneously suppressed, rendering the atom effectively unresponsive to the incident radiation. The effect arises from the nontrivial response of accelerated atoms to quantum vacuum fluctuations and provides a distinctive signature of the Unruh effect through the absence, rather than the enhancement, of stimulated transitions. We further provide an order-of-magnitude estimate showing that an electron-based implementation with spin splitting in combined electric and magnetic fields could access the required parameter regime. These results reveal an unexplored form of acceleration-modified light-matter interaction and identify spectral blind spots as a new manifestation of the Unruh effect.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses sophisticated Monte Carlo simulations to map out the first-order phase transition line in the 3D Blume-Capel ferromagnet. By analyzing energy distributions and free-energy barriers, the authors detail the crossover from weak to strong first-order behavior near the tricritical point. The study highlights both the success of their simulation methods and their inherent limitations when approaching critical points in higher dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the use of advanced simulation techniques (like generalized ensemble methods) to precisely locate critical points and characterize crossover regimes is methodologically relevant for understanding quantum phase transitions in AMO systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates first-order phase transitions in the three-dimensional Blume-Capel ferromagnet, focusing on characterizing the crossover behavior from weak to strong first-order regimes down towards low temperatures and connecting this regime to the tricritical point.

**Main result.** The analysis successfully characterizes the evolution of the energy probability density function and determines the interface tension approaching the tricritical region via its vanishing behavior. However, the field-mixing method shows increasing sensitivity limitations in 3D near the tricritical point for accessible system sizes.

**Method.** The research combines multicanonical simulations with two-parameter Wang-Landau sampling within a generalized-ensemble approach to study phase coexistence and finite-size scaling of thermodynamic observables.

**Model / system.** The model is the three-dimensional spin-1 Blume-Capel ferromagnet defined on a simple cubic lattice, governed by a Hamiltonian involving ferromagnetic exchange ($J$) and a crystal-field coupling ($\Delta$).

**Key observables.** Energy probability density function $P(e\Delta)$, free-energy barriers, interface tension ($\Sigma$), magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$), and pseudo-transition fields ($\Delta^*_L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$) and crystal-field coupling ($\Delta$); the tricritical point $(\Delta_t, T_t)$ is a key focus.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that finite-size scaling laws can be accurately applied to extrapolate bulk properties, though limitations are noted near the tricritical region due to numerical resolution issues in 3D.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram, show normalized probability distributions $P(e\Delta)$ revealing double-peaked structures, and plot the interface tension ($\Sigma_\infty$) as a function of temperature distance from the critical point.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically applies advanced simulation techniques (MUCA/Wang-Landau) to map out the phase boundary. It analyzes finite-size scaling of observables like energy distributions and free-energy barriers, comparing results derived from direct sampling with those from field-mixing analysis along different parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate first-order phase transitions in the three-dimensional Blume-Capel ferromagnet on the simple cubic lattice by combining multicanonical simulations with two-parameter Wang-Landau sampling. The generalized-ensemble approach enables a detailed characterization of the coexistence region over a broad range of temperatures and crystal-field couplings, extending from the vicinity of the tricritical point deep into the first-order regime. We analyze the finite-size scaling behavior of thermodynamic observables, with particular emphasis on the energy probability density function, free-energy barriers, and interfacial properties. The evolution of the double-peaked energy distributions reveals a gradual crossover from weak to strong first-order behavior as the temperature is lowered. From the scaling of the free-energy barrier we determine the interface tension along the coexistence line and characterize its approach to the tricritical region through its vanishing behavior. In parallel, a field-mixing analysis based on the joint density of states obtained from two-parameter Wang-Landau simulations is employed to locate first-order transition points and probe tricritical behavior. While this approach has been highly successful in two-dimensional realizations of the Blume-Capel model, we find that in three dimensions its practical implementation becomes increasingly sensitive in the vicinity of the tricritical region, where the shallow structure of the relevant scaling variable distribution limits the ability to resolve coexistence conditions for the system sizes currently accessible. These results delineate the range of applicability of the method in three dimensions and provide a consistent picture of the first-order regime of the model.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that spatially correlated disorder, inherent in moiré superconductors due to twist angle variations, fundamentally alters superconducting phase transitions. By modeling the system using a Random Impedance Network, the authors show that this correlation smears the BKT transition and masks intrinsic order parameter symmetries, suggesting $\sigma_1$ is the key diagnostic observable.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant because it uses advanced theoretical tools (elasticity theory coupled with network modeling) to analyze emergent phenomena in disordered quantum materials, providing diagnostic signatures ($\sigma_1$ vs. EMT failure) that are crucial for understanding complex many-body states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how spatially correlated disorder, arising from twist angle inhomogeneities in moiré superconductors, masks the expected Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) transition. Furthermore, this disorder obscures the ability to distinguish between different superconducting order parameter symmetries.

**Main result.** Correlated disorder smears the sharp BKT transition into a smooth crossover and causes low-temperature superfluid stiffness to decay in a manner mimicking nodal pairing, regardless of the true symmetry.

**Method.** The authors employ a Random Impedance Network (RIN) model built upon local critical temperatures ($T_{c,i}$) derived from elasticity theory describing twist angle variations. They compare these exact numerical results against simpler Effective Medium Theory (EMT) approximations.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as two-dimensional superconductors, specifically twisted multilayer graphene in a moiré pattern. Disorder is introduced via long-range correlated fluctuations in the local critical temperature ($T_{c,i}$), which are governed by elasticity theory and result in $\log|r|$ correlations for the twist angle.

**Key observables.** Superfluid stiffness ($J_s$), real part of optical conductivity ($\sigma_1$), DC resistivity ($ho$), and the local superconducting gap structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle variance ($	ext{Var}[	heta]$), mean twist angle ($	heta_0$), and temperature relative to local $T_c$ ($	au_i$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions involve modeling disorder via continuum elasticity theory leading to long-range correlations, and using the RIN approach to solve for global transport properties.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare exact RIN solutions to EMT approximations across varying disorder strengths, showing how $\sigma_1$ retains a residual value in the presence of correlated disorder where EMT fails.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using elasticity theory to derive long-range correlations ($C_	heta(r) \propto -\log|r|$). It then implements this into an RIN model, calculating transport observables ($\sigma_1$, $J_s$) and contrasting these exact results against the predictions of EMT.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional superconductors, including twisted multilayer graphene, should exhibit a BKT transition, and the $T$-dependence of the superfluid stiffness should distinguish between nodal or gapped order parameter symmetries. However, this picture dramatically changes when spatially correlated disorder is taken into account. Such correlations naturally arise in moiré systems due to twist angle inhomogeneities, which we model using elasticity theory. Using a random impedance network based on realistic disorder in the local $T_c$, we show that the finite-frequency conductance reveals a smeared percolative transition instead of a BKT transition. At low temperatures, the disorder can effectively obscure the distinction between nodal and fully gapped superconducting order parameters. We propose that the real part of the conductivity can be used as a key diagnostic observable to probe the relevance of correlated disorder.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper constructs a theoretical framework describing how electrons fractionalize into spinons and chargons within the pseudogap phase of cuprates. It demonstrates that the resulting dipolar interaction, dictated by lattice symmetry, generates a repulsive pairing glue peaked at the antiferromagnetic wave vector. Solving the BCS equation with this interaction confirms the emergence of $d_{x^2-y^2}$ superconductivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed microscopic mechanism for high-Tc superconductivity in cuprates by linking it directly to emergent gauge symmetries and spin fluctuations, which is highly relevant to strongly correlated electron physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a theory for the transition from a fractionalized Fermi liquid ($	ext{FL}^*$) into a $d_{x^2-y^2}$ superconductor, focusing on the mechanism of Cooper pair formation.

**Main result.** A repulsive dipolar interaction, arising from the projective action of translation symmetry on spinon-chargon bound states, is shown to be necessary and sufficient to induce $d_{x^2-y^2}$-wave superconductivity via BCS theory.

**Method.** The authors derive an effective Hamiltonian by treating electrons as spinon-chargon bound states, calculating the resulting dipolar interaction potential, and solving the self-consistent BCS gap equation.

**Model / system.** The model starts from a spin-fermion description of electron doping in cuprates, utilizing concepts of fractionalization into spinons and chargons coupled by an emergent U(1) gauge symmetry.

**Key observables.** Superconducting order parameter $\Delta(\mathbf{q})$, average superconducting gap magnitude, and the effective dipolar interaction $V_{q_1, -q_1; q}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron doping level (small doping on top of half-filling), antiferromagnetic wave vector ($Q$), and parameters related to the gauge field coupling strength ($m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system can be described by a deconfined U(1) gauge theory in the pseudogap regime, and approximations are made regarding continuum limits and projecting interactions onto lowest-energy bands.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of the Fermi surface with doping, schematic representations of the effective dipolar interaction in momentum space, and plots showing the BZ-averaged superconducting order parameter ($\Delta$) as a function of electron pocket fraction.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the fractionalized description (spinon/chargon), deriving the Coulomb interaction potential via gauge field integration, calculating the effective dipolar interaction based on symmetry constraints, and finally solving the BCS gap equation using this repulsive interaction to confirm $d$-wave pairing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Starting from the spin-fermion model or Hertz-Millis theory describing electrons coupled to anti-ferromagnetic spin fluctuations we develop a theory to describe the transition from a fractionalized Fermi liquid into a $d_{x^2-y^2}$ superconductor. We focus on small electron doping on top of the half-filled state. The doped electrons enter the system as spinon-chargon bound states, which form a small, reconstructed Fermi surface. The bound states are neutral under the emergent U(1) gauge symmetry of the fractionalized Fermi liquid, but interact via a dipolar two-body potential. We show that because of the projective action of translation symmetry on the spinons and chargons, the Fourier components of this repulsive dipolar interaction are peaked at the anti-ferromagnetic wave vector, thereby providing a robust microscopic mechanism for $d_{x^2-y^2}$ pairing in a fractionalized metal.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study develops an orbital-spin-lattice model to explain clusterization (trimerization) in transition metal oxides. The key finding is that while electronic interactions favor certain phases, the inclusion of lattice distortions is necessary to stabilize the experimentally observed trimer ground state. The authors use a combination of quantum ED and classical Monte Carlo simulations to map out complex phase diagrams.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a comprehensive framework for understanding how structural degrees of freedom can stabilize exotic magnetic/orbital orders in strongly correlated materials, which is highly relevant to modern condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to microscopically understand how cluster formation, specifically trimerization, occurs in orbitally degenerate transition-metal compounds due to the interplay between spin, orbital, and lattice degrees of freedom.

**Main result.** Incorporating ionic lattice displacements is crucial; it qualitatively alters phase competition, leading to the robust stabilization of a trimerized ground state that was otherwise unstable or degenerate with other phases.

**Method.** The authors derive an effective orbital-lattice model and employ both quantum Exact Diagonalization (ED) for small clusters and classical Monte Carlo simulations for finite-temperature studies.

**Model / system.** The system models transition-metal compounds ($	ext{LiVS}_2$, $	ext{LiVO}_2$) with electrons in the $t_{2g}$ manifold, starting from a Kanamori-Hubbard Hamiltonian. The effective model incorporates quantum $S=1$ spin degrees of freedom coupled to lattice distortions.

**Key observables.** Ground state phases (Trimer, Zigzag, Ferri), specific heat ($C$), and structure factors ($S_X(q)$) used to diagnose ordering types.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hund's coupling strength ($\eta = J_H/U$), lattice distortion parameter ($
u$), and elastic constant ($\kappa$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on projecting the low-energy sector onto the local $S=1$ triplet manifold, and later simplifying this to a classical orbital-lattice model for large-scale simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing transitions between Trimer, Zigzag, and Ferri phases as functions of coupling strengths ($\eta$ vs $
u$), and energy comparisons between different ordered states in both quantum and classical frameworks.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving the effective orbital-lattice Hamiltonian to performing ground state calculations (ED) comparing various competing phases. It then extends this to finite temperature using Monte Carlo simulations, culminating in a detailed phase diagram analysis showing the role of lattice coupling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive an effective orbital-lattice model with quantum $S=1$ degrees of freedom for transition-metal compounds, providing a microscopic understanding of cluster formation driven by the cooperative interplay of spin, orbital, and lattice degrees of freedom. Motivated by the trimerized phases observed in LiVS$_2$ and LiVO$_2$, we consider a triangular-lattice three-orbital system with two electrons per site occupying the threefold-degenerate $t_{2g}$ manifold. Starting from a multiorbital Kanamori-Hubbard Hamiltonian, we project the low-energy sector onto the local $S=1$ triplet manifold, in which two electrons occupy different orbitals according to Hund's coupling. The resulting effective model exhibits exchange networks whose geometry is determined by the orbital configuration. However, the orbital-driven exchange interactions alone do not stabilize the experimentally observed trimer phase. We find that by incorporating ionic lattice displacements that modulate transfer integrals and induce bond-dependent exchange couplings on shortened and elongated bonds, the phase competition is qualitatively altered, leading to the robust stabilization of a trimerized ground state within a fully quantum-mechanical framework. We further show that a simplified orbital-lattice model, in which the spin-exchange energy is replaced by effective bond energies, faithfully reproduces the essential ground-state properties of the microscopic model. This reduced description enables large-scale finite-temperature simulations and reveals a rich sequence of thermal phase transitions, including first-order, second-order, and Kosterlitz-Thouless transitions into distinct spin-, orbital-, and lattice-ordered phases.

</details>
