**Summary.** This paper develops an exact theoretical framework to calculate equilibrium cluster statistics for particles binding on a one-dimensional ring. By analyzing cooperative and anticooperative interactions, it derives key observables like cluster size distributions using advanced combinatorial methods. The results provide quantitative benchmarks for understanding how local interactions dictate the global spatial organization of adsorbed species.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous derivation of exact, finite-size statistical mechanics results for 1D systems provides crucial benchmarks that can be compared against more complex simulations or experimental measurements in biological contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the equilibrium clustering statistics for cooperative (attractive) and anticooperative (repulsive) binding interactions.

**Main result.** Exact finite-size expressions are derived for key observables like mean occupancy, domain walls, and cluster size distributions, characterizing how spatial organization changes with interaction strength.

**Method.** The analysis employs the grand-canonical formulation using both a transfer matrix approach and an advanced combinatorial cluster-based formalism to manage state space complexity.

**Model / system.** The model is a finite one-dimensional lattice gas of L sites with periodic boundary conditions, serving as a minimal system for adsorption/binding on ring-like substrates. The interactions are governed by nearest-neighbor coupling (J) and an effective chemical potential ($\mu$).

**Key observables.** Mean occupancy ($\langle\varphiangle$), mean number of domain walls ($\langle Wangle$), mean number of clusters ($\langle Kangle$), cluster-size distribution ($P(k)$), and site-weighted cluster-size distribution ($Q(k)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength (J) and chemical potential ($\mu$). Key regimes include strong cooperativity, strong anticooperativity, and the noninteracting Hill-Langmuir limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving exact expressions for finite periodic systems, utilizing a cluster-based combinatorial formulation to reduce state space complexity from $2^L$ to one scaling with integer partitions ($p(L)$).

**Figures summary.** Figures show contour plots of mean observables ($\langle\varphiangle$, $\langle Wangle$, $\langle Kangle$) versus interaction parameters, and parametric plots illustrating the dependence of cluster statistics on occupancy ($\langle\varphiangle$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives exact finite-size expressions for various correlation functions and observables using two complementary formalisms: a site-based transfer matrix approach and a combinatorial cluster-counting method. It analyzes limiting cases (e.g., $J 	o \pm\infty$, $J=0$) to characterize different binding regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study equilibrium clustering in a finite one-dimensional lattice gas of $L$ sites with periodic boundary conditions, as a minimal model for adsorption and binding on small ring-like substrates. Using a grand-canonical formulation with nearest-neighbor coupling, we derive exact finite-size expressions for the mean occupancy, the mean number of domain walls, and the mean number of clusters. Building on exact $k$-site correlation functions, we further derive expressions for the mean number of clusters of size $k$ and for two complementary size statistics: the cluster-size distribution, and the site-weighted cluster-size distribution. These observables characterize how spatial organization changes across attractive (cooperative) and repulsive (anticooperative) interactions, and highlight finite-size and parity-dependent effects of the underlying lattice, the latter being particularly pronounced near half filling in small systems. To access larger lattices without enumerating all $2^L$ microstates, we also develop a cluster-based combinatorial formulation in which configurations are classified by cluster counts and sizes, reducing the effective state space to a set whose size scales with integer partitions, $\approx e^{\sqrt{L}}$, rather than with $\approx e^{L}$. Taken together, our results provide exact benchmarks for finite periodic systems and suggest experimentally relevant cluster observables that complement occupancy-based measures of cooperativity, with particular relevance for binding on ring-like substrates for biological assemblies.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a comprehensive microscopic theory explaining the Transdimensional Anomalous Hall Effect in rhombohedral graphite. It attributes the effect to a Stoner instability that induces spin-valley polarization ($\eta$), which simultaneously controls both the intrinsic and planar components of the anomalous Hall response. The theoretical framework successfully predicts key experimental signatures like the common critical temperature ($T_c$) across different magnetic field orientations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of how a magnetic instability (Stoner) couples to structural/orbital effects (Peierls phase) to generate multiple correlated transport responses is highly relevant to understanding emergent quantum phenomena in strongly interacting electronic systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a microscopic theory for the Transdimensional Anomalous Hall Effect (TDAHE) observed in rhombohedral graphite.

**Main result.** The TDAHE is attributed to a flat-band Stoner instability coupled to Peierls-phase gap modulation, leading to a common critical temperature ($T_c$) governing both AHE and TDAHE responses.

**Method.** Theoretical calculations employing self-consistent Hartree-Fock methods, Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT), and comparison with Ising model simulations are used.

**Model / system.** The system is nine-layer rhombohedral graphite modeled by an $N$-layer ABC-stacked Hamiltonian. The physics hinges on the Stoner criterion ($Uho(\epsilon_F) > 1$) driving spin-valley polarization ($\eta$).

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall Conductivity ($\sigma_{	ext{int}}^{xy}$), Transdimensional AHE component ($\sigma_{	ext{PHE}}^{xy}$), and the common critical temperature ($T_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $N$ (number of layers, $N \ge 5$), Stoner interaction strength ($Uho$), orbital g-factor ($g_{	ext{orb}} \propto N-1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes the TDAHE is governed by a single order parameter ($\eta$) and that correlation effects are captured by mean-field treatments, with DMFT confirming $Z$-independence.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare theoretical predictions of valley polarization $\eta(T)$ against experimental bounds, showing agreement in the 2D Ising critical fluctuation regime for $T_c$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the TDAHE phenomenon to proposing the Stoner instability mechanism. It then derives the mathematical dependence of both Hall responses on the valley polarization $\eta$ and the orbital g-factor, culminating in quantitative predictions for $T_c$ and layer scaling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A ``transdimensional'' anomalous Hall effect (TDAHE), where both in-plane ($\Bpar$) and out-of-plane magnetic fields produce hysteretic Hall signals, was recently observed in nine-layer rhombohedral graphite~\cite{Li2026Nature}. We present a microscopic theory attributing the TDAHE to a flat-band Stoner instability coupled to Peierls-phase gap modulation. The flat-band density of states satisfies the Stoner criterion $Uρ(\varepsilon_F) > 1$~\cite{Bultinck2020}, driving a spin-valley-locked ferromagnet whose valley polarization $η$ breaks time-reversal symmetry and generates an intrinsic anomalous Hall conductivity (AHC). The orbital $g$-factor $g_{\mathrm{orb}} = e d_0 v_F (N{-}1)/2 \propto (N{-}1)$ then lets $\Bpar$ modulate the gap, producing the transdimensional response $\propto η$. A self-consistent $2N$-band Hartree-Fock calculation yields complete valley polarization ($η\to 1$) below a mean-field transition $T_c^{\mathrm{MF}} \approx 2.2$~K, reduced by 2D-Ising critical fluctuations to the experimental $T_c \approx 1.6$~K, with $R_{xy} \approx 1.5$~k$Ω$. Because both Hall responses are carried by one order parameter $η$, they share a single $T_c$, as observed; $T_c$ is governed by the Stoner product $Uρ$ and is insensitive to the intervalley exchange, which only gates whether the valley-polarized phase forms, so the exchange strength is not a fitted parameter. Beyond reproducing $R_{xy}$, $T_c$, and the phase window, the theory predicts a sharp onset of valley polarization between $N = 7$ and $N = 9$, a symmetry selection rule fixing the crescent Fermi surface to the $m{=}1$ nematic channel, and a transdimensional-to-conventional Hall ratio $\sigmaPHE/\sigmaAHE^{\mathrm{tot}} = g_{\mathrm{orb}}\Bpar/m$ independent of $η$ and $U$. The $Z$-independence of the intrinsic AHC is verified within dynamical mean-field theory.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a solvable transformer model to study Chain-of-Thought state tracking using permutation composition. By applying mean-field theory, the authors derive differential equations for order parameters describing attention retrieval ($A$) and logic alignment ($R$). The results reveal a predictable, staged learning process leading to a sharp transition in performance, offering mechanistic insight into how complex reasoning emerges from simple component interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This provides a rigorous, physics-based framework to analyze emergent computational capabilities (like CoT) within neural networks by mapping learning dynamics onto established concepts from statistical mechanics and phase transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the training dynamics by which transformer models acquire the ability to perform multi-step, compositional state tracking (Chain-of-Thought). Specifically, it aims to understand how attention retrieval mechanisms and MLP logic modules co-develop during this process.

**Main result.** The learning process is characterized as staged: initially, the logic module learns a heuristic, followed by the attention mechanism locking onto the correct action, enabling efficient alignment of the MLP. This leads to a sharp, theoretically predictable transition in final rollout accuracy.

**Method.** The analysis employs a statistical-physics mean-field description, deriving coupled differential equations for macroscopic order parameters ($A$, $R$, and $S$) that govern the system's evolution over training time ($\alpha$).

**Model / system.** The model is a simplified, solvable one-block transformer trained on a permutation-chain task. It separates action retrieval (via RoPE attention) from state application (via a specialized MLP logic module). The task requires predicting states resulting from composing permutations.

**Key observables.** Attention order parameter ($A$), Teacher-matrix alignment ($R$), Off-target logic overlap ($S$), and final rollout accuracy ($\varrho_L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Training time ($\alpha$), sequence length ($L$), size of permutation set ($N$), and the coupling strength parameters derived from the mean-field ansatz.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a solvable, constrained architecture; key approximations include using a mean-field description for the loss function and assuming Gaussian distributions for logits to derive continuous dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of order parameters ($A, R, S$) over training time, showing agreement between simulated results and theoretical predictions derived from differential equations. Another figure tracks final rollout accuracy, predicting a sharp transition near convergence.

**Paper structure.** The paper first sets up the solvable transformer model for state tracking. It then derives mean-field dynamics by defining order parameters ($A, R, S$) based on attention and logic module overlaps. The core of the work involves solving coupled differential equations governing these parameters over training time ($\alpha$), culminating in a theoretical prediction for the final task accuracy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chain-of-thought generation can turn a multi-step computation into a sequence of locally checkable state updates, but the training dynamics by which transformers acquire such updates remain poorly understood. We study this question in a solvable setting: a simplified one-block transformer trained by supervised next-token prediction on state sequences generated by composing permutations. The architecture separates fixed-lag action retrieval, learned by RoPE attention, from a specialized MLP logic module that applies the retrieved permutation to the current state. Using a statistical-physics mean-field description, we derive dynamics for three order parameters measuring attention retrieval, teacher-matrix alignment, and off-target logic overlap. These equations quantitatively match simulations for the order parameters and, combined with a logit-distribution approximation, qualitatively predict the sharp transition in final rollout accuracy. The analysis reveals staged learning: the logic module first learns a mixed heuristic; attention then locks onto the relevant action, enabling efficient MLP alignment. Together, these results provide a controlled mechanistic account of how attention-based retrieval and MLP-based logic co-develop during chain-of-thought state tracking.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces specialized singular vector finite element basis functions designed to overcome the limitations of standard FEM when simulating electromagnetic fields near sharp geometric features common in superconducting qubits. By incorporating these additive singular terms into tetrahedral elements, the authors achieve highly accurate results for critical parameters like energy participation ratios with computational costs drastically lower than commercial benchmarks.

**Why it may be interesting.** The accurate simulation of energy loss mechanisms in superconducting qubits is crucial for understanding decoherence rates ($	ext{T}_1$, $	ext{T}_2$), which are central topics in quantum information science. The method provides a powerful, computationally efficient tool to analyze these fundamental physical limits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Traditional Finite Element Method (FEM) basis functions fail to accurately model electromagnetic field behavior near singular features, such as those found in superconducting qubit geometries.

**Main result.** The development of specialized, additive singular vector basis functions allows for simulating complex electromagnetic structures with massive computational speedups while maintaining high accuracy comparable to commercial solvers.

**Method.** The authors derive and implement novel singular basis functions (node-singular, edge-singular, rotational) specifically tailored for tetrahedral elements in 3D electromagnetics.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on simulating electromagnetic fields within superconducting quantum circuits, such as GCPW resonators and Xmon qubits, where energy loss near geometric singularities dictates qubit coherence times.

**Key observables.** Participation Ratios (PMS, PMA, PSA) quantifying energy distribution at interfaces; Quality factor (Q); Field magnitude along singular lines.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Singular function order ($s=1, 2, 3$); Frequency ($\omega$); Material permittivities ($\epsilon_i, \epsilon_s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation domain is assumed source-free and lossless for the primary derivations; higher-order terms require careful handling of continuity across element interfaces.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate field behavior comparisons (standard vs. singular functions) near wedges, show geometric associations of basis functions in tetrahedra, and compare calculated qubit parameters like capacitance and PMS against varying function orders.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the failure of standard FEM at singularities to developing node-singular, then edge-singular, and finally higher-order rotational basis functions. It validates these functions by applying them to calculate key superconducting qubit metrics (PRs, Q) and demonstrating massive computational efficiency gains over commercial tools.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electromagnetic finite element method (FEM) implementations using traditional basis functions struggle to accurately represent field behavior near singular features such as conducting wedges. To combat this, specialized singular basis functions have been introduced to directly model the singular fields in these regions, leading to substantially improved performance. While these efforts have been pursued extensively in 2D, few functions have been developed for 3D elements. In this work, we develop basis functions for this in tetrahedra. Unlike prior functions, these basis functions are additive, meaning they are included alongside the standard vector basis functions to achieve more robust performance. Further, these functions are designed to be adaptable to tetrahedra touching several unique singular features by using combinations of basis functions singular with respect to each node and edge in the element, making them applicable to highly complex geometries. Higher-order interpolatory versions of the basis functions for modeling singular behavior with greater accuracy are also provided. These basis functions lead to substantial improvements in accuracy relative to the standard basis functions, and allow otherwise expensive simulations to be performed at far lower costs. As an application example, we perform simulations to extract critical quantities for designing superconducting qubits that significantly depend on the behavior of singular fields. In Ansys HFSS, this took 21.27 hours and a peak memory usage of 6.23 TB with 800 processors available, while using our singular basis functions achieved comparable results in 196 seconds while using 27.24 GB of memory and only 16 processors. Due to these benefits, our singular basis functions could be applied to enable design optimization of electromagnetic geometries with dominantly singular behavior, such as superconducting qubits.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study uses advanced electronic structure calculations to map out the phase diagram of superconductivity in nickelates under strain. It finds that tensile strain stabilizes a favorable $s^{\pm}$-wave pairing state, while compressive strain suppresses it. The work provides crucial guidelines for engineering high-temperature superconductivity by controlling lattice strain and doping levels.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on cuprate/nickelate physics, the methodology of using RPA to predict unconventional superconductivity based on orbital nesting and external tuning parameters (strain) is highly relevant to understanding correlated electron systems in general.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how mechanical strain (compression vs. tension) affects superconductivity in 1313-La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$ nickelate thin films.

**Main result.** Superconductivity is predicted to emerge via a robust $s^{\pm}$-wave pairing state specifically under tensile strain, while compressive strain suppresses it. The superconducting state requires an optimally sized $\gamma$ Fermi pocket.

**Method.** The research employs Density Functional Theory (DFT) combined with the Random-Phase Approximation (RPA) applied to a tight-binding model of the nickelate layers.

**Model / system.** The system is 1313-La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$, modeled as an alternating Single-Layer (SL)/Trilayer (TL) heterostructure. Calculations focus on the TL subsystem, analyzing its electronic structure and superconducting pairing instability.

**Key observables.** Superconducting pairing strength ($\lambda$), gap structures ($g_\alpha(\mathbf{k})$), self-doping effects, and Fermi surface pocket sizes ($\sigma$ at $\Gamma$, $\gamma$ at M).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strain (compressive vs. tensile), electron/hole doping level ($n$), and the size of the $\gamma$ pocket.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The RPA calculation restricts pairing analysis to the TL subsystem, neglecting direct participation from the SL subsystem in the instability calculation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures and Fermi surfaces under different strain/doping conditions (LSAO vs. KTO). Gap structure plots show the predicted $s^{\pm}$-wave gap symmetry on tensile-strained films.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares results obtained from simulations on LSAO substrates (compressive strain) versus those on KTO substrates (tensile strain), analyzing how doping and strain tune the pairing mechanism towards an optimal $s^{\pm}$ state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by recent progress in the 1313-La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$ nickelate thin films (Nie et al., Nature {\bf 652}, 628 (2026)), we systematically investigate the effects of both compressive and tensile strain in this system. Self-doping effects between the single-layer (SL) and trilayer (TL) blocks are observed in our studies in both cases, but are most pronounced under tensile strain. We find that superconductivity is unlikely to emerge in the 1313 film on the LSAO substrate even with further hole doping. Remarkably, within the random-phase approximation, under {\it tensile} strain, a robust $s^{\pm}$-wave pairing state emerges in the TL subsystem with sign changes between the small electron-like $σ$ pocket at the $Γ$ point and the small hole-like $γ$ pocket at the M point. These pockets are connected by a wave vector close to $(π,π)$. Our calculations also suggest that superconductivity in 1313-LNO requires an optimally sized $γ$ pocket, because an oversized $γ$ pocket suppresses pairing formation. Overall, our results predict strain-dependent electronic reconstruction in 1313-La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$ and provide guiding principles for engineering superconductivity under ambient pressure conditions.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a Polynomial-Time Approximation Scheme (PTAS) for the minimum-weight decoding of 2D and higher-dimensional topological stabilizer codes. By adapting techniques from Euclidean geometry problems, it overcomes the NP-hardness barrier for this crucial task in fault-tolerant quantum computation. This provides a computationally efficient decoder that can approximate the true error correction weight to any desired accuracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful algorithmic tool for quantum error correction codes by transforming an intractable optimization problem into one solvable efficiently up to arbitrary precision $\epsilon$.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is finding an efficient algorithm for minimum-weight decoding of two-dimensional topological translationally invariant (2D TTI) stabilizer codes.

**Main result.** The authors prove that this NP-hard decoding problem admits a Polynomial-Time Approximation Scheme (PTAS), guaranteeing a solution within $(1+\epsilon)$ of the true minimum weight in polynomial time.

**Method.** The method adapts Arora's PTAS for Euclidean problems, employing recursive lattice dissection and dynamic programming over 'portals' to solve subproblems.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on 2D TTI stabilizer codes (like the toric code), which are fundamental models used in fault-tolerant quantum computation. The decoding process involves finding a minimum-weight error operator consistent with an observed syndrome.

**Key observables.** Error weight ($|e|$), approximation ratio ($(1+\epsilon)$), and the syndrome ($\sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The accuracy parameter $\epsilon$, the lattice size $L$, and the dimension $D$ (generalizing from 2D to higher dimensions).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on structural properties like the Patching Property and Rerouting Property, which allow errors to be localized and manipulated across recursively defined regions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the TTI code structure, the recursive partitioning of the lattice into squares with 'portals,' and the dynamic programming steps used to combine solutions from smaller subproblems.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the NP-hardness of exact decoding, then developing a PTAS based on Arora's technique. The core is proving structural theorems (Patching/Rerouting) that enable solving the problem via dynamic programming over recursively dissected regions, culminating in the general $D$-dimensional extension.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional topological translationally invariant (2D TTI) stabilizer codes lie at the heart of fault-tolerant quantum computation, but using them requires solving the decoding problem. Minimum-weight decoding of these codes was recently shown to be NP-hard, even in basic settings, such as the color code with Pauli $Z$ errors and the toric code with Pauli $X$, $Y$ and $Z$ errors. Here, we prove that minimum-weight decoding of 2D TTI codes nonetheless admits a polynomial-time approximation scheme (PTAS), i.e., for any constant $\varepsilon>0$, a recovery operator of weight within a multiplicative factor of $1+\varepsilon$ of the minimum can be found in polynomial time. Our approach builds on Arora's PTAS for Euclidean problems, such as the traveling salesman problem, and applies when decoding can be cast in terms of point-like excitations connected by string-like errors. It therefore extends beyond two dimensions, covering certain higher-dimensional topological codes and quantum memories, including the toric code with phenomenological or circuit-level noise.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper derives the height field theory for the 2D dimer model by mapping its partition function onto an exact Grassmann integral. Using bosonization techniques, it shows that this lattice problem maps precisely to a continuum description governed by a massless Dirac fermion, yielding explicit results for correlation functions in terms of the height field.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantum field theoretic derivation connecting a classical statistical mechanics model (dimers) to a fundamental continuum description (height field), which is highly relevant for understanding critical phenomena in 2D systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to derive a continuum height field theory for the two-dimensional classical dimer model by starting from an exact Grassmann-integral representation of its partition function.

**Main result.** The derivation successfully maps the lattice physics onto a massless Dirac fermion, which via bosonization yields the known height field theory, providing explicit expressions for dimer observables in terms of the continuum height field.

**Method.** The methodology involves using an exact Grassmann-integral representation, taking the continuum limit by expanding lattice variables into slowly varying fields, and employing bosonization techniques to relate fermionic determinants to bosonic correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The system is the classical dimer model on bipartite lattices (e.g., square or honeycomb). The partition function sums over all close-packed configurations of dimers, which are characterized by local flux densities ($\mathbf{B}_\ell$) and a valence-bond-solid order parameter ($\Psi_i$).

**Key observables.** Dimer correlation functions $\langle d_{\ell_1}d_{\ell_2} \cdots d_{\ell_n}angle$, the continuum height field $h(\mathbf{r})$, and related bosonic/fermionic correlators ($\Gamma(\mathbf{r})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is restricted to 2D bipartite lattices, and the derivation relies on assuming that source terms (like $\mathbf{a}$ and $M$) are slowly varying in the continuum limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The key assumptions involve taking the continuum limit for lattice variables ($\mathbf{a}_{ib} \propto \partial_\mu a$), relating dimer sums to Grassmann determinants, and assuming that only specific source terms (flux and VBS order) contribute to asymptotic correlations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the lattice structures (square/honeycomb), mapping dimer configurations to directed-loop patterns, and visualizing the resulting height field contours ($h_p$).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the Grassmann integral representation for the partition function, performing a continuum limit expansion using slowly varying fields ($\mathbf{a}, M$), relating this determinant structure to bosonic correlation functions via bosonization, and finally deriving the explicit form of the height field theory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The classical dimer model on bipartite lattices hosts a Coulomb phase, characterized by algebraic correlations and topological order. Its long-wavelength properties can be described by the fluctuations of a vector field with zero divergence, which, in two dimensions, is equivalent to a continuum height model. We show how this field theory can be derived constructively for both square and honeycomb lattices, starting from an exact representation of the dimer model in terms of Grassmann integrals. Taking the continuum limit gives a massless Dirac fermion in two-dimensional Euclidean space, which, using bosonization in the path integral representation, maps exactly to the well-known height field theory, incorporating the relationship between its boundary discontinuity (or "tilt") and the flux. By including source terms coupling to the flux density and the local valence-bond-solid order parameter, which we show are the only ones required to describe the asymptotic long-distance correlations, we derive expressions for the dimer observables in terms of the height.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper outlines a comprehensive fabless architecture necessary to commercialize superconducting quantum chips by mimicking the successful model of the classical semiconductor industry. It argues that moving beyond lab prototypes requires standardizing design through five key pillars: PDKs, parameterized cells, multiphysics simulation (SPICE-Q), automated EDA flows, and an IP market. Ultimately, it defines a path toward scalable, manufacturable quantum hardware by treating physical complexity as a set of verifiable software interfaces.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial engineering roadmap, framing quantum hardware development not just as a physics problem but as an industrial systems integration challenge that requires rigorous standardization analogous to mature classical semiconductor flows.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of transitioning superconducting quantum chip development from a laboratory-based, vertically integrated model to a scalable, standardized fabless-foundry industrial ecosystem.

**Main result.** Scalability requires establishing five key pillars—PDKs, PCells, SPICE-Q simulation, Q-EDA automation, and a Q-IP market—to create verifiable, reusable software and process interfaces.

**Method.** The authors propose an architectural framework by drawing analogies from the classical semiconductor industry to define necessary industrial standards for quantum hardware design flow.

**Model / system.** The physical system is superconducting quantum chips (e.g., Transmons). The model involves a complex, multi-physics Hamiltonian ($H_{SPICE-Q}$) whose performance depends on coupling geometry, material loss, and packaging constraints.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate, Q-IP reuse rate, cross-foundry process consistency, and the overall system validation maturity ($V_{	ext{validation}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** PDK maturity, foundry yield, cryogenic test throughput, and the boundaries of Quantum Intellectual Property (Q-IP) licensing.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The transition assumes that establishing rigorous, standardized interfaces can overcome the inherent physical complexity and non-ideal nature of quantum devices, allowing for 'constrained decoupling' from full process ownership.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the limitations of the current IDM model, then proposing a multi-pillar fabless architecture (PDKs $ightarrow$ PCells $ightarrow$ SPICE-Q $ightarrow$ Q-EDA $ightarrow$ Q-IP), and finally detailing the required continuous closed-loop feedback mechanisms for industrial maturity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper proposes a fabless quantum-chip design and production architecture for superconducting quantum computing, centered on the SPICE-Q multiphysics simulation framework. The proposed ecosystem connects process-certified quantum PDKs, parameterized device cells, traceable model cards, SPICE-Q physical modeling languages, unified Q-EDA flows, foundry sign-off rules, cryogenic test feedback, and reusable quantum IP. In this model, design firms do not merely outsource fabrication; they prepare verified tape-outs under standardized process constraints and calibrated physical models. Its economic value lies in reducing repetitive device debugging, process exploration, and low-level layout effort, while its feasibility depends on PDK maturity, foundry yield, cryogenic test throughput, model-prediction accuracy, data-feedback mechanisms, and IP licensing boundaries. We argue that superconducting quantum chips can move from the current largely vertically integrated development model toward a fabless-foundry ecosystem only when hardware design is supported by standardized, verifiable, and reusable software and process interfaces. The required pillars are certified PDKs, PCell-based parameterized design, SPICE-Q cross-physics simulation, end-to-end Q-EDA automation, and a tradable quantum-IP market. By adapting lessons from the classical semiconductor industry to quantum hardware, this framework defines a path toward scalable, manufacturable, and commercially reusable superconducting quantum-chip design.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a method to find the best possible input quantum state for estimating an unknown phase shift under covariant measurement constraints. By reformulating the problem as maximizing a quadratic form involving Toeplitz matrices derived from the cost function, the authors derive explicit formulas for the minimum achievable error bound. The results confirm Heisenberg scaling in specific cases, advancing the theory of quantum metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental theoretical tools for quantum metrology, specifically characterizing the ultimate limits of precision achievable in phase estimation by optimizing the resource state itself, which is crucial for designing practical quantum sensors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to optimize the input quantum state $|\psiangle$ used for phase estimation when measurements are restricted to covariant types.

**Main result.** The optimal input state coefficients are determined by the eigenvector corresponding to the largest eigenvalue of a Toeplitz matrix derived from the cost function. This leads to an explicit lower bound on the average estimation cost, which can achieve Heisenberg scaling in certain limits.

**Method.** The authors optimize the average cost functional over all possible input states, reducing the problem to maximizing a quadratic form involving the Fourier coefficients of the cost function using techniques related to Toeplitz matrices and variational principles.

**Model / system.** The system models single-mode quantum phase estimation ($	heta$) where the measurement process is constrained to be covariant. The analysis uses the Fock coefficients ($c_n = \langle\psi|nangle$) of the input state $|\psiangle$ and a cost function $W(	heta, 	ilde{	heta})$.

**Key observables.** The minimum average estimation cost ($ar{C}^*$), the optimal input state amplitudes ($|c_n|$), and the largest eigenvalue ($\lambda_{\max}$) of the associated Toeplitz matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The mean photon number (related to energy scaling), the structure of the even, $2\pi$-periodic cost function $W(	heta, 	ilde{	heta})$, and the asymptotic limit ($N 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the measurement is covariant and that the cost function has non-negative Fourier coefficients. The derivation relies on Holevo's framework for optimal measurements given a fixed state.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the results concern explicit formulas for $ar{C}^*$ and the structure of the optimal state vector derived from matrix properties.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon Holevo's result by optimizing over the input state. It derives necessary and sufficient conditions using quadratic forms related to Toeplitz matrices, establishes the relationship between the minimum cost and $\lambda_{\max}$, and concludes with explicit calculations for a specific sinusoidal cost function.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the optimization of input states for phase estimation under covariant measurements. Building on Holevo's framework, which provides the optimal covariant measurement for a fixed input state, we further optimize over the input state itself. For a general even $2π$-periodic cost function with non-negative Fourier coefficients, we derive a necessary and sufficient condition for the optimal input state: Its Fock coefficients are determined, up to arbitrary phases, by the eigenvector corresponding to the largest eigenvalue of a Toeplitz matrix defined by the cost function. This characterization yields an explicit expression for the attainable lower bound of the average cost under optimal covariant measurements and shows that this bound asymptotically approaches zero in the infinite-energy limit. For the specific cost function $W(θ,\tildeθ)=4\sin^2[(θ-\tildeθ)/2]$, we obtain the optimal input state and the corresponding minimum average cost in closed form, demonstrating Heisenberg scaling with respect to the mean photon number.

</details>

**Summary.** This work compares Ni 1s and Ni 2p photoelectron spectra across various La-based nickelates to probe their electronic structure. The authors argue that the deeper Ni 1s core level offers a clearer view of intrinsic correlations by avoiding spectral overlaps present in the more commonly used Ni 2p measurement. By combining experiment with DFT+DMFT calculations, they successfully map material differences onto changes in fundamental electronic parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed spectroscopic analysis of strongly correlated nickelates using core-level photoemission spectroscopy provides direct experimental constraints on the effective Hamiltonians and correlation strengths used in theoretical models like DMFT, which is highly relevant to understanding high-Tc superconductivity mechanisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the challenge of accurately determining the electronic structure of La-based nickelates by comparing core-level photoemission spectra from different Ni core levels.

**Main result.** The deep Ni 1s core level provides a cleaner view of intrinsic electronic excitations compared to the Ni 2p level, which suffers from spectral overlap with the La 3d states. This allows for distinguishing material variations within the Ruddlesden-Popper series.

**Method.** The research combines experimental Hard X-ray Photoelectron Spectroscopy (HAXPES) measurements with theoretical calculations using Density Functional Theory combined with Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DFT+DMFT).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Ruddlesden-Popper series of nickelates, including La3Ni2O7, Nd3Ni2O7, and LaNiO3. The analysis focuses on the electronic correlation effects within these layered perovskite structures.

**Key observables.** Core-level binding energies (Ni 1s vs Ni 2p), spectral line shapes, satellite features, and intensity ratios.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Charge transfer energy ($\Delta_{dp}$), hybridization strength ($V(\epsilon)$), and the specific stoichiometry of the nickelate layers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the Ni 1s spectrum is less contaminated by neighboring orbitals compared to the Ni 2p spectrum, allowing it to probe intrinsic electronic correlations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Ni 1s and Ni 2p spectra across different nickelates (e.g., Nd3Ni2O7 vs La3Ni2O7), demonstrating spectral overlaps in Ni 2p and showing material-dependent variations in the cleaner Ni 1s spectrum.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the spectroscopic limitations of the Ni 2p level due to overlap, then presenting evidence from Ni 1s measurements that it is a viable alternative. Finally, theoretical calculations are used to model how changes in electronic parameters ($\Delta_{dp}$, hybridization) account for observed spectral differences between related compounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a comparative Ni core level photoemission study of La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$, Nd$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$, and LaNiO$_3$ using both the Ni $2p$ and the Ni $1s$. We address the challenges in analyzing the widely investigated Ni $2p$ spectra arising from the substantial overlap in energy of the Ni $2p$ with the La $3d$. We show that on the other hand the deep Ni $1s$ core level does provide a clean view on the intrinsic electronic excitations and we highlight its potential to resolve detailed differences in the electronic structure within the strongly correlated Ruddlesden-Popper series La$_{n+1}$Ni$_n$O$_{3n+1}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a Split-Head Quantum GAN (SH-QGAN) to accelerate the discovery of novel crystalline materials. By separating the prediction of global lattice parameters from local atomic coordinates, the model leverages quantum circuits to achieve unparalleled structural diversity while maintaining high thermodynamic precision. The results demonstrate that combining quantum feature mapping with physics-informed architectural priors is a powerful paradigm for generative materials science.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter structure generation, the core methodology—using quantum circuits to enhance generative sampling in high-dimensional physical spaces—is highly relevant for developing ML models that respect underlying physical symmetries and constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Discovering novel, stable crystalline materials is limited by classical generative models' inability to explore vast 3D chemical space without mode collapse.

**Main result.** The SH-QGAN successfully generates structurally diverse candidates that exhibit superior thermodynamic stability and geometric validity compared to purely classical ablation models.

**Method.** A physics-informed Split-Head Quantum GAN (SH-QGAN) is employed, which decouples lattice parameter prediction from atomic coordinate generation using quantum circuits for enhanced exploration.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the Mg-Mn-O crystalline material space. The model uses an SH-QGAN architecture with a classical WGAN-GP discriminator to generate structures by predicting global cell parameters and local atomic coordinates separately.

**Key observables.** Thermodynamic stability (Energy Above Convex Hull, E_hull), Topological Validity Rate, Structural Similarity Index Measure (SSIM), and electronic bandgap (3.07 eV for the candidate).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The model is evaluated on a constrained core dataset of Mg-Mn-O structures; training involves specific learning rates and micro-batch sizes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that decoupling lattice bounds from atomic coordinates (Split-Head) allows the quantum circuit to independently provide necessary spatial diversity beyond classical architectural constraints.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SH-QGAN schematic, loss convergence over epochs, structural hit-rate comparisons against ablation models, and electronic band structures confirming insulating properties for generated candidates.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of generative materials discovery, details the SH-QGAN architecture (decoupling heads), presents training methodologies using WGAN-GP, evaluates performance by comparing it to a classical ablation model, and concludes with DFT validation of top candidates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The discovery of novel crystalline materials is a critical challenge in computational materials science, often limited by the spatial representation limitations and mode collapse typical of classical generative models. Traditionally, developing Quantum GANs for continuous 3D space is hindered by the limited capacity of near-term hardware. To overcome this, we adapt a physics-informed "split-head" architecture right from the quantum trunk to explicitly decouple macroscopic lattice bounds from microscopic atomic coordinates, significantly maximizing resource efficiency. This study disentangles the contributions of quantum circuits from these architectural priors by evaluating a Split-Head Quantum Generative Adversarial Network against an architecture-matched classical ablation model. Evaluated on the highly constrained Mg-Mn-O system, the results reveal a highly nuanced performance dichotomy between the advanced models. The architecture-matched classical ablation model demonstrated superior thermodynamic precision. Conversely, the integration of quantum circuits in the SH-QGAN drove unparalleled structural breadth and latent space exploration, more than doubling the ablation's geometric validity and successfully generating novel, metastable candidates converging on the Mg2MnO4 stoichiometry. These findings clarify that while architectural separation of cell and atom generation drives strict thermodynamic precision, quantum feature mapping independently provides the spatial diversity necessary to overcome mode collapse. Both mechanisms offer distinct, complementary enhancements for the generative discovery of advanced materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves that one can find an error-set in the colour code whose weight approximates the true minimum weight decoding within an arbitrary factor $(1+\epsilon)$ using only polynomial time. This result demonstrates that approximate quantum decoding is computationally feasible, despite the underlying exact problem being NP-hard.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the computational complexity limits of quantum error correction, providing a theoretical breakthrough by showing that approximate decoding is tractable even when exact decoding is NP-hard.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining whether approximate minimum weight decoding for quantum error-correcting codes, specifically the colour code, can be achieved in polynomial time.

**Main result.** The authors prove that it is possible to find an error-set whose weight is within $(1+\epsilon)$ of the true minimum weight decoding in polynomial time, showing approximate decoding is not NP-hard.

**Method.** The approach adapts techniques from solving Euclidean optimization problems (like Arora's work), using dynamic programming and imposing structural constraints (portal respecting) to reduce the problem complexity iteratively.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the colour code, defined on an infinite hexagonal lattice or a finite region thereof. It is used as a quantum error-correcting code for detecting errors (X-errors) that generate a measurable syndrome.

**Key observables.** Syndrome ($S$), minimum weight decoding ($	ext{OPT}$), and the resulting approximate error-set weight $|E|$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Approximation factor $\epsilon$, code distance $d$, lattice size parameters $L$ and $M$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial polynomial algorithm derived is noted to be impractically large in its complexity, requiring significant theoretical leaps for practical implementation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the colour code structure on lattices (hexagonal/triangular), how errors trigger checks based on location (bulk/boundary), and geometric decompositions using rhombuses or hexagons to constrain error sets.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first tackling the general approximation problem, then applying techniques involving lattice decomposition (rhombus tiling) and imposing structural constraints ('portal respecting') via dynamic programming. Finally, it refines this process for specific bounded regions of the code.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently we showed that minimum weight decoding in the (6.6.6 planar) colour code is NP-hard. However, it remained an open question as to whether it was possible to approximate the minimum weight decoding arbitrarily closely in polynomial time. In this paper we prove that it is possible: for any $\varepsilon>0$ there is an polynomial time algorithm that, given a syndrome, can find an error-set generating that syndrome whose weight is at most $1+\varepsilon$ times the weight of the minimum weight decoding. As a consequence we see that, for any $\varepsilon>0$, there is a polynomial time algorithm that can correct all errors of weight up to $(1-\varepsilon)d/2$ in the distance $d$ colour code (so almost up to the theoretical $d/2$ limit).   The polynomial we give is impractically large, but it does open the door for sensible polynomial time algorithms that approximate minimum weight decoding and, in particular, shows that approximate decoding is not NP-hard.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a theoretically optimal method (GPF) for synthesizing diagonal unitary gates. Crucially, it proves that this optimal complexity is maintained when mapping the abstract circuit onto a physically constrained two-dimensional nearest-neighbor grid. This provides essential, closed-form resource estimates that overcome the limitations of heuristic compilation methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, analytically derived resource estimates for complex quantum operations on realistic hardware architectures, directly addressing the gap between theoretical circuit design and physical implementation constraints relevant to AMO platforms like trapped ions or superconducting qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing an asymptotically optimal circuit depth for synthesizing and compiling diagonal unitary operations on quantum hardware constrained by nearest-neighbor connectivity.

**Main result.** The authors show that the asymptotically optimal depth, $O(2^n/n)$, achieved in all-to-all connectivity using a Gray-Path Framework (GPF), is preserved when compiled onto a two-dimensional nearest-neighbor grid. Furthermore, they detail space-time trade-offs to reduce this depth by factors of $k$.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical circuit synthesis (using recursive decomposition into high/low subspaces) with rigorous compilation techniques that map logical connectivity requirements onto physical 2D lattice movements using offset states and SWAP layers.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum circuits realizing $n$-qubit diagonal unitaries, $\Lambda_n(	heta)$. The target hardware is modeled as a two-dimensional nearest-neighbor grid, with extensions to linear chains. The synthesis relies on the Gray-Path Framework (GPF) structure.

**Key observables.** Circuit depth ($R_z$ and CNOT layers), gate count, and asymptotic complexity bounds ($\Theta(2^n/n)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n$), the grid topology (2D nearest-neighbor vs. all-to-all), and the space-time trade-off factor ($k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** General-purpose compilers relying on heuristic search are intractable at scale; the analysis assumes the existence of a low-jump Gray code sequence for optimal routing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the recursive structure of GPF, show the mapping onto the two-row grid using offset states (e.g., Fig. 8), and plot normalized depth/gate count convergence curves demonstrating asymptotic optimality compared to search-based routers.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the optimal synthesis complexity via GPF in all-to-all connectivity, then details the mapping of this structure onto a two-row nearest-neighbor grid using offset states and SWAP layers. Finally, it analyzes depth-space trade-offs by replicating phase layers across additional rows.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Diagonal unitaries are a fundamental but resource-intensive class of quantum operations, arising as the phase separators of QAOA and the time-evolution blocks of Hamiltonian simulation. Under all-to-all connectivity their optimal depth is established, but on nearest-neighbor hardware general-purpose compilers fall back on heuristic search, which yields no analyzable cost bound and becomes intractable at the very sizes where depth is the bottleneck. We address synthesis and compilation jointly. On the synthesis side, we develop a Gray-Path Framework (GPF) that realizes any $n$-qubit diagonal unitary in asymptotically optimal $R_z$ and CNOT depth $O(2^n/n)$ without ancillas. Our main result is that compiling GPF onto a two-dimensional nearest-neighbor grid preserves this optimality: routing adds depth $Θ(2^n/n)$ and gate count $Θ(2^n)$. Because GPF fixes its entire interaction structure in advance, routing reduces to scheduling a known sequence, with no heuristic search. We give the construction both with and without ancillas: the ancilla-free, cost-optimized layout is a two-row grid, and a $2k$-row layout introduces a space--time tradeoff that cuts depth by $1/k$ while remaining asymptotically optimal for the enlarged register; both are deterministic and analyzed in closed form. The same complexity is also attained on a linear nearest-neighbor chain, so the preservation is topology-independent, holding on any architecture that contains such a chain. All routing bounds are closed-form, giving the concrete resource estimates that heuristic compilers cannot provide at scale.

</details>

**Authors:** Arda Aydin, Itzhak Tamo, Alexander Barg  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17268v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a generalized group-theoretic construction method for quantum LDPC codes by utilizing the action of a group on its cosets. This framework expands beyond previous constructions, yielding new high-performance codes whose parameters and noise thresholds are analyzed. The authors also provide systematic methods to build efficient syndrome extraction circuits for these novel codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful algebraic tool (group theory) to generate structured quantum codes, which is crucial for designing hardware-efficient error correction protocols in superconducting or trapped-ion platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to construct novel quantum Low-Density Parity-Check (LDPC) codes that surpass the limitations of existing constructions, particularly those based on Two-Block Group Algebra (2BGA) codes.

**Main result.** A new coset-based construction method is introduced using group actions on cosets, yielding several concrete examples of high-performing quantum LDPC codes with parameters like [[48, 8, 6]].

**Method.** The authors develop a general framework based on the action of a group G on the cosets of its subgroup H to define parity-check matrices for CSS quantum codes.

**Model / system.** The model is theoretical: constructing fault-tolerant quantum error correction codes, specifically two-block CSS quantum LDPC codes defined by group algebra actions $Q^H_G(a, b)$.

**Key observables.** Code parameters $[[n, k, d]]$, achievable syndrome extraction circuit depth ($w+2$), and noise thresholds ($\approx 0.65\%$ for weight-6 family).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Group structures $(G, H)$, stabilizer weights ($w_a, w_b$), code length $n=2[G:H]$, and thickness $	heta$ of the Tanner graph.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing planarity properties for subgraphs related to syndrome extraction cycles and assuming specific structural constraints on group elements to simplify the search space.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate relationships between various quantum LDPC codes (e.g., BB Code, ZSZ Code) and detail the structure of the wheel graph used in syndrome extraction analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from introducing the general coset-based construction to deriving mathematical properties like thickness bounds using planarity arguments, systematically searching for new code examples, and finally detailing the circuit implementation for syndrome extraction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Generalizing the construction of two-block group algebra (2BGA) codes, we introduce a family of two-block quantum LDPC codes constructed using the action of a group on the cosets of its subgroup. This replaces the regular group actions of the earlier two-block constructions and significantly expands the search space, yielding new quantum LDPC codes outside the 2BGA family. Through a computer search, we identify several new quantum LDPC codes, including weight-6 codes with parameters $[[48,8,6]]$, $[[96,8,10]]$, and $[[224,12,16]]$, as well as weight-8 codes with parameters $[[84,16,8]]$, $[[112,16,10]]$, $[[128,16,12]]$, and $[[168,16,15]]$. Furthermore, we introduce a maximally packed syndrome extraction schedule of depth $w+2$, including initialization and measurement steps, for any code with a maximum stabilizer weight of $w$ from our family. Under a standard circuit-level noise model, our codes, when decoded using BP-OSD, perform competitively with BB codes, achieving thresholds of $\approx0.65\%$ for the weight-6 family and $\approx0.35\%$ for the weight-8 family. Finally, we introduce a group-theoretic framework to generate sequences of graph-based covers of 2BGA codes, recovering and extending recent results on code constructions of this type.

</details>

### [Canonical regularization of the stationary Coulomb problem and an Aufbau-like spectral ordering](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17359v1)

**Authors:** Anand Aruna Kumar  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17359v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2606.17359_figures/2606.17359_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> Figure 2: Comparison of the degenerate Rydberg energies Enr with the shifted regularized spectrum Enr,l along the Aufbau-ordered sequence. The upper panel shows the full displayed range, while the lower panel expands the higher-state region where the energy separation is smaller.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the hydrogen atom using a regularized de Broglie-Bohm quantum mechanical framework. By imposing canonical constraints, the authors modify the standard radial and angular equations, leading to solutions involving generalized special functions with non-integral parameters. This regularization mathematically forces the energy spectrum to exhibit an Aufbau-like ordering for higher angular momentum states, lifting the inherent Coulomb degeneracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is theoretically interesting because it uses advanced mathematical techniques (de Broglie-Bohm formalism, canonical regularization) applied to a fundamental physical problem (Coulomb potential) to derive an emergent structure (Aufbau principle), providing a purely mathematical mechanism for spectral splitting.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate whether canonical regularization of the stationary Coulomb problem can intrinsically generate an Aufbau/Madelung spectral ordering, thereby lifting the inherent degeneracy found in the standard hydrogen atom model.

**Main result.** The regularized spectrum yields a sequence that follows the Aufbau/Madelung order for higher angular momentum states, while the $l=0$ sector retains the standard degenerate Rydberg sequence.

**Method.** The authors apply canonical regularization constraints to the de Broglie-Bohm representation of the Coulomb problem, leading to modified Sturm-Liouville equations solved using generalized special functions.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is the one-center stationary Coulomb potential (hydrogen atom). The mathematical framework involves decomposing the wavefunction into amplitude and phase components within a regularized de Broglie-Bohm representation.

**Key observables.** The energy eigenvalues $E_{n,r,\lambda}$ are key observables; the spectral ordering itself is the primary result.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regularization introduces non-integral parameters into the associated Laguerre, Legendre, and Bessel functions. The minimum labeling scheme $\lambda = l - 1/2$ is crucial for comparison.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The treatment is explicitly limited to an analytical examination of spectral ordering in a regularized one-center problem, not a replacement for full many-electron atomic structure theory (which requires considering screening and correlation).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the energy $E_{n_r,\lambda}$ vs $\lambda$, showing symmetry around $\lambda = -1/2$ and mapping this minimum branch to the s-sector. Figure 2 compares the degenerate Rydberg energies with the shifted regularized spectrum, demonstrating the Aufbau order.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the mathematical framework by applying canonical regularization to the de Broglie-Bohm equations for the Coulomb problem. It then solves the resulting separated radial, orbital, and axial differential equations using generalized special functions. Finally, it analyzes the energy quantization condition derived from these solutions to map out the spectral ordering.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The stationary hydrogen atom has Coulomb degeneracy across orbital levels, whereas the Aufbau/Madelung ordering is an empirical, many-electron rule established in atomic physics. We examine the hydrogen atom through a regularized de Broglie--Bohm representation, in which stationary amplitude current constraints generate separable Sturm--Liouville branches. In this formulation, the radial, orbital, and magnetic sectors acquire canonical Langer-like inverse square corrections. The modified boundary value problems allow analytical solutions and produce a hydrogen-like spectrum with regularized radial and angular indices. Consequently, radial Coulomb quantization acquires an orbital dependent shift, lifting the Coulomb degeneracy and producing a spectral ordering that follows the Aufbau/Madelung sequence.   On this basis, we construct the ordering of the regularized de Broglie--Bohm states and show that the spectral structure retains the standard degenerate Rydberg sequence in the l=0 sector. The separated amplitudes are represented by generalized special function branches, including the associated Laguerre, Legendre, and Bessel functions with non-integral parameters arising from regularized separation. Therefore, the treatment is intended as an analytical examination of spectral ordering in a regularized one center Coulomb problem rather than as a replacement for the many electron atomic structure theory.   Keywords: de Broglie--Bohm representation; Coulomb spectrum; canonical regularization; Langer correction; Sturm--Liouville equations; Aufbau principle; Madelung ordering; associated Legendre functions; associated Laguerre functions; Bessel functions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper studies permutation spin models on Cayley trees by applying the cavity method. It uses representation theory to diagonalize the system's transfer operator, yielding analytic criteria for phase transitions based on character eigenvalue ratios. The results allow researchers to predict critical points in these complex statistical mechanical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, mathematically rigorous framework (using group representation theory) to analyze phase transitions in complex lattice/tree structures that are far beyond simple mean-field treatments, connecting statistical mechanics to advanced algebraic methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research analyzes permutation statistical models defined on a Cayley tree structure to determine their critical points and stability regimes.

**Main result.** The linear stability of the uniform paramagnetic solution is controlled by character eigenvalue ratios derived from diagonalizing the edge convolution operator. The method provides analytic criteria for phase transitions in these complex spin systems.

**Method.** The cavity method, combined with non-commutative Fourier transforms using irreducible representations of the symmetric group $S_n$, is employed to linearize the homogeneous recursion equation around the paramagnetic state.

**Model / system.** The model is a permutation spin system where variables take values in the symmetric group $S_n$. The interaction weights are assumed to be class functions on $S_n$, leading to an edge convolution operator that can be diagonalized in representation space.

**Key observables.** Local stability condition derived from character eigenvalue ratios ($\omega_\lambda / \omega_{[n]}$), and order parameters like Potts-like magnetization ($m_{id}(\eta)$) and standard-representation magnetization ($m_{	ext{std}}(\eta)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coordination number $z$ of the Cayley tree, the size of the group $n$, and the coupling constants embedded in the weight function $W(g)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The weights are assumed to be class functions on $S_n$. The analysis relies on approximating bulk critical behavior using the Bethe/Cayley-tree approximation, which may fail for entanglement transitions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates how the magnetization of the cavity fixed point evolves as a function of inverse temperature $eta$ for various models, showing distinct flow regimes relative to the analytically derived instability points.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first setting up the model and applying the cavity method. It then uses representation theory to diagonalize the transfer operator, deriving stability criteria based on eigenvalue ratios. Finally, it verifies these analytic results through direct numerical iteration of the cavity equations for specific models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Motivated by permutation statistical models arising in random tensor networks, we study permutation models on a Cayley tree whose variables take values in the symmetric group $\Sn$. The pair interaction is assumed to depend only on the cycle type of the relative permutation. Then the Boltzmann weight is written as a class function on $\Sn$. This property diagonalizes the edge convolution operator in irreducible representation sectors. As a result, the linear stability of the uniform paramagnetic cavity solution is controlled by the character eigenvalue ratios. For cycle-factorized weights, these eigenvalues can be expressed as specializations of Schur functions. We derive the instability criteria and also verify their validity by comparison with direct numerical iterations of the cavity equation.

</details>

**Authors:** Shobhit Bhatnagar, Michele Pacenti, Nithin Raveendran, David Declercq, Bane Vasić  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.18240v1>

</div>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced decoding algorithms, notably impulse decoding, for Quantum LDPC codes by establishing a novel link: quantum degeneracy is equivalent to classical code shortening. The proposed methods significantly improve error correction performance and reduce complexity compared to prior state-of-the-art techniques under realistic noise models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QEC theory, the rigorous mathematical connection drawn between quantum degeneracy and classical coding concepts like 'shortening' provides deep insights into the underlying algebraic structure of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses quantum degeneracy in Quantum Error Correction (QEC) within stabilizer codes, which lacks a clear classical coding-theoretic interpretation.

**Main result.** It establishes that quantum degeneracy is mathematically equivalent to the classical operation of code shortening performed at the decoder. A novel decoding scheme called impulse decoding significantly outperforms existing methods under various noise conditions.

**Method.** The authors develop and compare multiple parallel decoding schemes, including impulse decoding and residual-error decoding, by leveraging the theoretical link between quantum degeneracy and classical code shortening.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Quantum Low-Density Parity-Check (QLDPC) codes constructed using the stabilizer formalism from pairs of classical linear codes (CSS codes). Performance is tested using specific examples like the [[288, 12, 18]] BB code.

**Key observables.** Failure rate vs. physical error rate; logical error rates; decoding latency; and computational complexity relative to existing decoders.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rate ($p$); number of parallel decoders ($N$); maximum residual error rounds ($R$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that restricting the search space via code shortening (e.g., fixing $\hat{e}_1=0$) is sufficient to maintain correctness while reducing complexity.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare impulse decoding performance against Belief Propagation variants, showing superior error correction capability; they also illustrate how limiting variable node shortening to a subset maintains high performance.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical link between degeneracy and code shortening. It then introduces Impulse Decoding as a parallel scheme based on this insight. Finally, it presents advanced variations combining impulse decoding with residual error correction for improved noise resilience.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction is essential for building scalable quantum computers. Within the stabilizer formalism, the Calderbank-Shor-Steane framework constructs quantum codes from pairs of classical linear codes. A distinctive feature in this setting is degeneracy, where multiple equivalent error estimates exist-a phenomenon that has no classical counterpart, and the lack of a meaningful classical coding-theoretic interpretation of which has remained a gap in the literature. In this paper, we demonstrate that degeneracy is closely related to the classical operation of shortening of a linear block code. Interestingly, the shortening here takes place at the decoder rather than at the encoder. Leveraging this insight, we present a parallel decoding scheme for quantum low-density parity-check codes, which we term impulse decoding, that significantly outperforms belief propagation with ordered statistics decoding, as well as several other existing techniques, under both code-capacity and circuit-level noise, with significantly lesser complexity. We then present another algorithm based on decoding of residual errors, which when combined with impulse decoding achieves further performance improvement under circuit-level noise.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work develops gauge-invariant criteria to distinguish and relate center-vortex condensation and monopole condensation in $(3+1)$D Yang-Mills theory using twisted partition functions on specific manifolds. The core achievement is proving that these two distinct topological phenomena are mathematically linked, suggesting a unified understanding of confinement mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly specialized, the rigorous mathematical framework connecting different types of topological defects (vortices vs. monopoles) via shared TQFT invariants is conceptually rich for understanding emergent order parameters in quantum field theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish gauge-invariant criteria for identifying center-vortex condensation and monopole condensation in $(3+1)$ dimensional Yang-Mills theories.

**Main result.** It proves a deep relationship showing that the gapped phase exhibiting center-vortex condensation necessarily implies the presence of monopole condensation, linking these two confinement mechanisms.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on using $\mathbb{Z}_N$-symmetry twisted partition functions calculated on specific 4-manifolds like the torus ($T^4$) and lens spaces ($L(qN; 1) 	imes S^1$).

**Model / system.** The primary model is SU(N) Yang-Mills theory, often studied in the context of an adjoint Higgs phase. The mathematical tools involve topological field theories (TQFTs) and analyzing partition function ratios on compactified spaces.

**Key observables.** The ratio of twisted partition functions $\mathcal{Z}[B]/\mathcal{Z}[0]$ serves as the diagnostic for condensation; other observables include Wilson loops and inter-quark potentials.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The gauge group rank $N$, the background flux parameters ($q, N$), and the assumption of a non-zero mass gap (gapped phase).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include locality, finiteness of the relevant Hilbert space dimension in the TQFT limit, and the existence of a mass gap for the equivalence theorems to hold.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the structure suggests mathematical derivations involving manifold decompositions (e.g., $M^4 = N_1^4 \cup_{Y^3} N_2^4$) and topological maps like Dehn twists.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first proposing criteria using twisted partition functions on $T^4$ and $L(qN; 1) 	imes S^1$. It then rigorously proves the equivalence between weak/strong condensation for both vortices and monopoles, culminating in topological calculations involving Bockstein maps on lens spaces.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose the gauge-invariant criteria of center-vortex condensation and monopole condensation using the $\mathbb{Z}_N^{[1]}$-symmetry twisted partition functions: The torus twisted partition function characterizes the center-vortex condensation, and the lens-space twisted partition function characterizes the monopole condensation. To justify our proposal, we study how these twisted partition functions behave in the adjoint Higgs phase and show that their leading nontrivial contributions come from the center vortex and monopole, respectively. Using the techniques of topological field theories, we uncover the relation between the center-vortex and monopole condensations, and in particular, we prove that the gapped phase with the center-vortex condensation necessarily shows the monopole condensation, too. We then study a center-vortex model with monopoles as an illustrative example, and the higher-charge monopole condensation gives an example of the symmetry fractionalization, which goes beyond the conventional Wilson-'t Hooft classification.

</details>
