**Summary.** The paper proposes Quantum Mechanics in Configuration Space (QMCS) as an alternative formalism to improve the conceptual link between QM and CM. By quantizing based on Newtonian mechanics using position-velocity states, QMCS supports independent observables like velocity ($\hat{v}$), which is often lost in standard canonical quantization. This suggests a more physically transparent quantum theory derived from classical principles.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to foundational theoretical physics, particularly concerning the precise mathematical structure required to bridge classical intuition (like velocity) into a fully consistent quantum description beyond standard canonical quantization rules.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses enhancing the conceptual continuity between quantum mechanics (QM) and classical mechanics (CM) by proposing an alternative formalism: Quantum Mechanics in Configuration Space (QMCS). It aims to resolve inconsistencies arising from standard canonical quantization.

**Main result.** QMCS, which quantizes based on Newtonian mechanics using position-velocity states $|x, v\rangle$, provides a framework that maintains strong conceptual continuity with classical dynamics and supports independent observables for velocity ($\hat{v}$) and acceleration ($\hat{a}$).

**Method.** The authors compare three formulations of CM (Newtonian, Lagrangian, Hamiltonian) to determine the most physically transparent quantization procedure. They then derive the quantum dynamics using a basis built on $(x, v)$ states.

**Model / system.** The model is a single mechanical particle moving in free space along one dimension ($x$). The formalism compares standard QM (based on canonical commutation relations) against QMCS (based on Newtonian mechanics).

**Key observables.** Position ($\hat{x}$), velocity ($\hat{v}$), momentum ($\hat{p}$), and acceleration ($\hat{a}$) are key observables, with the paper deriving new commutation relations like $[\hat{v}, \hat{a}] = i\hbar$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fundamental constant $\hbar$ governs the quantum corrections; the analysis focuses on the free space limit ($V(x)=0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that Newtonian mechanics provides the most physically perspicuous starting point for quantization. It also assumes QMCS can be compatible with various measurement interpretations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the conceptual relationship between different CM formulations and show how QMCS maintains a stronger link to classical dynamics compared to standard QM approaches.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem by comparing CM frameworks (Newtonian vs. Hamiltonian). It then introduces QMCS using the $(x, v)$ basis states. Finally, it derives the quantum dynamics for free space and contrasts its results with standard QM predictions regarding observable consistency.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

To enhance the way in which wave-particle duality is implemented in the modelling of quantum mechanical systems, Bukhari et al. [New J. Phys. 27, 084501 (2025)] recently introduced an alternative approach to quantum mechanics, namely quantum mechanics in configuration space. This formalism is based on a physically motivated quantisation of Newtonian mechanics and promotes the classical position-velocity states (x,v) to pairwise distinguishable quantum states. The resulting |x,v> states form the basis of the Hilbert space of individual quantum mechanical particles and evolve along classical trajectories. In this paper, we consider the modelling of a mechanical particle in free space and put quantum mechanics in configuration space into context. It is shown that this formalism increases the continuity between quantum and classical mechanics by avoiding a conceptual inconsistency associated with the definition of momentum in canonical quantisation. In addition, we emphasise that standard quantum mechanics and quantum mechanics in configuration space are based on two distinct formulations of classical mechanics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using a modified honeycomb microcavity array to create a platform exhibiting unpaired Dirac cones. By analyzing the nonlinear dynamics under resonant pumping, the authors predict and demonstrate the existence of stable, unidirectional dissipative edge solitons. This work advances topological photonics by showing robust energy localization in an inherently lossy system.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges topological physics with nonlinear optics/photonics by demonstrating robust, non-dissipative-like behavior (solitons) in a fundamentally dissipative quantum system, which is highly relevant for engineered light-matter interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates topological edge states emerging from a nonlinear microcavity system that supports unpaired Dirac cones, an unusual spectral feature.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate the existence of stable, unidirectional dissipative edge solitons circulating indefinitely along the periphery of the structure, which is a first example in this context.

**Method.** The study combines linear analysis using Bloch waves and plane-wave expansion with nonlinear time evolution simulations solved via Newton iteration for dissipative states.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as an array of microcavity pillars (fissioned honeycomb structure) supporting exciton-polaritons, governed by coupled Gross-Pitaevskii type equations incorporating spin-orbit coupling and external fields.

**Key observables.** Unpaired Dirac cones in the band structure, unidirectional edge states bypassing corners, and stable localized dissipative edge solitons.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spin-orbit coupling strength (beta), polariton loss rate (gamma), and resonant pumping detuning ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes specific parameter regimes where unpaired Dirac cones appear, and the soliton stability is linked to the presence of fissioned sites.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the linear band structure showing unpaired Dirac cones (Fig. 1), density distributions of bulk vs. edge states (Figs. 4b-d), and time evolution confirming stable circulation of the topological edge soliton (Fig. 6).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the unusual spectral topology (unpaired Dirac cones) in the linear regime, to analyzing the emergence of topologically protected edge states, and finally investigating the nonlinear dynamics leading to bistability and persistent dissipative solitons.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Among the most intriguing properties of honeycomb lattices is the presence of Dirac points that typically emerge in pairs, which can be destroyed by physical effects breaking certain symmetries of the system and leading to nontrivial band topology. We propose a nonlinear microcavity system supporting condensate of exciton-polaritons, where simultaneous breakup of inversion and time-reversal symmetries results in unusual spectrum with unpaired Dirac cones, profoundly affecting the properties of unidirectional edge states. Realized as an array of microcavity pillars, the inversion symmetry is broken by fission of pillar belonging to one of sublattices of honeycomb array into three pillars, while time-reversal symmetry is broken due to interplay of Zeeman splitting in the external magnetic field and spin-orbit coupling. Despite the absence of complete spectral gap, unidirectional edge states may still emerge that can circumvent array corners. Resonant optical pumping leads to reach bistability effects and allow selective excitation of the edge states. We obtain first example of stable localized dissipative edge soliton that circulates along the periphery of insulator over indefinitely long times without radiation. Our results suggest a new platform for nonlinear topological photonics and reveal nontrivial interplay between unpaired Dirac cones and nonlinear effects.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the observation of strongly coupled Moiré exciton and trion polaritons in semiconductor heterobilayers within an optical microcavity. By combining theory and experiment, the authors reveal a striking non-monotonic nonlinear response to excitation density. The findings highlight large group velocities and unprecedentedly large second-order nonlinearities controllable via doping.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it deals with cavity quantum electrodynamics (Cavity QED) in solid-state systems, specifically exploring strong light-matter coupling and nonlinear dynamics mediated by excitons/trions—a core topic in AMO physics and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the highly nonlinear optical response of strongly coupled Moiré exciton and trion polaritons in heterobilayers. A key focus is understanding the non-monotonic energy shifts and oscillator strength variations as a function of excitation density.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that the system exhibits strong coupling, leading to lower polariton branches with non-monotonic energy shifts and exceptionally large second-order nonlinearities ($\sim 10^2 \mu	ext{m}$ diffusion length).

**Method.** The work combines theoretical modeling using rate equations and Hamiltonian diagonalization with experimental optical measurements (PL spectroscopy) performed at cryogenic temperatures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an n-doped $	ext{MoSe}_2/	ext{WS}_2$ heterobilayer placed inside a metal-DBR microcavity. The model incorporates coupled exciton ($	ext{X}$) and trion ($	ext{X}^{-2}$) resonances, utilizing concepts like Lindhard screening to describe carrier interactions.

**Key observables.** Nonlinear dependence of oscillator strengths on polariton density; non-monotonic energy shift in the lower polariton branch; nominal diffusion length ($L_D$) for trion polaritons.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cryogenic temperature (5 K); initial dopant electron concentration ($n_e$); coupling constants ($\Omega$); screening coefficients ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes constant dephasing of 0D excitons when fixing the cavity resonance energy during density variation studies.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate device geometry, temperature-dependent PL spectra identifying exciton/trion resonances, and spatio-spectra showing extended diffusion tails near trion polariton energies.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical platform (heterobilayers in microcavities) to modeling the coupled dynamics using rate equations. It then analyzes density dependence via nonlinear shifts and concludes by quantifying large-scale spatial transport properties of the resulting polaritons.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Moiré multi-layers of transition metal dichalcogenides have been shown to exhibit optical responses that are endowed with a richness that is absent in single monolayers. Much of this can be attributed to the Moiré superlattice that modulates the electronic landscape of these heterostructures. Strongly coupled layer-hybridized excitons in MoSe2/WS2 heterobilayers have been shown to exhibit enhanced optical nonlinearities. In this work we strongly couple layer hybridized excitons and trions in n-doped $\text{MoSe}_2 / \text{WS}_2$ heterobilayers inside an optical microcavity. We find that the additional Lindhard screening from dopant electrons and the formation of trions result in a strikingly non-monotonic nonlinear response. The absence of electron capture in the Moiré superlattice plays a crucial role, promising very large second-order nonlinearities. In this work, trion polaritons manifest as high velocity hot polaritons, reaching nominal diffusion lengths approaching 100 microns.

</details>

The insets demonstrate Type-1 and Type-2 SRRs. (b) The setup including the electric dipole transmitting antenna and E-field probe receiving antenna that is moved by a spatial scanner. (c)-(e) Experimentally mea- sured distributions of the transmission coefficient |S21|, which characterizes the near-field distribution of the electric field component Ez, corresponding to (c) bulk, (d) edge, and (e) corner states. Each distribution is normalized to its maxi- mum.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates how to build a functional radio-frequency platform for studying Higher-Order Topological Insulators (HOTIs) using readily available printed circuit board technology. By engineering metasurfaces with split-ring resonators, the authors successfully observe both edge and corner localized electromagnetic states at microwave frequencies. This provides an accessible and scalable route for future experimental studies of topological physics in engineered media.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on microwave photonics rather than fundamental quantum mechanics, the concept of realizing topologically protected boundary modes using engineered artificial lattices is highly relevant to open quantum systems and photonic quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to create a scalable, reconfigurable radio-frequency (RF) platform that realizes the physics of photonic Higher-Order Topological Insulators (HOTIs). This overcomes limitations associated with expensive optical implementations.

**Main result.** The proposed metasurface, built from split-ring resonators (SRRs), successfully supports both edge-localized and corner-localized topological states at microwave frequencies. The approach is demonstrated to be inexpensive using printed circuit board technology.

**Method.** The study combines numerical simulations (using Eigenmode solvers for band structure and IPR calculation) with physical measurements of the transmission coefficient on fabricated PCB structures.

**Model / system.** The system is a metasurface composed of SRRs utilizing couplings between dipole ($p$) and quadrupole ($d$) modes, modeled by a tight-binding Hamiltonian on a dimerized lattice. The platform operates in the 1.5–1.7 GHz microwave range using PCB technology.

**Key observables.** Edge-localized states, corner-localized states, bulk band gap frequencies (e.g., 1.62–1.72 GHz), and state localization quantified by the Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating frequency range of 1.5–1.7 GHz; coupling constants $J$ and $K$; SRR geometry parameters like gap width ($w$) and capacitor tolerances.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on nearest-neighbor approximations in the tight-binding model, and the structure lacks full C4 rotational symmetry due to unit cell asymmetry.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of the metasurface using Type-1 (dipole) and Type-2 (quadrupole) SRRs. Simulations show eigenstate profiles confirming distinct bulk, edge, and corner localization patterns, while experimental data confirm field confinement matching theoretical predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the concept of HOTIs in RF metasurfaces using SRRs. It details the tight-binding model and uses numerical simulations to predict topological features (edge/corner states). The latter part validates these findings through physical measurements on fabricated PCB samples, comparing measured field localization against simulation results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photonic higher-order topological insulators (HOTI) are characterized by a hierarchy of topologically-protected states with different dimensionalities, making them especially interesting for potential applications that combine strong localization of electromagnetic fields and their robust waveguiding. However, their practical implementation often requires expensive processing techniques and is limited by accessible material parameters. In this paper, we demonstrate that a radio-frequency photonic HOTI can be implemented as a metasurface composed of split-ring resonators with couplings between dipole and quadrupole modes. We verify, by numerical simulations and experimentally at frequencies of 1.5-1.7 GHz, that a proposed metasurface supports corner- and edge-localized states. Our results reveal a scalable and easily reconfigurable GHz-range platform that employs printed circuit board technology, thus making crucial steps required for further experimental studies of photonic HOTI and the development of their microwave applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates anomalous pairing currents in bosonic lattices on a kagome structure. It shows that these pairing currents create a second, distinct chiral current channel not found in particle-conserving theories. The key diagnostic tool is the phase-sensitive leakage ratio ($\Lambda_I$), which allows for experimental verification of this unique topological feature in circuit or photonic arrays.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, measurable diagnostic ($\Lambda_I$) to distinguish genuine topological physics in bosonic systems from standard fermionic models, which is crucial for interpreting measurements in superconducting circuits or photonics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a microscopic statement regarding the distinct topological contribution of anomalous pairing currents ($	ext{PC}$) in bosonic systems, which is absent in particle-conserving models.

**Main result.** Bosonic pairing opens a second chiral current channel ($\langle I angle$) distinct from the single-particle hopping current ($\langle J angle$), providing a unique topological signature testable in driven quantum platforms.

**Method.** The analysis uses para-unitary diagonalization of the Bogoliubov Hamiltonian and derives lattice continuity equations to calculate both $	ext{SPC}$ and $	ext{PC}$.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled on a 2D kagome lattice using bosonic operators, incorporating hopping terms (single-particle coherence) and pairing terms (anomalous coherence). The model is directly mapped onto superconducting circuits or photonic lattices.

**Key observables.** Single Particle Current ($\langle J angle$), Pairing Current ($\langle I angle$), Phase-sensitive leakage ratio ($\Lambda_I$), Integer para-unitary Chern numbers ($C_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hopping phase ($\psi$), Pairing phases ($\varphi$), Coupling ratios (e.g., $
u_{	ext{off}}/
u_{	ext{on}}$), and lattice geometry (kagome).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard fermionic index theorem does not transfer literally because the diagonalization structure group is $U(m, m)$ rather than compact $U(2m)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare microscopic current decompositions ($	ext{SPC}$ vs $	ext{PC}$) around edges and defects for different phase configurations (Case 1, Case 2, Case 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first defining the full bosonic Hamiltonian, deriving separate continuity equations for hopping and pairing currents, analyzing topological invariants via para-unitary diagonalization, and finally comparing results across distinct physical regimes (anomalous vs. confined).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that bosonic pairing opens a second chiral current channel on a 2D kagome lattice, absent from any particle-conserving model. From the continuity equation, we derive both a hopping current and an anomalous pairing current on the lattice, and predict chiral circulation in bulk-gapped phases with integer para-unitary Chern numbers, as well as a phase-sensitive leakage ratio, $Λ_{\cal I}$, for the pairing current around a defect. This ratio can be tuned from confined to strongly anomalous regimes at fixed topology. The two channels differ microscopically: the hopping current is sourced by the on-bond single-particle coherence, whereas the pairing current is sourced by the off-site anomalous coherence, whose spatial range is governed by the BdG pairing gap rather than by the single-particle gap. This separation produces distinct defect-induced signatures in real space, identifies a regime in which bulk topology and anomalous edge response coexist with no analogue in particle-conserving matter, and is directly testable in driven photonic lattices and superconducting-circuit arrays.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes a theoretical model explaining Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) in molecular junctions by combining quantum tunneling theory with dissipation. It shows that even weak, current-dependent spin perturbations can lead to near-perfect spin polarization. This mechanism successfully explains the observed symmetry of CISS regarding current reversal while remaining consistent with fundamental transport laws.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses spin transport phenomena in nanoscale molecular junctions, which is a key area bridging condensed matter physics with quantum chemistry and AMO concepts like spin-orbit coupling effects on tunneling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to elucidate the mechanism behind Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) in dc transport through insulating chiral molecules, particularly explaining how large spin polarization can arise while respecting fundamental symmetries.

**Main result.** Quantum tunneling combined with dissipation is shown to amplify small spin-dependent perturbations, yielding high spin polarizations ($\sim 100\%$) and naturally reproducing the characteristic current-direction invariance of CISS.

**Method.** The analysis employs theoretical modeling using a 1D scattering approach, incorporating the WKB approximation for tunneling probabilities in the presence of a weak Zeeman interaction derived from the applied current.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as quantum tunneling through an insulating chiral molecule placed between two leads. The physics is governed by spin-dependent molecular orbital energies ($V_\sigma$) perturbed by a current-induced magnetic field, and dissipation is included via an external resistor ($R$).

**Key observables.** Spin Polarization (SP), relative transmission difference ($T_{	ext{split}}$), and the dependence of these quantities on applied bias voltage ($V_b$) and molecular length ($L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The characteristic mean free path ($\ell_\alpha$), the magnitude of the Zeeman coupling ($\alpha(I)$), and the resistance $R$ (which extends the measurable voltage range).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes that single-particle descriptions are insufficient due to Kramers' degeneracy, necessitating the inclusion of dissipation. The analysis relies on the WKB approximation for tunneling.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare current ($I_\sigma$) vs bias voltage ($V_b$) curves with and without an added resistor ($R$), demonstrating that $R$ extends the voltage range where high SP is maintained. Another figure confirms the scaling of SP with system length $L$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the model by first establishing the necessity of dissipation to overcome single-particle limitations, then deriving the tunneling probabilities using WKB, and finally demonstrating how the resulting current-voltage characteristics naturally exhibit the required symmetry (current-direction invariance).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a minimal model for chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS) in dc transport through insulating chiral molecules, based on quantum tunneling and interaction-induced spin splitting. As a concrete realization of the latter, we consider a weak Zeeman interaction of the particle spin with the current-induced magnetic field, recently shown to occur in helical molecules. We show that quantum tunneling, combined with dissipation, amplifies the effect, so that even such a small spin-dependent perturbation can yield spin polarizations on the order of 100\% across a wide range of applied bias voltages. Furthermore, our tunneling scenario naturally reproduces the characteristic CISS symmetry of the current-voltage dependence -- namely, the invariance of the spin-polarization sign under reversal of the current direction -- while fully respecting Onsager's reciprocity relations.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates the stability of vortex and edge states in Dirac materials driven by structured light beams. By treating impurities and polarization deviations perturbatively, the authors show that while these imperfections break ideal symmetries, the fundamental topological signatures remain observable. The work provides critical guidance on realizing such systems experimentally.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges topological physics (Dirac materials, edge states) with non-equilibrium quantum optics (Floquet engineering). The detailed analysis of how symmetry breaking mechanisms (impurities, detuning) affect quantized light-matter interactions is highly relevant for designing robust quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how impurity scattering and polarization detuning affect light-induced vortex and edge states in finite-size, massive Dirac materials driven by a Vortex Light Beam (VLB). The goal is to understand the robustness of these topological features under realistic imperfections.

**Main result.** Both vortex and edge state signatures remain observable despite impurities and deviations from perfect circular polarization. Polarization detuning causes bulk states to gradually fill the dynamical gap via second-order level repulsion, while off-center impurities break rotational symmetry by mixing angular momentum sectors.

**Method.** The analysis employs Floquet theory applied to a time-dependent Dirac Hamiltonian, solving for quasienergy spectra and real-space wavefunctions using numerical methods adapted for finite geometries. Perturbative corrections are calculated for both impurity potentials and polarization detuning terms.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D massive Dirac material ($H_D$) irradiated by a VLB, leading to a time-periodic Floquet Hamiltonian ($	ext{H}_F$). The analysis incorporates perturbations from static Gaussian impurities and polarization detuning ($\lambda$), which modifies the effective Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Quasienergy spectrum ($\epsilon_m$), real-space probability density ($ho(r)$), Local Density of States (LDOS), and spectral gap closing/filling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Vortex Orbital Angular Momentum ($\ell$), polarization detuning ($\lambda$), impurity strength ($V_0$) and spatial extent ($a_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a perturbative approach for both impurities and detuning, assuming the topological edge states remain resolvable. It also relies on specific symmetry constraints (e.g., total angular momentum conservation in ideal cases).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the VLB incidence profile, the quasienergy spectrum showing gap opening/closing with $\ell$ or $\lambda$, and real-space probability densities demonstrating state localization and charge redistribution patterns due to impurities.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the ideal Floquet system (vortex/edge states) to analyzing perturbations. It first examines impurity effects, showing angular momentum mixing; then, it analyzes polarization detuning, revealing second-order gap filling via bulk state hybridization; finally, it synthesizes these findings to show robustness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study impurity scattering and polarization detuning in finite-size vortex-light-beam-driven massive Dirac systems. In finite geometries, circularly polarized vortex light opens a dynamical gap where topological edge states coexist with photoinduced multiply quantized vortex states. We analyze how finite-size effects, vorticity, and effective particle-hole symmetry manifest in the quasienergy spectrum, real-space states, and local density of states. We show that angular-momentum mixing due to localized impurities and impurity clusters reshape vortex states, while when produced by circular polarization, it leads to a gradual filling of the dynamical gap with bulk-derived states. Our results indicate that both vortex and edge signatures remain observable in the presence of impurities and realistic polarization deviations, providing guidance for experimental realizations.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work reveals that superconductivity in flat Chern bands is governed by a novel 'geometry-driven' Kohn--Luttinger mechanism. By analyzing the alignment between the attractive Coulomb interaction and geometric form factors, the authors predict specific pairing symmetries (s-wave or chiral p/f-wave) depending on spin polarization. This establishes quantum geometry as a fundamental principle organizing unconventional superconductivity in topological materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the concept of geometric constraints (quantum geometry) dictating emergent many-body phases is highly relevant to understanding topological order in synthetic quantum platforms or engineered lattice systems studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates the mechanism driving superconductivity near correlated topological phases, specifically focusing on a geometry-driven Kohn--Luttinger (KL) pairing mechanism.

**Main result.** They establish that quantum geometry dictates optimal pairing strength in flat Chern bands, leading to extended-$s$-wave instability for unpolarized pairing and chiral $p$/$f$-wave order for spin-polarized pairing.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Random Phase Approximation (RPA) screening of the Coulomb interaction within the framework of a gap equation solved on the Skyrmion lattice model.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using flat Chern bands, realized minimally via the Skyrmion lattice model. The effective attraction is derived from RPA-screened interactions and analyzed near local band extrema.

**Key observables.** Superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$), order parameter ($\Delta$), pairing symmetry (s-wave, p-wave, f-wave), and superfluid stiffness ($D(T)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fermi filling ($
u$), magnetic length scale ($l_B$), and the degree of spin polarization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating the form factor near band extrema and uses the Skyrmion lattice model as a minimal realization for flat Chern bands.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the band structure, comparison between SLM and LLL form factors, RPA potential strength vs. filling, and $T_c$ dependence on filling and temperature effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the geometry-driven KL mechanism using the Skyrmion lattice model Hamiltonian and RPA screening. It then analyzes spin-unpolarized and spin-polarized pairing channels separately to determine the resulting superconducting symmetry and stability, concluding with finite-temperature analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent observations of superconductivity near correlated topological phases in flat bands suggest a facile link between flat-band geometry and electron pairing. In this work, we reveal a geometry-driven Kohn--Luttinger mechanism in which Landau-level-like form factors align the attractive lobe of the RPA-screened Coulomb interaction with the form-factor peak, generating an anomalously strong attractive channel near local band extrema. Using the Skyrmion lattice model as a minimal realization, we show that for spin-unpolarized pairing the form-factor magnitude enforces an emergent momentum-space translational symmetry and selects an extended-$s$ instability concentrated at small Fermi pockets, while for spin-polarized pairing the form-factor phase drives chiral $p$- and $f$-wave order without invoking spin fluctuations. The band-extrema enhancement persists in higher Landau-level analogs and survives finite-temperature screening and Berezinskii--Kosterlitz--Thouless phase fluctuations. Our work establishes quantum geometry as a key organizing principle for unconventional pairing in flat Chern bands.

</details>

### [Room-temperature tuning and probing of Fermi polarons in atomically thin semiconductors on a plasmonic metasurface](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17278v1)

**Authors:** Tingting Wu, Francesca Maria Marchetti, Antonio Tiene, Antonio I. Fernández-Domínguez, Miao Qi, Zhe Wang, Lin Liu, Lei Wei, Francisco J. Garcia-Vidal, Mengxiao Chen, Qi Jie Wang, Yu Luo

**Summary.** This research develops a novel platform combining atomically thin semiconductors ($	ext{WS}_2$) with plasmonic metasurfaces to study Fermi polarons at room temperature. By using scattering spectroscopy, the authors achieve quantitative control over polaron branch spectral weights by tuning both the film thickness and applying uniaxial strain. This establishes a powerful tool for manipulating correlated electronic states in 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides an excellent example of using plasmonics to enhance optical contrast for probing subtle many-body physics (FPs) in solid-state systems, bridging fundamental correlated electron theory with device-relevant heterostructures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is extending active, quantitative, branch-resolved probing of Fermi polarons (FPs) from monolayer to stacked geometries in atomically thin semiconductors.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated that integrating $	ext{WS}_2$ with a plasmonic metasurface allows for room-temperature, strain-tunable spectroscopy to resolve and manipulate the attractive and repulsive polaron branches across multiple layers.

**Method.** The study combines dark-field scattering spectroscopy measurements with theoretical modeling based on coupled oscillator Hamiltonians (COM) to fit spectral features.

**Model / system.** The system involves electron-doped $	ext{WS}_2$ flakes (1L to 4L) placed on a strain-tunable plasmonic metasurface. The physics is modeled via the Fermi polaron quasiparticle interacting with the degenerate Fermi sea and coupled to surface plasmons, forming three hybrid polariton branches.

**Key observables.** Polaron branch spectral weights ($w_{AP}, w_{RP}$), Rabi coupling strength ($\Omega$), energy splitting between branches, and temperature dependence of these features.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Layer number (1L to 4L), applied uniaxial strain (tensile/compressive), electron doping level, and plasmonic resonance frequency.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes that the optical response in multilayer samples is dominated by the metal-proximal layer, simplifying the full vertical averaging problem. PL measurements are sometimes used as an independent benchmark for intrinsic parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup (metasurface integration), spectral evolution under strain and thickness changes, and theoretical fitting of coupled oscillator models to extract coupling strengths and spectral weights.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing a robust scattering spectroscopy technique that overcomes PL quenching in multilayers. It then systematically investigates how layer number controls the overall polaron response, culminating in demonstrating that uniaxial strain acts as a powerful knob to deterministically redistribute oscillator strength between the attractive and repulsive branches.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Fermi polaron, arising from interactions between a mobile impurity and a degenerate Fermi sea, is a many-body quasiparticle that provides a sensitive probe of strongly correlated electronic phases in atomically thin semiconductors. In doped transition-metal dichalcogenides, the attractive and repulsive polaron branches are well established in monolayers. However, extending active control and quantitative, branch-resolved probing to stacked geometries has remained elusive because spectral quenching and weak optical contrast restrict access to Fermi polaron signatures. Here, we integrate electron-doped WS$_2$ flakes from monolayer to quadrilayer with a strain-tunable plasmonic metasurface, enabling high-contrast scattering readout at room temperature through coupling between Fermi polaron resonances and surface plasmons. This platform enables quantitative extraction of polaron branch spectral weights and coupling strengths across different layer numbers. We uncover a systematic thickness dependence of the spectral-weight distribution and demonstrate continuous and fully reversible spectral-weight transfer between attractive and repulsive branches in bilayers and quadrilayers, with near-complete transfer achieved in bilayers. By identifying layer number and strain as complementary control parameters for Fermi polarons, our results establish metasurface-enabled scattering spectroscopy as a practical route to resolve and manipulate many-body resonances in stacked van der Waals semiconductors, bridging idealized monolayer polaron physics and device-relevant architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors introduce Photoexcitation single-charge AFM (PE-AFM) to probe photoexcitation processes in isolated molecules. By synchronizing laser pulses with AFM tip oscillations, they detect charge transfer events related to excited states. This technique provides unprecedented atomic-scale characterization of molecular photophysics, revealing the role of long-lived electronic states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum optics/molecular physics with nanoscale characterization. The ability to map out multi-state potential energy surfaces and measure lifetimes *in situ* at the single-molecule level is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems dynamics in condensed matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is obtaining atomic-scale insights into photoexcitation processes and subsequent charge transfer dynamics within single, electronically isolated molecules.

**Main result.** The proposed Photoexcitation single-charge AFM (PE-AFM) technique successfully detects these photocurrents with angstrom spatial resolution, revealing the involvement of long-lived excited states like $	ext{Q}_1$.

**Method.** The method combines Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM) with synchronized nanosecond laser pulses to induce photoexcitation and measure subsequent electron tunneling events via changes in cantilever damping.

**Model / system.** The system involves single molecules, such as copper phthalocyanine ($	ext{CuPc}$), adsorbed on non-conductive dielectric films ($	ext{NaCl}$) over a metal substrate. Theoretical modeling uses interacting Hamiltonians to describe the multi-state electronic structure (e.g., $	ext{D}_0$, $	ext{Q}_1$) and potential energy surfaces.

**Key observables.** Photoexcitation-driven AFM contrast, cantilever damping modulation ($\Delta f$), photoemission current, excited-state spectral features, and transition voltages ($V_G$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Long-lived state lifetime ($	au \approx 11 \, \mu	ext{s}$), spatial resolution ($	ext{\AA}$), laser wavelength ($\lambda = 640 	ext{ nm}$), and gate voltage dependence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The technique assumes electronic decoupling of molecules via insulating films, and analysis often relies on modeling the rate-limiting step being dictated by the longest-lived excited state pathway.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate simulation results for near-field electric field enhancement in dielectric environments, the PE-AFM operating principle showing charge cycling, polarization dependence of the signal, and spatial maps revealing orbital selectivity based on gate voltage.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for atomic-scale photoexcitation sensing to detailing the PE-AFM methodology (including simulations), demonstrating its capability via spectroscopy and mapping ($	ext{CuPc}$), and finally using advanced fitting techniques to characterize long-lived excited state dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Atomic-scale insights into light-matter interaction can be obtained using light-assisted scanning probe microscopy techniques. Recently, photoexcited charge carriers have been detected by means of scanning tunnelling microscopy, enabling the study of photo-induced charge transfer with atomic-scale spatial resolution. Here, we propose an approach based on atomic force microscopy (AFM), namely photoexcitation single-charge AFM (PE-AFM), to detect photoexcitation in single molecules adsorbed on non-conductive dielectric films. Synchronizing laser pulses to the oscillation of the tip of an AFM enables the detection of electron tunnelling events that follow photoexcitation. We demonstrate the PE-AFM technique for individual copper phthalocyanine molecules and achieve photoexcitation-driven AFM contrast with ångström spatial resolution. The observed sub-molecular contrast suggests the involvement of a long-lived quadruplet excited state. When combined with the recently developed AFM excited-state spectroscopy including lifetime measurements, PE-AFM enables comprehensive characterization of electronic states involved in photoexcitation and subsequent intersystem crossing, establishing a powerful platform for investigating photophysical processes at the single-molecule level.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using AC magnetic-flux-driven SQUID diode spectroscopy as a novel diagnostic tool for mapping the Current-Phase Relation (CPR) in unconventional superconductors. By analyzing the resulting 2D phase diagram of the diode efficiency, researchers can distinguish between different CPR harmonics ($\sin\phi$, $\sin(\phi/2)$, $\sin(2\phi)$) based on characteristic Bessel function patterns. This method offers a robust way to probe exotic superconducting states like those found in topological or multiband materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique provides a powerful, non-invasive transport measurement to probe microscopic quantum details of superconducting order parameters (like fractional or higher harmonics) that are otherwise difficult to measure directly.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is unambiguously extracting individual Current-Phase Relation (CPR) harmonics from the CPR spectrum, as different components yield nearly identical static interference patterns obscured by device asymmetries and dynamics.

**Main result.** AC magnetic-flux-driven SQUID diode spectroscopy provides a high-resolution diagnostic tool, revealing distinct fingerprints of various CPR harmonics ($\sin\phi$, $\sin(\phi/2)$, $\sin(2\phi)$) through characteristic Bessel function dressings in the 2D phase diagram $\eta(\varphi_{ac}, \omega)$.

**Method.** The authors use complementary reductions of fast-driven dynamics, including Kapitza-type perturbation theory and Jacobi–Anger averaging, to analyze the diode efficiency $\eta$ as a function of AC flux amplitude ($\varphi_{ac}$) and frequency ($\omega$).

**Model / system.** The system is an asymmetric DC SQUID with unequal junction critical currents. The model incorporates Josephson junctions whose CPRs can contain higher-order or fractional harmonics, such as $\sin(\phi/2)$ (4$\pi$-periodic) and $\sin(2\phi)$ ($\pi$-periodic).

**Key observables.** Diode efficiency $\eta(\varphi_{ac}, \omega)$, which exhibits characteristic nodal line patterns governed by Bessel functions ($J_0(\xi_0)$, $J_0(\xi_0/2)$, $J_0(2\xi_0)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** AC flux amplitude ($\varphi_{ac}$), driving frequency ($\omega$), and the damping regime (overdamped to underdamped).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations like assuming $eta_L \ll 1$ for Jacobi-Anger reduction, and that the system dynamics can be mapped onto an effective CPR structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate static ($\eta(\varphi_{dc})$), frequency ($\eta(\omega)$), and full 2D phase diagrams ($\eta(\varphi_{ac}, \omega)$) showing distinct arc patterns corresponding to different underlying CPR harmonics.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theoretical framework by first establishing the SQUID model, then applying fast-averaging techniques (Jacobi-Anger), and finally mapping the resulting effective CPR components onto measurable signatures in the 2D phase diagram $\eta(\varphi_{ac}, \omega)$ for various harmonic content.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The current-phase relation (CPR) of a Josephson junction encodes microscopic information on superconducting states through higher-order and fractional harmonics. However, their unambiguous extraction is challenging, as different CPR components produce nearly identical static interference patterns that are further obscured by device asymmetries, damping, and dynamical effects. Here, we propose probing individual CPR harmonics via the ac magnetic-flux-driven diode effect in asymmetric dc SQUIDs with unequal junction critical currents. Using two complementary reductions of the fast-driven dynamics -- a Kapitza-type perturbation theory for the conventional junction and a Jacobi--Anger averaging for a general CPR -- we show that ac flux modulation dresses each harmonic with a distinct Bessel function, yielding characteristic signatures in the diode efficiency $η(φ_{\rm ac},ω)$ as a function of ac flux amplitude $φ_{\rm ac}$ and frequency $ω$. We verify and extend these predictions by numerical solutions of the coupled dynamical equations for CPRs containing $\sin\varphi$, $\sin(\varphi/2)$, and $\sin 2\varphi$ terms ($\varphi$: superconducting phase difference), and construct phase diagrams of $η(φ_{\rm ac},ω)$. Distinct CPR components are revealed to produce characteristic weak, sparse, dense, or intermodulated arc patterns that remain robust in both overdamped and underdamped regimes. This suggests ac-flux-driven SQUID diode spectroscopy as a probe of current-phase relations in topological materials, multiband systems, and other unconventional superconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how energy localizes into solitons within complex, damped, nonlinear metamaterials subjected to disorder and driven at superharmonic frequencies. Using advanced perturbation methods like the method of multiple scales, the authors derive governing equations that reveal a crucial difference in phase dynamics compared to simpler resonances. The findings confirm that controlled disorder can induce soliton formation, supporting applications for vibration control strategies.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of energy localization and soliton formation in disordered nonlinear lattices is a core topic bridging condensed matter physics (disorder effects) and nonlinear dynamics. The extension to superharmonic resonances provides new insights into wave propagation control mechanisms beyond standard primary resonance studies.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a framework to study energy localization, specifically soliton dynamics, in nonlinear disordered metastructures operating under superharmonic resonance conditions.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that the phase plays a distinct role in superharmonic resonances compared to primary resonances, and shows that soliton nucleation is strongly dependent on disorder reducing the natural frequency.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on the Method of Multiple Scales applied to derive governing equations for soliton dynamics at second order, leading to novel restitution equations.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a metastructure composed of coupled Duffing oscillators with linear and nonlinear coupling terms, incorporating disorder-induced periodicity breaking. The analysis focuses on the steady-state regime under superharmonic excitation.

**Key observables.** Soliton nucleation rate, amplitude profiles ($u_n$ vs $a_n$), convergence criteria ($\max |d A_n/dt| < 10^{-7}$), and Relative Standard Deviation (RSD) quantifying robustness against uncertainty.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superharmonic excitation frequency ($\Theta$), disorder strength ($\deltaho$), coupling orders ($\alpha=3$), and the number of degrees of freedom ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes strong excitation, employs perturbation expansions up to $\epsilon^2$, and makes several approximations regarding neglecting certain couplings or non-resonant terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate space-time evolution showing convergence criteria satisfaction for localized states versus non-convergent periodic/chaotic responses based on the excitation frequency. Sweeps show soliton nucleation rates dependence on disorder strength and excitation frequency.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model and applying multiple scales to derive governing equations (including novel $\epsilon^2$ terms). It then analyzes the dynamics by comparing analytical solutions with numerical simulations, focusing on the distinct role of phase in superharmonic resonance compared to primary resonance. Finally, it assesses robustness against disorder uncertainty.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper proposes a framework for energy localization in nonlinear oscillator chains operating in non-fundamental resonance, with emphasis on superharmonic regimes. The system is modeled as a metastructure composed of Duffing oscillators with both linear and nonlinear coupling, incorporating disorder-induced periodicity breaking. Under the assumption of strong excitation, the method of multiple scales is employed to derive the governing equations for soliton dynamics. At first-order perturbation, the classical form of the Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation emerges, whereas second-order analysis yields a previously unreported equation arising from the restitution of time scales. Analytical and numerical results demonstrate the nucleation of solitons in both hardening and softening regimes, based on two approaches: direct time-domain simulations from initially motionless states and numerical continuation in the frequency domain. A key finding is the distinct role of phase in superharmonic resonances compared to the primary resonance; specifically, the coexistence of multiple frequency components in the steady-state response precludes interpreting the soliton directly as a displacement envelope. Instead, the resulting secular terms captures the soliton associated with the resonant contribution, while transient components remain present under superharmonic excitation. Furthermore, robustness against disorder uncertainty is assessed by determining the tolerance levels that preserve the phenomenon. These results support the development of vibration control strategies aimed at mitigating the increase in resonant frequencies associated with the geometric downscaling of mechanical systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates quasiparticle transport in various quantum chain models, demonstrating that the time evolution of the probability distribution function mimics classical random walk behaviors. By analyzing phonon-assisted hopping and disordered chains, the authors establish a direct quantum analogue to anomalous diffusion processes, particularly those exhibiting ballistic or U-shaped spreading.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges coherent quantum dynamics with classical anomalous diffusion processes, providing a microscopic quantum origin for phenomena like Lévy walks observed in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes how quasiparticle transport properties behave in quantum systems, specifically comparing coherent quantum dynamics to classical stochastic models like Lévy walks.

**Main result.** Quantum probability distributions exhibit behavior analogous to classical Continuous-Time Random Walk (CTRW) models, showing ballistic spreading or U-shaped profiles due to underlying coherence mechanisms.

**Method.** The analysis involves solving time evolution equations for the probability distribution function (PDF) using tight-binding Hamiltonians and applying advanced mathematical techniques like integral transforms and saddle-point approximations.

**Model / system.** The models include simple tight-binding chains, heterogeneous ensembles of chains with random hopping amplitudes, and phonon-assisted hopping mechanisms in 2D semiconductor systems (like TMDs).

**Key observables.** Time-dependent probability distribution function (PDF) $P_j(t)$, mean squared displacement $\langle x^2 angle$, and the shape transition of the PDF (bimodal to monomodal).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hopping amplitude ($T$), phonon coupling strength ($\omega_{ct}$), disorder parameters ($\mu, \sigma$), and time $t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as the continuum limit, saddle-point approximation for integrals, and assuming specific forms for random phases or hopping amplitudes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quasi-particle PDF ($P_j$) showing transitions from bimodal to monomodal shapes in heterogeneous ensembles, and demonstrate U-shaped/ballistic profiles in phonon-assisted hopping models.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes transport using several quantum models: simple tight-binding, heterogeneous ensembles (varying disorder), random time-dependent hopping, and finally the detailed analysis of phonon-assisted hopping mechanisms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We analyze the transport properties of quasiparticles locally excited at an initial time moment in several exactly solvable quantum models. It is revealed that, in the investigated quantum systems, the time-dependent probability distribution function (PDF) exhibits behavior similar to that of classical continuous-time random walk (CTRW) models, such as Lévy walks or diffusing diffusivity.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes that Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) in chiral molecules originates from a current-induced Molecular Orbital Moment (MOM). This MOM arises from an effective gauge field associated with structural distortions, allowing the effect to be explained without invoking intrinsic spin-orbit coupling. The theory successfully predicts characteristic asymmetries in transport measurements like MChA.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical mechanism for spin selectivity based purely on geometric and electronic structure effects (gauge fields/orbital moments), which is highly relevant to molecular spintronics and quantum transport phenomena studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the unresolved microscopic origin of Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) in helical molecules, proposing a mechanism that accounts for intrinsic molecular contributions.

**Main result.** CISS can arise from a current-induced Molecular Orbital Moment (MOM), whose direction is governed by a gauge field linked to the molecule's structural handedness, all without requiring intrinsic spin-orbit coupling.

**Method.** The theory models transport using a tight-binding Hamiltonian incorporating a Peierls phase derived from bond length variations, and calculates observables via Green's functions.

**Model / system.** The system uses chiral molecules like heptahelicene ($\chi^{\pm}[7]	ext{H}$), modeled by a tight-binding Hamiltonian. The mechanism centers on the generation of an emergent MOM due to circulating charge currents in molecular loops.

**Key observables.** Magnetochiral conductance asymmetry (MChA) and CISS magnetoresistance, I-V characteristics for enantiomers ($\chi^+$ vs $\chi^-$), and the Molecular Orbital Moment (MOM).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bias voltage ($V_B$), external magnetic field ($B_{	ext{ext}}$), coupling parameters ($t_B, t_T$), and the gauge flux ($\Phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory builds upon incorporating a distortion-induced gauge field to satisfy Onsager-Casimir reciprocity relations and explain zero-bias crossings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate I-V curves and MOM in enantiomers under bias/field, showing the influence of structural gauge fields ($\mathbf{A}_\chi$) on transport asymmetry. Later figures detail the decomposition of MOM into Fermi surface and sea contributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework based on modifying the Hamiltonian with a Peierls phase derived from molecular distortion to generate an emergent MOM. It then calculates transport properties (I-V curves, MChA) using Green's functions, analyzing how this MOM dictates chirality effects in the absence of intrinsic SOC.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The microscopic origin of the chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS) in helical molecules remains an open question. Recent experiments suggest that a significant contribution to CISS arises from the molecule itself, which is disregarded in existing interfacial or scattering based theories. Here we present an alternative theory of CISS to address this molecular contribution. The mechanism is based on the circulation of charge current in molecular loops that generates a molecular orbital moment (MOM). The direction of the MOM is governed by the gauge field arising from the structural distortion of the molecule and is associated with the handedness of the molecule. Such a MOM can produce finite CISS magnetoresistance and magnetochiral conductance asymmetries that are even in bias voltage, without violating the Onsager-Casimir reciprocity relations. Depending on the Fermi level and bias voltage, the MOM can be controlled externally, which can result in additional crossings of the enantiomer $I-V$ curves. Finally we explain the origin of the electrical magnetochiral anisotropy within the same framework, which establishes its generic applicability.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates whether near-zero-energy states observed on the surface of an iron-based superconductor are true Majorana zero modes, crucial for topological quantum computing. Using spin-polarized STM, the researchers analyzed defects and found that the spectral signatures are consistently explained by trivial Yu-Shiba-Rusinov bound states rather than robust topological edge modes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly focused on AMO systems, the rigorous experimental methodology—using local probes (STM) combined with quantum mechanical diagnostics (spin/energy resolution)—provides a template for identifying exotic quasiparticles in condensed matter systems relevant to open quantum systems research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central goal is to experimentally distinguish between true Majorana zero modes (MZMs) and topologically trivial defect states observed on the surface of an iron-based superconductor.

**Main result.** Through detailed spin-polarized scanning tunneling spectroscopy, the authors conclude that the observed near-zero-energy localized states at various defects are better explained by Yu-Shiba-Rusinov bound states originating from unpaired electrons rather than true topological Majorana modes.

**Method.** The study employs advanced Scanning Tunneling Microscopy/Spectroscopy (STM/STS), including spin polarization measurements, to map the density of states across different surface defects like step edges and line defects.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the iron-based superconductor Fe(Te,Se). The analysis involves comparing measured spectroscopic signatures against theoretical models describing YSR bound states coupled by magnetic impurities.

**Key observables.** Near-zero-energy localized states (Zero-Bias Peaks), spin polarization of the density of states ($	ext{SP-STS}$), and spectral weight transfer between positive and negative energies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature, external magnetic field strength (up to 3 T), and tunneling bias voltage ($V_{	ext{bias}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that true topological modes would exhibit specific signatures (e.g., dispersing behavior or unique spin dependence) which were not consistently found.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate various defect types on the surface, showing $	ext{d}I/	ext{d}V$ maps and line spectra taken across step edges, wrinkles, and bends to characterize in-gap states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically examines different classes of defects (step edges, Type I/II line defects) using STS. It progresses from initial observation of zero-bias peaks to detailed analysis involving spin polarization and comparison with established theoretical models for trivial bound states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Majorana zero modes, which obey non-Abelian exchange statistics, are promising candidates for topological quantum computation due to their robustness against environmental perturbations. The iron-based superconductor Fe(Te,Se) has been identified as an intrinsic topological superconductor, possibly hosting Majorana zero modes. In this paper, we report the observation of near-zero-energy localized states at multiple structural defects on the Fe(Te,Se) surface, which could be misidentified as Majorana zero modes without additional verification. By using spin-polarized scanning tunneling spectroscopy, we demonstrate that the near-zero-energy localized states on step edges and line defects originate from topologically trivial Yu-Shiba-Rusinov states. In addition, zero-energy bound states are also observed for regions without surface defects. A combined spatial and magnetic field dependent analysis of the spin-resolved tunneling spectra in these regions reveals that this type of zero-energy states cannot be attributed to the presence of Majorana bound states. These findings emphasize the importance of spin-dependent studies of low-energy states for pursuing Majorana zero modes.

</details>

### [Semiclassical Analysis of Tunneling in Graphene under Nonuniform Electrostatic and Magnetic Fields](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17689v1)

**Authors:** Maria V. Perel  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17689v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **3/5** · `Kondo & dissipative impurity` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper presents a comprehensive semiclassical theory for tunneling in graphene junctions under combined nonuniform electrostatic and magnetic fields. By developing explicit transfer matrix formalisms, the authors calculate transmission coefficients and predict resonance conditions, including 'magic' angles, demonstrating how external fields control electronic transport properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of semiclassical methods (WKB/transfer matrices) to solve quantum transport problems in structured materials like graphene is highly relevant for understanding mesoscopic electronic phenomena, which often bridge between continuum theory and discrete quantum effects.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a semiclassical theory to analyze the tunneling of Dirac fermions through an n-p-n junction in monolayer graphene when subjected to both nonuniform electrostatic and magnetic fields.

**Main result.** Explicit expressions for reflection and transmission coefficients, including their phases, are derived as functions of energy and incidence angle. The work also establishes semiclassical conditions for Fabry–Pérot resonances and 'magic' angles where enhanced transmission occurs.

**Method.** The analysis employs the WKB (semiclassical) approximation, treating the problem as a connection problem solved via transfer matrices across different potential regimes.

**Model / system.** The physical system is monolayer graphene described by the 2D massless Dirac equation. The model incorporates spatially varying electrostatic potentials ($U(x)$ forming an n-p-n junction) and perpendicular magnetic fields ($B(x)$), both dependent only on one spatial coordinate.

**Key observables.** Reflection coefficients ($r$), transmission coefficients ($t$), phases of $r$ and $t$, Fabry–Pérot resonance conditions, and 'magic' angles.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Energy ($E$), incidence angle ($	heta$), magnetic field strength ($B(x)$), and the spatial variation parameters of the potentials.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fields are assumed to be smooth functions vanishing outside a finite interval. The analysis relies on the semiclassical approximation, which is valid away from turning points.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic geometry of Klein tunneling under external fields and show numerical results detailing the angular dependence of transmission induced by the magnetic field.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the theory by first establishing the 1D Dirac equation governing the system. It then uses the transfer matrix formalism to calculate scattering coefficients for single junctions ($n-p$, $p-n$) and finally extends this to analyze Fabry–Pérot resonances across the full $n-p-n$ structure.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a semiclassical theory of tunnelling of Dirac fermions through an n-p-n junction in monolayer graphene subjected to a perpendicular magnetic field. Electrostatic and magnetic fields are assumed to be smooth functions of a single spatial coordinate, supported on a finite interval, vanishing outside it, and thus ensuring asymptotically free states. In contrast to earlier studies restricted to constant magnetic fields and exactly solvable electrostatic potential profiles, we consider a general electrostatic potential forming an n-p-n junction and an arbitrary magnetic field, and formulate the corresponding scattering problem. Within the semiclassical approximation, and under an additional assumption on the incidence angle, the problem reduces to a connection problem for a pair of degeneracy points, treated using results from our earlier work. We obtain explicit expressions for the reflection and transmission coefficients, including their phases, as functions of energy and incidence angle. Furthermore, we derive semiclassical conditions for Fabry-Pérot resonances and "magic" angles, and analyse the resulting interference pattern. Numerical results demonstrate the angular dependence of transmission induced by the magnetic field.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a novel spin-wave phase shifter by integrating a domain-wall racetrack into a magnonic waveguide. By exploiting the stray field from magnetic domain walls, researchers can induce large, controllable phase shifts ($\pm 90^\circ$) for propagating spin waves. This demonstrates a scalable route toward functional elements for information processing in magnonics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical magnetism, the concept of using engineered magnetic structures to control wave propagation phases is analogous to controlling quantum beam paths or implementing non-linear phase gates in photonic/quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop a compact, programmable spin-wave phase shifter for magnonic circuits by utilizing the stray field generated by magnetic domain walls.

**Main result.** Moving domain walls on a racetrack structure can induce significant and tunable phase shifts (up to $\pm 90^\circ$) in propagating Damon-Eshbach spin waves without altering the waveguide's physical geometry.

**Method.** The analysis relies heavily on micromagnetic simulations (MuMax3) complemented by semi-analytical models using the Wentzel–Kramers–Brillouin (WKB) approximation to calculate phase accumulation.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a YIG film waveguide patterned with a Permalloy ($	ext{Py}$) domain-wall racetrack. The stray field from pinned magnetic domain walls modifies the internal effective magnetic field experienced by the spin waves propagating in the YIG layer.

**Key observables.** Spin-wave phase shift ($\Delta\varphi$), SW wavelength change, and SW intensity attenuation ($\Delta A_{SW}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bias fields (e.g., $-15 	ext{ mT}$), operating frequencies (2.5–5 GHz), and material parameters for YIG and Py.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The WKB model assumes slowly-varying fields, and the analysis often focuses on a single central 1D slice approximation of the stray field profile.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry (Py racetrack over YIG), dispersion relations comparing bare vs. DW-modified waveguides, and spatial profiles of effective magnetic fields ($B_{	ext{eff},z}$) showing the influence of domain walls.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical setup (racetrack/DW interaction), developing theoretical models (WKB approximation for phase shift calculation), presenting simulation results comparing DW-induced shifts to simpler stripe geometries, and concluding with the potential application in magnonic circuits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a spin-wave phase shifter controlled using a domain-wall racetrack. The concept is demonstrated using micromagnetic simulations of a Permalloy domain-wall racetrack placed above a YIG film. The stray field from pinned domain walls modifies the internal magnetic field in the YIG region under the racetrack. This leads to a local change of the spin-wave wavelength and thereby enables control of the phase accumulated by Damon-Eshbach spin waves propagating through the region. Moving domain walls on the racetrack, the same physical structure can provide phase shifts of up to +/-90 degrees, without changing the waveguide geometry. A model based on the semiclassical approximation confirms that the phase shift is dominated by the domain-wall-induced stray field. These results suggest a route toward a compact programmable spin-wave phase shifter for interference-based magnonic circuits for information processing. Moreover, the demonstrated magnonic device integration with a magnetic domain-wall racetrack can lead to its application in in-memory computing.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports on achieving significant supercurrent rectification in graphene Josephson junctions using inherent structural disorder. By combining low-temperature experiments with theoretical modeling, the authors prove that even minor, unavoidable disorder can induce diode behavior by breaking mirror symmetry. This work is vital for advancing superconducting electronics based on 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clean platform (graphene) for observing non-reciprocal quantum transport phenomena, which are crucial for developing superconducting electronic components and understanding symmetry breaking in mesoscopic superconductors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study demonstrates that graphene Josephson junctions inherently exhibit supercurrent rectification (Josephson diode effect) due to asymmetric disorder, even in highly clean systems.

**Main result.** Supercurrent rectification efficiency exceeding 20% was achieved under an out-of-plane magnetic field, confirming that residual long-range disorder is sufficient to break the necessary mirror symmetry.

**Method.** The work combines experimental measurements of V-I curves and Fraunhofer patterns with theoretical modeling based on a tight-binding Hamiltonian approach.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an hBN-encapsulated graphene Josephson junction contacted by Niobium leads. The theory models the transport using a tight-binding Hamiltonian incorporating disorder potentials $V(x, y)$ and magnetic field effects.

**Key observables.** Supercurrent rectification efficiency ($\eta$), switching currents ($I_{	ext{sw}+}, I_{	ext{sw}-}$), and Fraunhofer interference patterns modulated by current direction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Out-of-plane magnetic field ($B$), backgate voltage ($V_{BG}$), and the strength/nature of the long-range scattering potential (disorder).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that residual disorder provides the necessary asymmetry to break mirror symmetry, and approximations like the Andreev limit are used in theoretical derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate device schematics, V-I curves showing directional switching currents at different magnetic fields, and Fraunhofer patterns demonstrating non-reciprocity.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the necessity of symmetry breaking (time-reversal + mirror) to presenting experimental evidence of high rectification efficiency, followed by theoretical modeling confirming that disorder is the source of this asymmetry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Josephson diodes are non-reciprocal superconducting devices characterized by different switching currents depending on the current flow direction. They recently attracted considerable theoretical and experimental attention, in view of their possible application as rectifying elements in the field of superconducting electronics, and as probes to investigate symmetry breaking mechanisms in mesoscopic systems. In this work, we show that graphene Josephson junctions provide rectification of supercurrent with an efficiency exceeding 20%. The effect appears applying a mT out-of-plane magnetic field and is enhanced close to the nodes of the Fraunhofer interference pattern. Our theoretical model identifies long-range scattering potentials in the junction as the symmetry-breaking mechanism, which yields supercurrent rectification in highly transparent junctions. While graphene stands as an ultra-clean transmission medium, our work shows that unavoidable residual disorder in a clean two-dimensional system is sufficient to promote this effect. Tailoring of the inversion (mirror) symmetry breaking could be obtained via proper design of external gates.

</details>
