**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates ferroelectricity in superlubric van der Waals heterostructures, demonstrating that the polarization mechanism is not simply due to sliding but results from a hybrid electronic-ionic coupling involving spacer layer buckling. By developing and analyzing an energy functional dependent on both stacking order and buckling, the authors establish these systems as a distinct class of ferroelectrics exhibiting complex switching behaviors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter ferroelectrics, the use of coupled order parameters ($	ext{s}$ and $\delta$) to describe emergent collective behavior under external fields shares conceptual parallels with modeling many-body dynamics or phase transitions in open quantum systems where multiple degrees of freedom interact non-trivially.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To understand the physical mechanism of polarization and ferroelectricity in Superlubric sliding ferroelectrics (SL-sFEs) and how they fundamentally differ from conventional sliding ferroelectrics.

**Main result.** Polarization arises from a unique hybrid electronic-ionic coupling between interlayer sliding and the out-of-plane buckling of the spacer layer, leading to distinct ferroelectric hysteresis types.

**Method.** The study employs first-principles calculations (DFT) combined with continuum Landau-like models parameterized by key order parameters like stacking ($s$) and buckling ($\delta$).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using van der Waals heterostructures, such as $	ext{MoS}_2/	ext{hBN}/	ext{MoS}_2$, where the total energy depends on sliding and spacer buckling parameters.

**Key observables.** Out-of-plane polarization ($P$), interlayer sliding friction/energy barrier, and out-of-plane buckling ($\Delta z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strain ($\eta_{	ext{BN}}$) imposed on the spacer layer; critical limits defined by flat ($\delta=0$) or maximum buckling ($\delta_m$) states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The polarization is not solely driven by sliding, but requires an active coupling mechanism mediated by the spacer's out-of-plane deformation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate polarization vs. strain/buckling parameter ($\delta$), showing smooth approaches to zero polarization at $\delta=0$, and map out four distinct types of ferroelectric hysteresis loops based on critical field ordering.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical system (SL-sFEs), deriving a continuum energy functional dependent on two order parameters ($s$ and $\delta$), analyzing the stationary conditions for these parameters, and finally classifying the resulting complex ferroelectric phase diagrams via hysteresis loops.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

One strategy to lower the switching barrier in a sliding ferroelectric (sFE) is to insert an incommensurate spacer to reduce sliding friction, creating a superlubric sliding ferroelectric (SL-sFE). However, how polarization survives across the effectively decoupled outer layers remains an open question. We show that SL-sFEs are fundamentally different from conventional sFEs: polarization is not driven by sliding alone, but by an intricate coupling between interlayer sliding and the out-of-plane buckling of the spacer layer. This coupling results in a unique hybrid electronic-ionic polarization arising from asymmetric orbital hybridization. The interplay of these order parameters generates several distinct types of ferroelectric hysteresis, including mixed first- and second-order transitions, multi-step switching, and antiferroelectric-like behavior, establishing SL-sFEs as a distinct class of ferroelectrics.

</details>

### [Dispersive mode coupling in $p$-doped semiconductor nanomechanical resonators](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17392v1)

**Summary.** The paper theoretically analyzes dispersive mode coupling in $p$-doped semiconductor nanoresonators by incorporating strain effects on the valence band hole energy. It demonstrates that doping strongly enhances this nonlinear interaction, providing a mechanism to tune mechanical properties via carrier concentration. This has practical implications for developing highly stable quantum sensors and clocks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work models nonlinear dynamics in a solid-state platform where electronic structure (hole doping) directly modifies mechanical properties. The strong, tunable nonlinearity makes it relevant to quantum sensing and optomechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how doping in semiconductor nanomechanical resonators enhances dispersive mode coupling (cross-Kerr nonlinearity) between vibrational modes.

**Main result.** Doping significantly strengthens the coupling compared to intrinsic effects, and this enhancement is nonmonotonic with hole density. The system can utilize this effect for temperature stabilization or compensating frequency shifts.

**Method.** The analysis uses a free energy expansion in strain ($\Delta F$) derived from diagonalizing the Luttinger–Kohn–Bir–Pikus Hamiltonian for valence band holes.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a $p$-doped semiconductor nanomechanical resonator, modeled by coupling mechanical vibrational modes (governed by $H_{lin}$) to nonlinear terms arising from strain-dependent hole free energy ($\Delta F$).

**Key observables.** Cross-Kerr coupling parameters ($\gamma$), frequency shifts ($\delta\omega_\alpha$), and phase diffusion coefficients ($D_{	ext{ph}}^\alpha$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hole density ($n_h$), temperature ($T$), and the ratio of doping-induced to intrinsic nonlinearities.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The adiabatic approximation is used, assuming hole thermalization time is much shorter than vibrational timescales. The analysis often neglects renormalization effects in coupling parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature dependence of eigenfrequencies and cross-Kerr shifts ($\aleph_{TL}, \aleph_{LT}$), as well as the nonmonotonic density dependence of these shifts for different crystal orientations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the Hamiltonian, deriving the strain-dependent free energy expansion, calculating mode coupling coefficients using this expansion, and finally analyzing the resulting frequency shifts and temperature/density dependencies for specific resonator geometries (thin plates).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dispersive coupling between vibrational modes with different frequencies is a major nonlinear dynamical effect. We show that in $p$-doped semiconductors such coupling is strongly enhanced. Moreover, the coupling parameters increase with the order of the nonlinearity. The doping-induced dispersive coupling becomes much stronger than the intrinsic one already for moderately strong doping. Its dependence on the hole density is nonmonotonic, and the temperature dependence becomes nonmonotonic for higher densities. Relevant mesoscopic frequency fluctuations are briefly discussed. The results are applied to Si resonators, where doping is used to compensate the temperature dependence of a clock mode, whereas another low-frequency mode is used as a thermometer to enable temperature stabilization.

</details>

**Authors:** Andrew N. W. Hone, Vladimir S. Novikov, Jacek Szmigielski  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.17238v1>

**Summary.** This work analyzes complex, multi-component generalizations of the Camassa–Holm equation using Clifford algebras. It establishes deep connections between these PDEs and integrable Hamiltonian systems by employing advanced mathematical tools like spectral theory and reciprocal transformations. The research yields explicit solutions for peakon dynamics in the high-frequency limit, providing a comprehensive classification of related integrable models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical PDEs, the rigorous mathematical machinery—integrability theory, spectral methods, and Hamiltonian reduction—is highly relevant to understanding non-linear wave phenomena in quantum field theories or effective descriptions of many-body systems where continuum limits are taken.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates integrable, coupled systems of partial differential equations (PDEs) that represent vector perturbations of the Camassa–Holm equation within the mathematical framework of Clifford algebras.

**Main result.** The authors provide a complete classification of integrable two-component CH perturbations and derive explicit solutions for multi-peakon dynamics in the high-frequency limit, showing connections to Liouville integrable Hamiltonian systems.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced techniques including spectral theory on Clifford algebras, reciprocal transformations, Lax pair formulations, and reduction to finite-dimensional Hamiltonian systems via travelling wave assumptions.

**Model / system.** The core model is a $d$-component vector PDE system derived from an isospectral deformation problem in $	ext{C}\ell(W)$, generalizing the scalar Camassa–Holm equation. Specific focus is placed on the two-component case ($d=2$) and its high-frequency (Hunter-Saxton) limit.

**Key observables.** Conserved functionals/densities, peakon positions ($x_N$), amplitudes ($m_{ij}$), and the Weyl function $W(\lambda, t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of components ($d$), the arbitrary constant $\alpha$, and the specific parameters defining the initial conditions for multi-peakons.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes zero background solutions when studying travelling waves, and integrability is defined by the existence of an infinite hierarchy of local higher symmetries.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of two-component peakon positions and amplitudes for the HS2 system, showing asymptotic periodicity or divergence. Other figures show Liouville tori projections related to the Hamiltonian reduction.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general integrable structure via Clifford algebras and symmetries (symmetries classification) to analyzing specific reductions like the $d=2$ case using reciprocal transformations, culminating in explicit solutions for multi-peakon dynamics in the high-frequency limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Starting from a spectral problem posed in a Clifford algebra with $d$ generators and Euclidean signature, we study an integrable, coupled system of PDEs that can be viewed as a vector perturbation of the Camassa--Holm equation with residual orthogonal symmetry. In the two-component case $d=2$, we show that the travelling wave solutions correspond to a Liouville integrable Hamiltonian system with two degrees of freedom, making use of a reciprocal transformation linking the coupled PDEs to a symmetry of the Hirota--Satsuma system. We also present a symmetry classification of all integrable two-component perturbations of Camassa--Holm, and find that besides the $d=2$ system analyzed here, the coupled 2CH system studied by Olver and Rosenau (as well as by Chen, Liu and Zhang, and Falqui), and equations related to either of those systems by Miura transformations, we also obtain a new system that (to the best of our knowledge) has not been reported previously. For the case of an arbitrary number of components $d$, we additionally investigate the short-pulse (high-frequency) regime, in which the limiting dynamics are governed by a vector-valued Hunter-Saxton type system. Furthermore, we provide a detailed analysis of the corresponding measure-valued (weak) solutions associated with this system.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates intrinsic frequency dispersion in MOSFETs operating at cryogenic temperatures. The authors demonstrate that this dispersion originates from variable-range hopping through band-tail states caused by ionized impurities distributed throughout the depletion region, not just at the oxide interface. This provides a critical correction for accurately modeling high-frequency behavior in cryo-CMOS circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state electronics, the underlying physics—variable-range hopping in disordered potentials induced by impurities—is a canonical problem studied in condensed matter theory related to localization phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To characterize intrinsic frequency dispersion in the channel impedance of cryogenic MOSFETs, particularly deep into the strong-inversion region.

**Main result.** The frequency dispersion is attributed to variable-range hopping through band-tail localized states induced by ionized impurities distributed throughout the depletion region, even when drift conduction dominates.

**Method.** Impedance measurements using LCR meters and analysis via Cole–Cole (Nyquist) plots, combined with DC electrical characterization across varying temperatures and gate voltages.

**Model / system.** The physical system is cryogenic complementary metal-oxide-semiconductor (cryo-CMOS) MOSFETs. The model involves analyzing transport mechanisms in the presence of electrostatic disorder from ionized impurities within the semiconductor depletion region.

**Key observables.** Frequency dispersion characterized by a depressed semicircle in Cole–Cole plots; temperature dependence of conductance ($g_d \propto \exp(T/T_0)^{-1/3}$); extracted circuit parameters ($R_{DC}, R_{AC}, C_{AC}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cryogenic temperatures (down to 4.2 K); strong-inversion regime; high channel doping levels.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The frequency dispersion is governed by hopping conduction through impurity-induced band-tail states, which persists even when drift conduction dominates the DC current.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $	ext{I}_d-	ext{V}_g$ curves and Cole–Cole plots demonstrating the transition to depressed semicircles at low temperatures. Other figures detail the comparison between conventional interface-confined models and the proposed impurity-distributed model.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of intrinsic frequency dispersion in cryo-MOSFETs, presents measurements showing this dispersion via Cole-Cole plots, develops a theoretical framework attributing it to VRH through distributed band-tail states, and contrasts this finding with conventional interface-only models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cryogenic complementary metal-oxide-semiconductor (cryo-CMOS) technology is essential for quantum computing interfaces, which require precise modeling of dynamic device behavior. The output impedance of MOS field-effect transistors (MOSFETs) is frequency dependent, which has been conventionally attributed to extrinsic parasitics. Here, we report an intrinsic frequency dispersion in the channel impedance of cryogenic MOSFETs that persists deep into the strong-inversion region. Through a Cole-Cole analysis, we characterize this dispersion as a depressed semicircle in the impedance plane and attribute its behavior to variable-range hopping through band-tail localized states. Unlike conventional models where band-tail states are confined to the oxide interface, we demonstrate that in MOSFETs with high channel doping the band-tail states are induced by ionized impurities and distributed throughout the depletion region. Our paradigm accounts for frequency dispersion under strong inversion. This work demonstrates that ionized-impurities-induced hopping governs the dynamic response of cryo-MOSFETs channel impedance even when drift conduction dominates, offering critical insights for accurate small-signal modeling and high-frequency cryo-CMOS circuit design.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports on measuring nonreciprocal second-harmonic resistance ($R_{2\omega}$) in the Weyl semimetal $	ext{TaAs}$ to prove the existence and chirality of Fermi arcs. By comparing measurements on opposing surfaces, they observed a robust sign reversal and higher-order angular modulations that are absent in bulk conduction, establishing nonlinear transport as a direct probe for these topological surface states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel, highly sensitive, and topologically protected diagnostic tool for characterizing surface states in Weyl semimetals using nonlinear electrical transport, which is valuable for developing next-generation topological electronic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To experimentally resolve the chiral nature of Fermi arcs—topologically protected surface states in Weyl semimetals—by developing a nonlinear transport measurement technique.

**Main result.** The study unambiguously demonstrates the chiral nature of Fermi arcs through observing a surface-switchable, sign-reversed, and higher-order nonreciprocal second-harmonic response ($R_{2\omega}$) that is robust up to room temperature.

**Method.** Fabrication of micro-scale devices using Focused Ion Beam (FIB) techniques followed by nonlinear AC transport measurements ($R_{2\omega}$) on opposing topological surfaces.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the archetypal Weyl semimetal, Tantalum Arsenide ($	ext{TaAs}$), which hosts topologically protected surface states known as Fermi arcs connecting bulk Weyl nodes in momentum space.

**Key observables.** Second-harmonic magnetoresistance ($R_{2\omega}$), exhibiting sign reversal between top and bottom surfaces, and angular modulations fitting $\sin(	heta)$ and $\sin(3	heta)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature operation, magnetic field strength (up to 8.9 T), current density ($J$), and surface orientation (top vs. bottom).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Linear transport measurements are dominated by bulk conduction, while the nonlinear response is uniquely sensitive to the topological surface states; theoretical models based on Boltzmann transport fail quantitatively.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magnetic field dependence of $R_{2\omega}$ showing opposite signs for top/bottom surfaces (Fig 2a), current and angular dependencies revealing $\sin(	heta)$ and $\sin(3	heta)$ patterns (Figs 2c, 4b, 4c), and thickness dependence confirming surface origin (Fig 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the need for a new probe (nonlinear transport) due to bulk dominance in linear measurements. It presents evidence of surface-specific signals via sign reversal, confirms topological protection through robustness across temperature/thickness, and finally uses higher-order angular modulations ($\sin(3	heta)$) as definitive 'smoking-gun' evidence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Weyl semimetals host topologically protected surface states, known as Fermi arcs, which connect bulk Weyl nodes in momentum space. Both bulk Weyl nodes and Fermi arcs are anticipated to be chiral. The chirality of bulk bands has been confirmed through observations of the chiral anomaly and Weyl orbits. In contrast, despite their discovery more than a decade ago, the chiral nature of Fermi arcs has remained unresolved. Here we report Fermi-arc-induced nonlinear transport in the archetypal Weyl semimetal TaAs. Using focused ion beam techniques, we fabricated micro-scale devices that enable simultaneous transport measurements on opposing topological surfaces. While linear transport remains dominated by bulk conduction, nonlinear transport uncovers surface-specific contributions, including an exceptionally large third-order nonreciprocal response that exceeds conventional expectations and highlights the crucial role of the singular arc endpoints. Our findings unambiguously demonstrate the chiral nature of Fermi arcs and establish nonlinear transport as a direct probe of these topological surface states. By revealing a surface-switchable, room-temperature nonlinear response that is topologically protected, this work introduces a new functionality in Weyl semimetals. Given the abundance of natural materials predicted to host topological semimetal states, these results open opportunities for exploring nonlinear transport phenomena and device concepts across a broad class of systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses AC calorimetry to study magneto-quantum oscillations in the superconductor V2Ga5. By analyzing these oscillations, the authors successfully map out key parameters of the Fermi surface and demonstrate that the material exhibits a robust, field-independent Berry flux. This establishes ac calorimetry as a powerful technique for probing topological electronic hybridization in complex intermetallics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the precise measurement and characterization of the geometric phase (Berry flux) using bulk thermodynamic measurements provides an experimental handle on topological electronic structure, which is relevant to advanced condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To probe the bulk electronic structure, including topological properties, of the superconductor V2Ga5 using heat capacity magneto-quantum oscillations (MQOs).

**Main result.** The study confirms a dominant Fermi surface pocket ($\gamma$ pocket) via MQO analysis and demonstrates that the net Berry flux is invariant with magnetic field orientation, establishing ac calorimetry as a powerful probe for topological orbital hybridization.

**Method.** Highly sensitive AC calorimetry measurements of heat capacity under applied magnetic fields, analyzed using Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) to resolve oscillation frequencies.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the single-crystalline superconductor V2Ga5. The analysis relies on Lifshitz–Kosevich theory to interpret MQO signals originating from the $\gamma$ Fermi surface pocket near the Z point of the Brillouin zone.

**Key observables.** Oscillation frequency (126.6 T), effective cyclotron mass, Dingle temperature, quantum relaxation time, and Berry flux ($\Phi_B$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (down to 0.3 K) and magnetic fields up to 8 T; the measured Berry phase is $\sim 0.8\pi$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that MQOs are governed by standard quantum oscillation theory, and that the observed flux invariance relates to a conserved hybridization phase twist.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare MQO signals in heat capacity and magnetization vs. field, show FFT spectra confirming peak frequencies (e.g., 126.6 T), and illustrate the anisotropy of the $\gamma$ pocket.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by presenting the observation of MQOs via AC calorimetry, quantifying fundamental parameters like mass and disorder ($T_D$), and culminating in a topological analysis demonstrating field-orientation invariance of the Berry flux.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Unlike de Haas--van Alphen measurements, heat-capacity magneto-quantum oscillations directly probe the oscillatory bulk quasiparticle density of states. Here, we report the observation of MQOs in V$_2$Ga$_5$ single crystals studied via highly sensitive ac calorimetry. The strongest MQO signal is observed for a magnetic field applied along the vanadium chains, in excellent agreement with de Haas--van Alphen magnetization data. A single dominant frequency of 126.6 T resolved by fast Fourier transform confirms the true bulk origin of the elliptical $γ$ Fermi-surface pocket located near the Z point of the Brillouin zone. The angular dependence of the FFT frequency closely tracks the anisotropy of the $γ$ pocket, as supported by first-principles calculations. Analysis of the temperature- and field-dependent MQO amplitudes allows the precise determination of the effective cyclotron mass, Dingle temperature, quantum relaxation time, carrier mobility, and electron mean free path. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the net Berry flux is invariant with respect to the magnetic-field orientation, as a consequence of a conserved hybridization phase twist within the $γ$ pocket. These findings establish ac calorimetry as a powerful macroscopic probe of topological orbital hybridization in complex intermetallics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on bilayer ZrTe5, demonstrating that it can realize a double Quantum Spin Hall (QSH) phase beyond standard topological classifications. Using first-principles calculations, the authors show that applying uniaxial strain allows tuning between this exotic double QSH state and a conventional single pair QSH state. This establishes the material as a promising platform for studying tunable, dissipationless spin transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is interesting because it explores realizing exotic topological states (double QSH) in a solid-state material platform using computational methods, which informs the search for novel quantum materials relevant to spintronics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates topological phases beyond the conventional Z2 classification, specifically realizing and characterizing a Double Quantum Spin Hall (double QSH) phase in an accessible material system.

**Main result.** Bilayer ZrTe5 is shown to host a tunable phase diagram containing both conventional (Z2=1) and double QSH (Z2=0) phases, demonstrating enhanced edge conductance in the latter.

**Method.** The research relies on first-principles density functional theory calculations (PBE/HSE functionals) combined with advanced topological analysis techniques like calculating the Z2 index and Spin Hall Conductivity (SHC).

**Model / system.** The physical system is bilayer Zirconium pentatelluride (ZrTe5), a layered van der Waals material. The study analyzes its electronic structure under varying uniaxial or biaxial lattice strains.

**Key observables.** Quantized Spin Hall Conductivity (SHC), which exhibits values of 2e^2/h for conventional QSH and 4e^2/h for the double QSH phase.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Uniaxial strain is the key tuning parameter driving the transition between the two topological phases. The robustness of the quantized response is shown over energy windows up to ~100 meV.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the bilayer system due to exfoliation feasibility, and it establishes that the Z2 invariant alone is insufficient for classifying these advanced topological states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure, a topological phase diagram mapping out QSH vs. double QSH regimes under strain, and band structures confirming the quantized SHC in both phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical motivation by discussing limitations of Z2 classification, presents calculations on bilayer ZrTe5 showing its stable realization of the double QSH phase, details how uniaxial strain drives a transition to the conventional QSH phase, and quantifies the resulting enhanced edge transport properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum spin Hall insulators (QSH) are topological materials that host helical edge states protected against backscattering, making them ideal candidates for dissipationless spin transport. Within the conventional $\mathbb{Z}_2$ classification, only phases with an odd number of edge state pairs ($\mathbb{Z}_2 = 1$) are topologically nontrivial, whereas even-channel systems ($\mathbb{Z}_2 = 0$) lie beyond this framework but can host robust edge transport characterized by a spin Chern number. Experimentally accessible realizations of such phases remain rare, particularly in systems with sizeable band gaps. Here, we show that bilayer ZrTe$_5$ realizes a double quantum spin Hall phase in its energetically most stable structure. Using first principles calculations, we demonstrate that uniaxial strain drives a transition from this phase to a conventional single pair QSH phase with $\mathbb{Z}_2 = 1$. The double QSH phase hosts two pairs of helical edge states, resulting in enhanced edge conductance and a quantized spin Hall response that remains robust over an energy window of up to $\sim$100 meV. These results establish bilayer ZrTe$_5$ as a tunable platform connecting conventional and double QSH phases within a single material. More broadly, they demonstrate that untwisted van der Waals bilayers can host topological phases beyond the conventional $\mathbb{Z}_2$ classification.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a robust topological index, $Q$, designed to detect 'fragile' topological phases in 2D materials possessing specific symmetries. By analyzing the interplay between Wilson loops and the Euler class, the authors prove that this index predicts the existence of bulk Dirac cones. This provides an experimentally verifiable spectroscopic signature for identifying new classes of quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful new theoretical framework for classifying topological matter that goes beyond standard invariants like $	ext{Z}_2$ indices, offering a pathway to predict novel electronic properties in complex crystalline systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to find robust, experimentally accessible signatures for 'fragile topological phases'—topological states that lack generic protected boundary states and can be trivialized by adding valence bands.

**Main result.** The authors establish a new topological index, $Q$, which guarantees the existence of bulk Dirac cones in 2D materials possessing both time-reversal ($T$) and twofold rotation ($	ext{C}_2$) symmetries. This signature is robust even when the standard Euler class might suggest otherwise.

**Method.** The methodology combines theoretical analysis using toy models (Hamiltonians) with computational predictions derived from first-principles calculations on five different 2D materials.

**Model / system.** The focus is on electronic band structures in crystalline insulators exhibiting $T$ and $	ext{C}_2$ symmetries. The diagnostic tools involve analyzing Wilson Loops ($	ext{W}_\ell$) and the associated Euler class ($\chi$), leading to the specialized index $Q$.

**Key observables.** Dirac points (both TRIM and generic), the topological index $Q$, the Wilson Loop spectrum, and the Euler class $\chi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The symmetry constraints ($T$ and $	ext{C}_2$), the band structure parameters ($m$ in toy models), and the specific material compositions tested.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the system can be described by isolated, pair-wise coupled bands for index calculation, and it relies on the preservation of $T$ and $	ext{C}_2$ symmetries during calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of band degeneracies (Dirac points) in toy models as parameters change, showing how gap closing leads to Dirac point formation. Other figures show the calculation of Wilson Loops and Euler loops for specific materials, confirming $Q 
eq 0$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from introducing the problem of fragile topology to developing advanced diagnostic tools (Wilson/Euler loops) in toy models, formalizing the index $Q$, and finally applying this robust index to test its prediction across five distinct real-world materials using DFT calculations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Some topological phases of matter are called fragile. They do not generically host protected gapless boundary states and they can be trivialized by adding additional valence bands. Here we show that fragile topology nevertheless has robust signatures: it yields bulk Dirac cones in two-dimensional materials with arbitrarily many bands. In systems with time-reversal and twofold rotation symmetry, we establish a fragile topological index which guarantees the presence of gap closing points in the band structure. We test this prediction using first-principles calculations in five materials and find that all of them host such Dirac points, suggesting that this is a widespread phenomenon. Our results provide a robust spectroscopic signature of fragile topology which can be directly accessed in experiment. Further, they enable an alternate method for finding Dirac cones in ab-initio simulations, and may be used as a route towards identifying materials with nonlinear transport properties.

</details>
